# 47. Sūrat Muḥammad

Thirty-eight verses. Medinan. It takes its name from **the occurrence of the Prophet's name in the second verse**: **وَءَامَنُوا۟ بِمَا نُزِّلَ عَلَىٰ مُحَمَّدٍ.**

It is transmitted that the sūra is also known by the name **Qitāl** (**سُورَةُ ٱلْقِتَال** — the sūra of fighting). This second name comes from the content of the sūra: the greater part of the text was spoken in a setting of conflict, to a community inside that conflict.

**In this commentary the matter of naming deserves to be recorded once again:** the traditional name of the sūra is taken not from its first verse but from a proper name in the middle of its second. And that proper name is not the subject of the sūra — the sūra does not recount the life of the Prophet. The name is a **marker** picked out from inside the text, not a summary of it.

## The name "Muḥammad": where it occurs in the Qurʾān

The name **مُحَمَّد** occurs in **four places** in the Qurʾān:

| | Place | Context |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Āl ʿImrān 3/144** | *"Muḥammad is only a messenger; messengers have passed away before him."* |
| 2 | **al-Aḥzāb 33/40** | *"Muḥammad is not the father of any of your men."* |
| 3 | **Muḥammad 47/2** | *"Those who believe in what has been sent down to Muḥammad…"* |
| 4 | **al-Fatḥ 48/29** | *"Muḥammad is the Messenger of God."* |

A fifth, in another form: **أَحْمَد** — at al-Ṣaff 61/6, in the annunciation of Jesus (ʿĪsā). That word was treated in detail in the section `061-saff.md` (the root ح-م-د, the dispute over whether *Aḥmad* is an elative or an active participle, and the question of whether the word is a proper name or an attribute). **I do not repeat it here.**

Only this will be recorded here: **all four occurrences are in Medinan sūras**, and in all four there stands beside the name a **limit** or a **fixing of position**. Āl ʿImrān says "he is only a messenger"; al-Aḥzāb says "he is not your father"; al-Fatḥ says "he is the Messenger of God"; and here, at 47/2, what is believed in is **not the name itself but "what has been sent down to him"**: *āmanū **bimā nuzzila ʿalā** Muḥammad.*

**I record this commonality as my own reading.** That all four verses carry a sentence of limitation is a fact that can be verified from the text; I do not claim that there is a deliberate design among them.

## What is the sūra doing?

This sūra has a single key word, and that word is the skeleton of the text: **عَمَل** — deed, work done.

The root **ع-م-ل** occurs twelve times in the sūra. And in most of the places where it occurs, **a verb of annulment** is attached before or after it. The table:

| Verse | Expression | Verb | Its subject |
|---|---|---|---|
| **1** | *aḍalla aʿmālahum* | **أَضَلَّ** — made them lose their way | God |
| **2** | *wa ʿamilu'ṣ-ṣāliḥāt* | — | The believers |
| **4** | *fa-lan yuḍilla aʿmālahum* | **لَن يُضِلَّ** — will not make them lose their way | God |
| **8** | *wa aḍalla aʿmālahum* | **أَضَلَّ** | God |
| **9** | *fa-aḥbaṭa aʿmālahum* | **أَحْبَطَ** — swelled them up and voided them | God |
| **12** | *wa ʿamilu'ṣ-ṣāliḥāt* | — | The believers |
| **14** | *sūʾu ʿamalih* | — | A person's own deed |
| **28** | *fa-aḥbaṭa aʿmālahum* | **أَحْبَطَ** | God |
| **30** | *yaʿlamu aʿmālakum* | — | God knows |
| **32** | *wa sa-yuḥbiṭu aʿmālahum* | **سَيُحْبِطُ** | God |
| **33** | ***lā tubṭilū aʿmālakum*** | **لَا تُبْطِلُوا۟** — **do not annul** | **You** |
| **35** | *wa lan yatirakum aʿmālakum* | **لَن يَتِرَكُمْ** — will not diminish | God |

**What the table says is the sūra itself.**

For thirty-two verses the one who voids the deeds is always **God**, and what is voided is always the deeds of **others**: *aʿmālahum* — their deeds. The pronoun is third person. The one addressed is a spectator.

And **in the thirty-third verse two things change at once:**

- The pronoun turns to the **second person**: *aʿmālakum* — **your** deeds.
- The subject of the verb becomes **the one addressed himself**: *lā tubṭilū* — "**you** must not annul".

That is: after saying for thirty-two verses "their deeds were voided", the sūra turns to the one it addresses and says this: **you can do this to yourself too.**

And when the sūra ends, the last word is again about deeds, but this time in the opposite direction: **وَلَن يَتِرَكُمْ أَعْمَٰلَكُمْ** — "He will not diminish anything of your deeds" (35).

**The whole tension of the sūra lies between these two sentences:** a God who does not diminish a deed, and a person who can annul his own.

## The structure of the sūra

| Verses | Subject | Key |
|---|---|---|
| **1-3** | Two groups, two verbs; and the ground given explicitly | *aḍalla* / *kaffara* — *dhālika bi-anna* |
| **4-6** | The moment of encounter; captives; and those killed on that road | *fa-idhā laqītum* — *mannan aw fidāʾan* |
| **7-9** | A conditional sentence: if you help you are helped; and its opposite | *in tanṣurū* — *taʿsan lahum* |
| **10-11** | Travelling the earth and looking; the one who has a protector and the one who has none | *ʿāqibatu'lladhīna min qablihim* |
| **12-15** | Two lives, two descriptions; four rivers and a single drink | *kamā taʾkulu'l-anʿām* — *anhārun* |
| **16-19** | Listening and taking nothing from it; those who are increased; and *fa'lam* | *mādhā qāla ānifan* — *fa'lam* |
| **20-23** | When a sūra is sent down: the disease in the heart becomes visible | *naẓara'l-maghshiyyi ʿalayhi* |
| **24-28** | Pondering and locks; those who turn back and the secret agreement | *a-fa-lā yatadabbarūn* — *aqfāluhā* |
| **29-31** | The bringing out of rancours; the bent of speech; and the test | *laḥni'l-qawl* — *la-nabluwannakum* |
| **32-35** | Do not annul your deeds; do not grow slack | *lā tubṭilū* — *lā tahinū* |
| **36-38** | The life of this world; the direction of miserliness; and the warning about replacement | *laʿibun wa lahw* — *yastabdil qawman ghayrakum* |

The sūra opens with **الَّذِينَ كَفَرُوا۟** (1) and closes with **أَمْثَٰلَكُمْ** (38). That is, it begins in the third person and ends in the second. Through the length of the sūra the direction of the warning turns slowly towards the one addressed.

## Its being Medinan, and the historical frame

There is no disagreement that the sūra is Medinan. The indications inside the text already show it: the field of battle, the captive, ransom even where there is no booty, the description of the hypocrites, the wish "if only a sūra were sent down", the call to spend — all of these are matters belonging to the Medinan period.

**One exception is transmitted.** It is said that the thirteenth verse — *"How many a town stronger than your town which drove you out…"* — came down during the emigration, on the road. This is a report widely transmitted in the classical sources; **I do not present it with a claim of certainty.** The wording of the verse appears to support the report (*allatī akhrajatka* — "which drove you out"), but the wording itself gives no time.

**I give no date of revelation for the sūra as a whole.** Different findings are transmitted in the sources — before Badr, after Badr, and later periods; I have no sound measure by which to prefer among them.

**But this much can be read from the text:** the sūra speaks at a time when the fighting is not **beginning** but **going on**. For the fourth verse regulates not how fighting is to begin but what is to be done when an encounter takes place and what is to become of captives; the twentieth verse opens with "when a sūra in which fighting is mentioned is sent down" — that is, the mention of fighting is by now a known thing; and the thirty-fifth verse says "do not grow slack" — and slackness happens only in a work that is going on.

## A note on method: how this sūra will not be read

Per the method (`STYLE.md`), I am obliged to draw two limits before entering this sūra. They will affect the reading from beginning to end.

**One. This sūra is not a text of the law of war, and no legal ruling will be drawn from it in this commentary.** The fourth verse is one of the most debated texts in the chapter on captives in classical jurisprudence; **I will transmit that debate in the form of a table of disagreement** and will prefer no view. The work of this commentary is to show what the text says; it is not to say what is to be done.

**The second, and more important: this sūra will not be attached to any event, any side, any conflict of the present day.** I write this not merely as a precaution but as a matter of **truthfulness**: the sūra speaks about a particular situation in which a particular community found itself at a particular time. To equate that situation with a situation of today is to make the text say something it does not say.

**Three. The sūra speaks not about an identity but about an attribute.** This was treated at length in the section `059-hasr.md`, and the measure set there holds here too: *"The criticism of the sūra is directed not at an identity but at a stance."*

Indeed this sūra shows it itself. The heaviest descriptions in the sūra are not about "those who deny" but about **those who say they believe**: those with a disease in their hearts (20, 29), those who look as one in the swoon of death (20), those who agree in secret (26), those who turn back (25). And all the address after the thirty-third verse — miserliness, slackness, turning away — is **to the believers.**

**The sūra turns the direction of the threat from the outside inward.** A reading that forgets this has done the exact opposite of what the sūra does.

## The basmala

The analysis of the basmala was made in the section `001-fatiha.md`.

A note particular to this sūra: a text that opens with the names **al-Raḥmān and al-Raḥīm** regulates the field of battle in its fourth verse. This tension was recorded in the section `059-hasr.md` as well, and here, as there, it is **not resolved but recorded.**

This much is visible from the text: the fourth verse does not issue an order to fight; it regulates **what is to happen when an encounter takes place** and **what is to be done when the fighting has been put down**. And one of the two items of that regulation is **release without payment**. This will be treated in detail below.

---

## 47/1 — ٱلَّذِينَ كَفَرُوا۟ وَصَدُّوا۟ عَن سَبِيلِ ٱللَّهِ أَضَلَّ أَعْمَٰلَهُمْ

*Alladhīna kafarū wa ṣaddū ʿan sabīli'llāhi aḍalla aʿmālahum*

"Those who deny and turn others away from the path of God — [God] has made their deeds go astray."

### The word order of the opening: no predicate, a noun

The sūra opens with a nominal sentence, and its subject is a **relative pronoun**: *alladhīna* — "those who".

This is not an opening often seen in the Qurʾān. Most sūras open with a glorification (*sabbaḥa*), an oath (*wa'l-fajr*), a call (*yā ayyuhā*), a command (*qul*) or a piece of news (*atā amru'llāh*). Here the opening is **directly the description of a group** — and no predicate comes until the description is finished.

And the description is made with two verbs:

- **كَفَرُوا۟** — they denied.
- **وَصَدُّوا۟ عَن سَبِيلِ ٱللَّهِ** — and they turned others away from the path of God.

**That the two stand side by side is not accidental, and the distinction has to be seen.** The first concerns the person himself: he believed or he did not. The second concerns **someone else**: cutting off another's road.

That is, the first sentence of the sūra describes not bare denial but **an obstruction added to denial**. The Qurʾān often uses these two verbs together, and in this sūra it brings them together two more times: 32 (*kafarū wa ṣaddū ʿan sabīli'llāh*) and 34 (the same expression). That is, **the two verbs of the opening sentence will be repeated word for word twice more towards the end of the sūra.**

This is a frame built inside the sūra: 1 — 32 — 34.

### صدّ — the root ص-د-د

**Root: ص-د-د.** The primary sense the lexicographers record: **to turn away from something, to turn**; and, used transitively, **to turn someone else away, to hold back, to obstruct.**

The root has two directions, and both are found in the Qurʾān:

| Use | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **Intransitive** — *ṣadda ʿan* | He himself turned away | *"When they see you they **turn away** from you"* (at al-Zukhruf 43/57, *yaṣiddūn* from the same root) |
| **Transitive** — *ṣadda… ʿan* | He held someone else back | **The use here** |

From the same root **صَدِيد** (*ṣadīd*) — pus; and it is not to be confused with **صَدَف**. Also **الصَّدَد** — the front of a thing, what stands opposite it; in Turkish the expression *bu sadedde*, meaning "in this connection", is a borrowing from this root.

**At the centre of the root there is a picture of "cutting off the way ahead":** *ṣadd* is to get in front of the direction in which someone is going.

### سَبِيل — path

**Root: س-ب-ل.** *Sabīl* means path; but in Arabic there is more than one word for a road, and the difference between them matters:

| Word | Root | Emphasis |
|---|---|---|
| **طَرِيق** (*ṭarīq*) | ط-ر-ق | The road walked upon, beaten — *ṭarq* (to strike) |
| **صِرَاط** (*ṣirāṭ*) | ص-ر-ط | The wide, open main road — treated in al-Fātiḥa |
| **سَبِيل** (*sabīl*) | س-ب-ل | The road easily travelled, the flowing road |

In the root of *sabīl* there is **flowing and pouring**: *sabala'l-māʾ* — he poured out the water; **سُنبُلَة** (*sunbula*, an ear of grain) is from the same root — the place where the grains are ranged and flow; and **إِسْبَال** — the letting down of a covering.

**That is, *sabīl* is the road on which moving forward is easy and fluid.** This gains its meaning when read together with the verb *ṣadd*: something that is flowing has its way cut off.

**I record this connection as my own reading;** the "flowing" sense of the root is lexical data, but this picture between the two words is a connection I have made.

### أَضَلَّ أَعْمَٰلَهُمْ — to make lose the way

**Root: ض-ل-ل.** Its concrete sense: **to lose the road, to be unable to find where a thing is.** The Arabs say *ḍalla'l-baʿīr*: the camel was lost. And what is striking is this: the same root is used both for **the one who is lost** and for **the thing that is lost.**

**أَضَلَّ** — Form *ifʿāl*, transitive: **he made it lost, he caused it to be lost.**

Now let us look at the work of the word in this verse. The verse does not say:

- *"He destroyed their deeds"* (لم يقل: أهلك)
- *"He wiped out their deeds"* (لم يقل: محا)
- *"He rejected their deeds"* (لم يقل: ردّ)

**It says: He made their deeds lost.** That is, the deeds **are still there** — but their owner cannot find them. To lose a thing is not for that thing to cease to exist; **it is for the connection with it to be cut.**

The Qurʾān opens out this picture elsewhere. At al-Furqān 25/23 the same idea comes with another comparison: *"And We shall turn to whatever deed they did and make it **scattered dust**."* At Ibrāhīm 14/18: *"Their deeds are **like ashes** which the wind scatters violently on a stormy day."*

**In all three verses the deeds do not cease to exist; they become *unreachable*.**

### Who is the subject of the verb?

In the sentence the subject of the verb **أَضَلَّ** is not stated explicitly; it is a concealed pronoun. And who the concealed pronoun is can be understood from the preceding word: **سَبِيلِ ٱللَّهِ** — the divine name in the expression "the path of God".

**This is a point of word order:** rather than saying "God has voided their deeds", the verse names God **not as the subject of the verb but inside a construct**; then it leaves the verb without stating its subject.

The result: although the sentence is a sentence of judgement, the name of the one who judges is not pronounced directly.

**I do not speak with certainty about this choice.** I record it as an observation: in the same sūra, at another occurrence of the same verb (47/4: *fa-lan yuḍilla aʿmālahum*), the subject is again concealed. The sūra keeps to this pattern.

### Tense: why the perfect?

**أَضَلَّ** is past tense. Yet the group described is still alive and its deeds are still going on.

The classical explanation invokes a use often seen in the Qurʾān: **the perfect that announces certainty.** If it is certain that a thing will come about, it is recounted in the past tense even though it has not yet happened. In the section `056-vakia.md` this pattern was treated by way of another example.

**But a second reading is possible here too, and it stands closer to the text:** the loss of the deeds is not something that will happen in the future but something **already done and finished**. That is, the verse does not issue a threat, it makes a **diagnosis**: those deeds were lost long ago; their owner is not yet aware of it.

**I record this second reading as my own preference, and it is not binding.** The first explanation is the widespread one in the classical commentaries.

---

## 47/2 — وَٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوا۟ وَعَمِلُوا۟ ٱلصَّٰلِحَٰتِ وَءَامَنُوا۟ بِمَا نُزِّلَ عَلَىٰ مُحَمَّدٍ وَهُوَ ٱلْحَقُّ مِن رَّبِّهِمْ كَفَّرَ عَنْهُمْ سَيِّـَٔاتِهِمْ وَأَصْلَحَ بَالَهُمْ

*Wa'lladhīna āmanū wa ʿamilu'ṣ-ṣāliḥāti wa āmanū bimā nuzzila ʿalā Muḥammadin wa huwa'l-ḥaqqu min rabbihim kaffara ʿanhum sayyiʾātihim wa aṣlaḥa bālahum*

"And those who believe and do righteous deeds and believe in what has been sent down to Muḥammad — and it is the truth from their Lord — [God] has covered over their evil deeds and set right their **bāl**."

### The symmetry of the opening — and the place where the symmetry breaks

**The first two verses of the sūra form a pair.** The same pattern is built twice, for two groups. Let us see the symmetry first:

| | **47/1** | **47/2** |
|---|---|---|
| Subject | **ٱلَّذِينَ كَفَرُوا۟** | **وَٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوا۟** |
| Verb of description (2nd) | **وَصَدُّوا۟ عَن سَبِيلِ ٱللَّهِ** | **وَعَمِلُوا۟ ٱلصَّٰلِحَٰتِ** |
| Further description | — | **وَءَامَنُوا۟ بِمَا نُزِّلَ…** |
| **Predicate (verb)** | **أَضَلَّ** — made them lose their way | **كَفَّرَ** — covered over |
| **Object of the verb** | **أَعْمَٰلَهُمْ** — their deeds | **سَيِّـَٔاتِهِمْ** — their evil deeds |
| Second predicate | — | **وَأَصْلَحَ بَالَهُمْ** |

**The symmetry is not complete, and its incompleteness is the real matter of the verse.**

The expected symmetry would have been this: if the deeds of the first group are voided, it should have been said that the deeds of the second group are **accepted** — something like *wa qabbila aʿmālahum*. But the verse does not say that.

**The object changes.** In the first verse what is acted upon is **deeds**. In the second verse what is acted upon is **evil deeds**. That is:

- The **good work** of the denier is touched → it is lost.
- The **bad work** of the believer is touched → it is covered.

**This is not an accident but a choice; and it says two things.**

**First:** the acceptance of the believer's deed is not spoken of, because that is not the matter of the verse. The matter of the verse is what happens to **the minus column in the ledger** of the two groups. The denier's plus column is erased; the believer's minus column is erased.

**Second — and this is finer:** the verse **says nothing at all about the believer's good deeds**, because it had already counted them at the outset: *wa ʿamilu'ṣ-ṣāliḥāt*. That is, the righteous deed occurs not as the subject of the reward but as **a part of the description of the group**. The reward is added on top of the description.

**In the description of the denier, by contrast, there was no deed.** The description was "they denied and they obstructed"; then the ruling was "He made their deeds lost". That is, the deed occurred not in the description but **in the ruling** — and there it was lost.

**This reading is mine.** The difference of object between the two verses is a fact standing in the text; the division of labour I draw from it is my own inference.

### وَءَامَنُوا۟ — why twice?

*Āmanū* occurs twice in the verse:

1. **وَٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوا۟** — those who believe.
2. **وَءَامَنُوا۟ بِمَا نُزِّلَ عَلَىٰ مُحَمَّدٍ** — and who believe in what has been sent down to Muḥammad.

The repetition is not idle. Several explanations are given in the classical commentaries:

| View | Explanation |
|---|---|
| **1** | The first belief is **absolute** (belief in God); the second is the binding of that belief **to a definite content**. A descent from the general to the particular. |
| **2** | Its purpose is to exclude those who believe in the earlier books but do not believe in the one newly come: the claim of faith is not completed unless the revelation that comes is accepted. |
| **3** | Emphasis (*taʾkīd*); the second clause is an unfolding of the first. |

**I make no preference.** I record this: what the second *āmanū* attaches to is **not a person but a text** — *bimā nuzzila*, "in what has been sent down". What is believed in is not Muḥammad but **what has been sent down to him**. This is the verse's own wording, and its being in the sentence from which the sūra's name is taken is worth noting.

### نُزِّلَ — Form *tafʿīl* and the passive

**نُزِّلَ** — root ن-ز-ل, Form *tafʿīl*, **passive**: "it was sent down".

**Two points:**

**One — the matter of the form.** The lexicographers and the exegetes record a distinction between **إِنزَال** (*ifʿāl*) and **تَنزِيل** (*tafʿīl*): *ifʿāl* announces a sending down all at once, *tafʿīl* a sending down piece by piece, gradually. The Qurʾān's coming down piecemeal over twenty-three years is named with the word *tanzīl*.

**This distinction is recorded in the classical sources; but it has also been pointed out that it is not applied consistently everywhere in the Qurʾān.** Indeed in the twentieth verse of this sūra the two forms will stand side by side **in the same sentence** (*law lā nuzzilat sūra… fa-idhā unzilat sūratun muḥkamatun*), and I shall take up this distinction separately there.

**Two — the passive.** It says "was sent down", not "We sent down". The subject of the verb is not stated — just as the subject of *aḍalla* was not stated in the preceding verse. In both of the sūra's first two verses the agent is veiled.

### وَهُوَ ٱلْحَقُّ مِن رَّبِّهِمْ — the interposed clause

This is a *jumla muʿtariḍa* (an interposed clause): a judgement squeezed in while the description is still running.

**The word order has three details:**

**One. A definite predicate.** *wa huwa'l-ḥaqqu* — "it is **the** truth". The predicate carries the definite article. In Arabic a definite predicate announces **restriction** (*qaṣr*): "it is the truth itself", "the truth is this". Had it been said *wa huwa ḥaqqun* (indefinite), it would have been "it is a truth" — one among others.

**Two. مِن رَّبِّهِمْ** — "from their Lord". The pronoun refers back to the believers. That is, the truth is described not as an abstract correctness but as something **coming from their Lord**. The attribution is made to the source.

**Three.** And this clause's link with the third verse is direct: the third verse will repeat the same word with the same construct — *ittabaʿu'l-ḥaqqa **min rabbihim***. That is, the interposed clause in the second verse is the preparation for the ground given in the third.

### كَفَّرَ عَنْهُمْ سَيِّـَٔاتِهِمْ — to cover

**Root: ك-ف-ر.** And there is something here that has to be said carefully.

**The first word of the first verse and the verb of this verse are from the same root.**

- 47/1: **كَفَرُوا۟** — *kafarū* (they denied).
- 47/2: **كَفَّرَ** — *kaffara* (He covered over).

The same three letters. A different form, an opposite outcome.

The primary sense of the root is **to cover**. **This root was analysed in full in `002-bakara.md` (2/6) and taken up again in `109-kafirun.md` and `057-hadid.md`; I do not repeat it here.** In summary: the farmer is called *kāfir* because he **covers** the seed with earth — and the reading of the expression *aʿjaba'l-kuffāra nabātuh* at al-Ḥadīd 57/20 in the sense of "the farmers" was treated in detail in `057-hadid.md`. The night, too, is called *kāfir*, because it covers everything. *Kufr* is the covering of the truth.

**And *takfīr* (Form *tafʿīl*) performs the same covering in the positive direction: covering over a sin.**

That is, in the first two verses of the sūra **the same verb works twice:**

| | The one who covers | What is covered |
|---|---|---|
| **47/1** | Man | **The truth** |
| **47/2** | God | **Sin** |

**The Qurʾān appears to set up a relation of requital by using the same root: the deed of the one who covers the truth is lost; the sin of the one who does not cover the truth is covered.**

**I record this comparison as my own reading.** That the two words are from the same root is lexical data and beyond dispute; the symmetry between them is one I am setting up, and I ascribe it to no classical exegete.

**There is a particle too: عَنْ.** *Kaffara **ʿanhum*** — "covered them over, [removing it] from them". When the verb is made transitive with the particle *ʿan* it carries, besides covering, a sense of **removal**. The sin is both covered and taken away from the person.

### أَصْلَحَ بَالَهُمْ — and the word بال

**This is the least known word in the verse, and it deserves to be dwelt upon.**

**بَال** — root **ب-و-ل**. The lexicographers give the word these senses:

| Sense | Use |
|---|---|
| **1. State, condition, how things are going** | *mā bāluk?* — "what is your state, what is the matter with you?" |
| **2. Heart, inward self, inner world** | *khaṭara bi-bālī* — "it came into my mind, into my heart" |
| **3. Importance, weight** | *amrun dhū bāl* — "a matter of importance" |
| **4. Ease, inward spaciousness** | *rakhiyyu'l-bāl* — "easy in heart, free of care" |

**These four senses are not disconnected from one another.** The common core is this: *bāl* is a person's **inward condition** — not the state visible from outside but the inner position that produces that state.

There is no exact English equivalent. "State" is too external; "heart" too emotional; "mind" too abstract. **The nearest is perhaps "inward state" or "the order of one's heart".**

**In the Qurʾān the word *bāl* occurs in four places**, and all four are instructive:

| Place | Expression | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| **Yūsuf 12/50** | *mā bālu'n-niswati'llātī qaṭṭaʿna aydiyahunn* | "What was the **case** of the women who cut their hands?" |
| **Ṭāhā 20/51** | *fa-mā bālu'l-qurūni'l-ūlā* | "Then what of the **state** of the earlier generations?" |
| **Muḥammad 47/2** | *wa aṣlaḥa **bālahum*** | "He set right their **inward state**" |
| **Muḥammad 47/5** | *wa yuṣliḥu **bālahum*** | "He will set right their **inward state**" |

**Two of the occurrences are in this sūra**, and both with the same verb: *aṣlaḥa / yuṣliḥu.*

### أَصْلَحَ — the root ص-ل-ح and the repetition inside the verse

**Root: ص-ل-ح.** Its primary sense: **a thing's being serviceable, sound, unspoiled.** Its opposite is **فَسَاد** (corruption).

And note: at the centre of the word lies not **repair** but **fitness**. A tool is *ṣāliḥ* — that is, it is in a state to do its work. Food is *ṣāliḥ* — it has not gone bad.

**Inside the verse this root occurs twice:**

1. **ٱلصَّٰلِحَٰتِ** — righteous deeds (in the description).
2. **أَصْلَحَ** — He set right (in the ruling).

**That is: the *bāl* of those who do righteous work is set right (*iṣlāḥ*).** The same root names first what a person does, then what is done to a person.

**This may be the most striking place in the verse, and it raises this question:** why was the setting right of the *bāl* chosen as the requital?

The answer that can be read from the text is this: **the verse gives the requital not as an outward reward but as an inward condition.** Neither property, nor rank, nor victory. Evil deeds are covered and **the inward order** is repaired.

**And this gains its meaning in the context of the sūra.** This is a sūra that came down to a community in a setting of war. In such a setting the expected promise is victory. The verse does not speak of victory — it speaks of **the setting right of the inward state**. The fifth verse will repeat the same expression for **those killed in the fighting**; there too the promise is the same: *wa yuṣliḥu bālahum.*

**I record this finding as my own reading.** The meaning of the word and its two occurrences are textual data; the interpretation I draw from them — that the promise lies not outside but within — is my own inference.

---

## 47/3 — ذَٰلِكَ بِأَنَّ ٱلَّذِينَ كَفَرُوا۟ ٱتَّبَعُوا۟ ٱلْبَٰطِلَ وَأَنَّ ٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوا۟ ٱتَّبَعُوا۟ ٱلْحَقَّ مِن رَّبِّهِمْ كَذَٰلِكَ يَضْرِبُ ٱللَّهُ لِلنَّاسِ أَمْثَٰلَهُمْ

*Dhālika bi-anna'lladhīna kafaru'ttabaʿu'l-bāṭila wa anna'lladhīna āmanu'ttabaʿu'l-ḥaqqa min rabbihim; kadhālika yaḍribu'llāhu li'n-nāsi amthālahum*

"That is because those who deny followed falsehood, and those who believe followed the truth from their Lord. Thus does God set forth for people their likenesses."

### ذَٰلِكَ بِأَنَّ — the ground given explicitly

**This pattern is one of the most remarkable of the Qurʾān's manners of speech, and this sūra uses it like a spine.**

**ذَٰلِكَ** — a demonstrative: "this, this here". What does it point to? The **two rulings** given in the two preceding verses: the making lost of the deeds of the one, the covering over of the evil deeds of the other.

**بِأَنَّ** — the particle *bāʾ* here is **causal** (*sababiyya*): "because of its being…". And *anna* turns the clause that follows into a verbal noun.

The skeleton of the sentence, then: **"That [ruling] is because of this."**

**And one has to see what this does.**

When a ruling is issued, two things can be done: the ruling can be issued and left, or the ground of the ruling can be stated. The second does not mean that the one who issues the ruling owes something to the one addressed; but it does mean that he aims at the addressee's **understanding**.

**Here the Qurʾān does the second.** Two verses issued a ruling, the third wrote out the ground. And the ground was not left as something to be guessed; **it was put at the head of the sentence.**

### The distribution of the pattern within the sūra

**This pattern occurs five times in this sūra**, and its distribution shows an order:

| Verse | Expression | Ground of what |
|---|---|---|
| **3** | *dhālika bi-anna…* | The two rulings in verses 1-2 |
| **9** | *dhālika bi-annahum karihū mā anzala'llāh* | The *taʿsan lahum* in verse 8 |
| **11** | *dhālika bi-anna'llāha mawla'lladhīna āmanū* | The destruction in verse 10 |
| **26** | *dhālika bi-annahum qālū…* | The turning back in verse 25 |
| **28** | *dhālika bi-annahumu'ttabaʿū mā askhaṭa'llāh* | The blow of the angels in verse 27 |

**Five grounds, all five in the same pattern.** And note: **all five come immediately after the ruling.** That is, wherever the sūra issues a heavy ruling, it writes the ground in the next verse.

**This determines how the sūra is to be read.** This sūra carries hard sentences; but not one of its hard sentences has been left without a ground. To take a ruling out of its context is to take it away from its ground as well — and the ground is what determines to whom the ruling applies.

**In terms of the method (`STYLE.md`), this yields a direct consequence:** the verses issue their ruling not upon a group but upon the **attributes** counted in the ground. The ground of the third verse is not "those who deny"; it is **"their having followed falsehood"**. If the attribute changes the ruling changes too — and indeed the sūra will make this an explicit condition at 47/34: *"then they died as deniers"*.

### ٱتَّبَعُوا۟ — Form VIII

**Root: ت-ب-ع.** To go after, to follow. And the form here is **ٱتَّبَعَ** — Form VIII (*iftiʿāl*).

Among the senses Form VIII announces is **taking on the result of an act** (*muṭāwaʿa*), and another is **effort and intent**: doing the act willingly and with labour spent.

**That is, *ittabaʿa* does not mean "he was dragged along behind"; it means "he set off after it, he attached himself to it".** The verb includes choice.

And this carries the whole weight of the verse: **both groups do the same act.** Both have set off after something. The difference lies not in the act but in **the object**.

### ٱلْبَٰطِل and ٱلْحَقّ — the roots of two words

**بَاطِل — root ب-ط-ل.** The primary sense the lexicographers record: **a thing's coming to nothing, being void, being unable to stand.** *Baṭala'sh-shayʾ* — that thing became invalid, it fell.

At the centre of the word lie **hollowness and inability to hold**. *Bāṭil* is not merely what is false; it is what **cannot bear weight**. Step on it and it caves in.

And a note on the language: the lexicographers record from the same root the word **بَطَل** (*baṭal*) as well — **hero, champion.** At first glance this is odd. One of the explanations given is this: he is called *baṭal* because he **renders void** those who come against him, brings their work to nothing. Another explanation has to do with the blood shed beside him going unrequited.

**I transmit these derivational explanations as the lexicographers record them; I make no preference among them.** It should also be recorded that the word lives on in Turkish as *batal* and *iptal*.

**And this detail matters:** this root will occur **twice** in the sūra — here as **ٱلْبَٰطِل** (3), and in the thirty-third verse as **لَا تُبْطِلُوا۟**. That is, at the beginning of the sūra there are "those who follow falsehood"; towards its end the believers are told "do not **annul** (*ibṭāl*) your own deeds".

**The same root names first what others follow, then what the believers can do to themselves.** I shall take up this link separately at verse 33.

**حَقّ — root ح-ق-ق.** Its primary sense: **to be fixed, to stand in place, to come about, to be one's due.** *Ḥaqqa'l-amr* — the matter came about, became fixed.

**That is, at the centre of the root lies *thubūt* (standing in place)** — and this is the exact opposite of *bāṭil*. *Bāṭil* falls, *ḥaqq* stands.

**The opposition of the two words is a physical opposition before it is a moral one:** one stays standing, the other does not. The Qurʾān draws this picture explicitly at al-Raʿd 13/17: the torrent comes, the foam rises to the top and goes; what is of use to people stays where it is — *"thus does God set forth truth and falsehood in a likeness."*

### مِن رَّبِّهِمْ — a second time

In the third verse **مِن رَّبِّهِمْ** is again added behind the word *ḥaqq* — just as it was in the second verse.

**And there is no such attribution for falsehood.** The sentence says "they followed falsehood" and stops; it does not say where falsehood comes from.

**This asymmetry is worth noting.** A source is shown for the truth; none is shown for falsehood. As a reading I record this — **it is my own inference:** if the sense of "hollowness" in the root of *bāṭil* is kept in mind, the absence of a source becomes meaningful; for *bāṭil* is not something that comes from somewhere but the emptiness left where the truth is not.

**I do not present this as a binding exegesis.** The fact standing in the text is this: *ḥaqq* occurs with an attribution, *bāṭil* without one.

### يَضْرِبُ ٱللَّهُ لِلنَّاسِ أَمْثَٰلَهُمْ

**ضَرَبَ مَثَلًا** — "to strike a likeness". The verb Arabic uses for giving an example is **ضرب**, and this sounds odd in English. The lexicographers explain: the verb *ḍarb* also carries the sense of "to set something firmly in a place, to drive in a stake" (*ḍaraba'l-khayma* — he pitched the tent). A likeness is "struck" because it is **driven into** the mind.

**And in this sūra the root ض-ر-ب will occur three times, in three different senses:**

| Verse | Expression | Sense |
|---|---|---|
| **3** | *yaḍribu'llāhu… amthālahum* | To give a likeness |
| **4** | *fa-ḍarba'r-riqāb* | To strike (the field of battle) |
| **27** | *yaḍribūna wujūhahum wa adbārahum* | To strike (the moment of death) |

**Three occurrences, the same root, three different works.** I record this as a textual observation; I do not claim that the sūra has set it up deliberately.

**أَمْثَٰلَهُمْ** — "their likenesses". Whom the pronoun refers to has been disputed: to people (that is, "He sets forth for people their own condition in a likeness"), or to the two groups ("He gives the likeness of these two groups")? **Both readings are found in the classical commentaries; I make no preference.**

This much is clear: the verse characterises the two verses before it as **a likeness**. That is, verses 1-2 are not merely a ruling about two groups; they are set down as an **example**.

And the root **م-ث-ل** will occur three times in the sūra: here (3), at 47/10 (*wa li'l-kāfirīna amthāluhā*), and **in the last word of the sūra** (47/38: *thumma lā yakūnū amthālakum*). The sūra is framed by a word for "likeness".

---

## 47/4 — فَإِذَا لَقِيتُمُ ٱلَّذِينَ كَفَرُوا۟ فَضَرْبَ ٱلرِّقَابِ حَتَّىٰٓ إِذَآ أَثْخَنتُمُوهُمْ فَشُدُّوا۟ ٱلْوَثَاقَ فَإِمَّا مَنًّۢا بَعْدُ وَإِمَّا فِدَآءً حَتَّىٰ تَضَعَ ٱلْحَرْبُ أَوْزَارَهَا ذَٰلِكَ وَلَوْ يَشَآءُ ٱللَّهُ لَٱنتَصَرَ مِنْهُمْ وَلَٰكِن لِّيَبْلُوَا۟ بَعْضَكُم بِبَعْضٍ وَٱلَّذِينَ قُتِلُوا۟ فِى سَبِيلِ ٱللَّهِ فَلَن يُضِلَّ أَعْمَٰلَهُمْ

*Fa-idhā laqītumu'lladhīna kafarū fa-ḍarba'r-riqāb; ḥattā idhā athkhantumūhum fa-shuddu'l-wathāq; fa-immā mannan baʿdu wa immā fidāʾan ḥattā taḍaʿa'l-ḥarbu awzārahā. Dhālika; wa law yashāʾu'llāhu la'ntaṣara minhum wa lākin li-yabluwa baʿḍakum bi-baʿḍ. Wa'lladhīna qutilū fī sabīli'llāhi fa-lan yuḍilla aʿmālahum*

"So when you **encounter** those who deny — the striking of necks. Then, when you have subdued them, bind the bond fast. Thereafter either release without payment, or ransom — until the war lays down its burdens. So it is. Had God willed, He would [Himself] have taken retribution from them; but [it is so] that He may test some of you by means of others. And as for those killed in the path of God, [God] will never make their deeds go astray."

---

**This verse is one of the most frequently torn from its context in the sūra and perhaps in the Qurʾān. Per the method (`STYLE.md`), I shall do four things here in order:**

1. I shall show **how the sentence is built** — because the way the sentence is built determines its scope.
2. I shall analyse the roots of the words.
3. I shall show **the limits the verse sets within itself**.
4. I shall transmit the debate in the classical commentaries **in the form of a table of disagreement** — and **I shall issue no legal ruling.**

**And I write this at the outset:** this verse will not be attached to any event, any side, any group of the present day. This is not a sentence of precaution; it is a limit required by the wording of the verse. I shall show its ground below.

---

### The first matter: how does the sentence begin?

**The first word of the verse is not a command but a condition.**

**فَإِذَا لَقِيتُمُ** — *fa-idhā laqītum*: "**when you encounter**".

To see this changes everything. The sentence could have been built like this:

- *uqtulu'lladhīna kafarū* — "kill those who deny." **It does not say this.**
- *ughzū ʿalayhim* — "march against them." **It does not say this.**
- *jāhidūhum* — "struggle against them." **It does not say this.**

The sentence begins with **إِذَا**. In Arabic *idhā* is **the adverb of time for a situation whose occurrence is expected**: "when it happens". And the property of a conditional sentence is this: **the answer is not in force until the condition has come about.**

That is, what the verse regulates is not the **starting** of a war; it is what is to happen **when an encounter takes place** on the field of battle.

### لَقِىَ — the root ل-ق-ي

**Root: ل-ق-ي.** Its primary sense is **to meet, to come upon, to come face to face.** From the same root: **لِقَاء** (*liqāʾ*, meeting), **تَلَقَّى** (to meet, to receive — Adam's (Ādam) "receiving" of the words at al-Baqara 2/37), **مُلَاقَاة**, and **أَلْقَى** (to throw, to set before).

**At the centre of the word lies a *coming face to face*** — and this is not a one-sided movement. *Liqāʾ* requires both parties to be at that point.

The Qurʾān uses this verb in the context of war in several places, and wherever it uses it, it recounts the same thing: **not the moment of attack, but the moment when the lines meet.**

- *"O you who believe! When you **encounter** a force, stand firm"* (al-Anfāl 8/45).
- *"O you who believe! When you **encounter** those who deny in a marching host, do not turn your backs to them"* (al-Anfāl 8/15).

**That is, the verb itself describes the scene: two ranks face to face.** This is not a street, a house, a marketplace.

### فَضَرْبَ ٱلرِّقَابِ — the most striking place in the word order

**This part of the verse is grammatically extraordinary, and it has to be seen.**

What is expected as the answer of the sentence is an **imperative verb**: *fa'ḍribū'r-riqāb* — "strike the necks". But the verse does not say this.

The verse says **فَضَرْبَ ٱلرِّقَابِ**: a **verbal noun**, in the accusative. The vowelling of the word is *ḍarba* (accusative), not *iḍribū*.

**The grammarians resolve this as follows:** there is an **absolute object** here whose verb has been elided (dropped). The implied form is: *fa'ḍribū'r-riqāba ḍarban* — "strike the necks with a striking". The verb has been dropped, the verbal noun has remained. This construction is called **مَصْدَر نَائِب عَن فِعْلِه**: the verbal noun that stands in place of its verb.

**And what does this do?**

The books of rhetoric record two functions of this construction:

| Function | Explanation |
|---|---|
| **Abbreviation (*ikhtiṣār*)** | A verbal noun in place of an imperative compresses the utterance. The language of urgent situations. |
| **Emphasis (*taʾkīd*)** | The absolute object comes in order to strengthen the verb. |

**And there is a third point, often recorded by the lexicographers:** the verbal noun, unlike the verb, **carries no tense and no person.** The verb says "you, strike"; the verbal noun says only "striking". That is, the expression ceases to be a command directed at a person and becomes **the description of a situation**.

**I record its fit with the scene as an observation:** the sentence is as short as the moment of encounter itself. This is not a place for building long sentences.

### ٱلرِّقَاب — "necks"

**Root: ر-ق-ب.** And the family of senses in this root is interesting: *raqaba* — neck; *raqīb* — one who watches; *murāqaba* — watching; *irtiqāb* — waiting and watching.

The lexicographers make the connection thus: **the one who watches cranes his neck.** That is, *murāqaba* is a work done with the neck; the word has passed from the organ to the act.

**رِقَاب** — the plural of *raqaba*.

**The Qurʾān uses this word elsewhere in a wholly different context, and this needs to be recorded:**

- *"…and for [the freeing of] **necks**"* — **وَفِى ٱلرِّقَابِ** (al-Baqara 2/177 and al-Tawba 9/60): one of the categories of alms is the bringing of slaves to freedom, and the Qurʾān names this by saying "necks".
- *"…the freeing of a **neck**"* — **فَتَحْرِيرُ رَقَبَةٍ** (al-Nisāʾ 4/92, al-Māʾida 5/89, al-Mujādala 58/3 — the last treated in `058-mucadele.md`).
- *"**The loosing of a neck**"* — **فَكُّ رَقَبَةٍ** (al-Balad 90/13; treated in `090-beled.md`).

**That is, the overwhelming majority of this word's occurrences in the Qurʾān are in the context of a neck's being *loosed*.** The word occurs in seven places in the Qurʾān; six of them have to do with the loosing of a bond. The only use in the context of war is **this verse**.

**I record this distribution as a textual fact.** I draw no ruling from it; but seeing where the weight of the word lies within the Qurʾān corrects the picture in the hands of one who reads the verse.

**Why not "kill them" but "the striking of necks"?** Two explanations are given in the classical commentaries: (a) **metonymy** (*kināya*) — killing named in its most explicit form; (b) that this is the place of the blow which in battle is most effective and gives the quickest result, so that the expression also includes **not prolonging the suffering**. **I transmit this second explanation; I do not present it as a firm report.**

### حَتَّىٰٓ إِذَآ أَثْخَنتُمُوهُمْ — the root ث-خ-ن

**This is the turning point of the verse, and the concrete sense of the root carries everything.**

**Root: ث-خ-ن.** Its primary sense is **to thicken, to grow dense, to grow heavy.**

- **ثَخِين** (*thakhīn*) — thick, dense. Of a liquid: viscous, having lost its fluidity. Of a cloth: thick.
- **ثَخُنَ ٱلشَّىْءُ** — the thing thickened, grew heavy.
- **أَثْخَنَ** (Form *ifʿāl*) — to thicken something, to make it heavy; and from here: **to bring someone to a state where he cannot move, to land a heavy blow and break his resistance.**

The Arabs say *athkhanahu'l-jurḥ*: the wound weighed him down, left him motionless. And *athkhana fi'l-arḍ* — to press down heavily in the land, to establish dominance.

**That is, the picture of the word is this: something that has been flowing thickens and stops.** Conflict is a flow; *ithkhān* is the change in the consistency of that flow to the point of stopping.

**An exact English equivalent is hard to find. "To subdue" is close; but "to thicken / to weigh down" gives the root better.** I use "subdue" and write the real picture of the root beside it.

**The word occurs in two places in the Qurʾān:**

| Place | Expression |
|---|---|
| **al-Anfāl 8/67** | *"It is not for a prophet to take captives until he has **subdued** in the land"* — *ḥattā yuthkhina fi'l-arḍ* |
| **Muḥammad 47/4** | *"…then, when you have **subdued** them"* — *ḥattā idhā athkhantumūhum* |

**The two verses use the same word and both describe the same threshold: taking captives only after *ithkhān*.** The relation between the two is one of the most disputed matters in the classical commentaries, and I shall take it up below in the table of disagreement.

### حَتَّىٰ — the threshold itself

The particle **حَتَّىٰ** here announces a **limit** (*ghāya*): "until…". And this is the most important structural element of the verse.

**There are two *ḥattā*s in the verse:**

1. **حَتَّىٰٓ إِذَآ أَثْخَنتُمُوهُمْ** — "until you have subdued them".
2. **حَتَّىٰ تَضَعَ ٱلْحَرْبُ أَوْزَارَهَا** — "until the war lays down its burdens".

**That is, nothing in the sentence has been left without a limit.** The striking has an upper bound (*ithkhān*), and the regulation as a whole has an upper bound (the end of the war).

**This is a fact read from the wording of the verse and it needs to be put on record:** the text sets two separate end points for itself.

### فَشُدُّوا۟ ٱلْوَثَاقَ — the tightening of the bond

**شَدَّ** — root ش-د-د: to tighten, to make firm, to strengthen. From the same root *shadīd* (severe), *ashadd* (stronger).

**وَثَاق** — root و-ث-ق: to be firm, to be trustworthy. From the same root **مِيثَاق** (*mīthāq* — a firm covenant), **وَثِيقَة** (*wathīqa* — a document), **ثِقَة** (*thiqa* — trust).

**وَثَاق**, for its part, names the thing that is tied, the **bond, the rope**.

**And there is a point of language here:** from the same root come both "the covenant that gives assurance" (*mīthāq*) and "the captive's bond" (*wathāq*). The common core is **firmness and not coming loose**. *Mīthāq* is the word that will not be undone; *wathāq* is the bond that will not be undone.

**I transmit this relation by way of the root sense the lexicographers record; the picture drawn between the two words is my own inference.**

**And what the sentence says here is a *change of status*.** Where the striking ends is where the binding begins. The one who is bound is no longer a combatant — he is a **captive**. The verse makes this transition with a single *fāʾ*: *fa-shuddū.*

### فَإِمَّا مَنًّۢا بَعْدُ وَإِمَّا فِدَآءً — two options, and no third

**This is the most frequently skipped place in the verse, and it is the place this commentary will dwell on most.**

What is to happen after the captive is bound? The verse gives two options:

**إِمَّا … وَإِمَّا** — in Arabic this is the pattern of **leaving a choice** (*takhyīr*): "either… or…". It announces that one of two alternatives is to be chosen.

| Option | Word | Its meaning |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | **مَنًّۢا** (*mannan*) | **Release without payment** — setting free as a favour |
| **2** | **فِدَآءً** (*fidāʾan*) | **Release for a ransom** — taking a price, or by exchange of captives |

**And no third option is mentioned.**

I write this plainly because this part of the verse often stays invisible: **all the possibilities the verse counts regarding captives are two forms of setting free.** Both end with the captive's release; the difference between them is whether the release is with or without a price.

**This is not an exegetical interpretation; it is the wording of the verse itself.** The pattern *immā… wa immā* enumerates the alternatives counted. In this verse the two alternatives enumerated are these.

**And the order too is worth noting: *mann* comes first.** Release without price is mentioned before release for a price. In Arabic, priority in order does not always announce superiority; but I record it as an observation.

### مَنّ — the root م-ن-ن

**The root م-ن-ن has been treated in several places in this commentary:** `002-bakara.md` (2/262-264 — *lā tubṭilū ṣadaqātikum bi'l-**manni** wa'l-adhā*), `095-tin.md`, `068-kalem.md`, `049-hucurat.md` (49/17 — *yamunnūna ʿalayka an aslamū*), `074-muddessir.md`. **I do not repeat it here.**

The two directions the root carries were recorded earlier in this commentary, and here they are decisive:

| Direction | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **Positive** | **To give without return, to bestow a favour** | *al-Mannān*; God's favour at al-Baqara 2/262 |
| **Negative** | **To throw a gift back in the face of the one who received it** | al-Baqara 2/264: *bi'l-manni wa'l-adhā* |

And the measure recorded in `049-hucurat.md`: **the difference lies not in what is given but in what is done after the giving.**

**In this verse the word is in the *first* sense: release without payment.** *Mannan* — an absolute object whose verb has been dropped (the implied form: *fa-immā an tamunnū mannan*).

**And the primary sense of the root matters here.** The lexicographers also connect *mann* with "cutting": *manna'l-ḥabl* — he cut the rope. From here: to bestow is to **cut** a bond and give without return; the one who receives is **cut off** from the debt of return.

**What the word carries in this verse is exactly that: the bond is undone and no return is asked for it.** In the preceding clause the bond had been **tightened** (*shuddu'l-wathāq*); here the same bond can be undone with no price taken at all.

**I record this reversal of direction between the two clauses as my own reading.**

### فِدَاء — the root ف-د-ي

**Root: ف-د-ي.** Its primary sense: **to save one thing by giving another; to get back by paying a price.**

- **فِدْيَة** (*fidya*) — the price paid in place of an obligation. At al-Baqara 2/184, the *fidya* of one who cannot fast.
- **فِدَاء** (*fidāʾ*) — the price of ransom.
- **إِفْتِدَاء** — to ransom oneself. This root was taken up in `070-mearic.md` (al-Maʿārij 70/11-14: *yawaddu'l-mujrimu law **yaftadī** min ʿadhābi yawmi-idhin bi-banīh*) and in `057-hadid.md`.

**The basic idea the root carries is an *exchange*:** something is given, something is taken.

**And in the context of war two forms of *fidāʾ* are recorded in the classical sources:** release for property, and **the exchange of captives** (a trade against the Muslim captives on the other side). The second form is closer to the root sense of the word — for there the exchange is, in the full sense of the word, a life for a life.

### حَتَّىٰ تَضَعَ ٱلْحَرْبُ أَوْزَارَهَا — the war's laying down of its burdens

**This is one of the most striking expressions in the Qurʾān, and its word order deserves particular examination.**

**Who is the subject of the sentence?** The subject of the verb *taḍaʿa* is **ٱلْحَرْب** — **the war** itself.

That is, the sentence does not say: "until you lay down your weapons", or "until those who fight set down their burdens". It says: **until the war lays down its own burdens.**

**This is an *istiʿāra makniyya*** (an implicit metaphor): the war is imagined as a being carrying a load on its back. And that being sets its load on the ground at the end of the road.

### أَوْزَار — the root و-ز-ر

**This root has been treated in three places in this commentary:** `094-insirah.md` (**وِزْر** — a heavy load, and from there sin), `075-kiyame.md` (**وَزَر** — a refuge, a rock on a mountain), and `053-necm.md` (53/38: *allā taziru wāziratun wizra ukhrā* — and there the word **وَزِير**, "the one who shares the load"). **I do not repeat it here.**

What was recorded there and is decisive here is this:

> "At the centre of the root lies the idea of **weight and mass**. *Wazar* is the steep and **heavy** mass of rock on a mountain. *Wizr* is the **weight** carried. *Wazīr* is the one who shares the weight."

**What I shall add here is the particular use of the root in this verse.**

**أَوْزَار** — the plural of *wizr*: loads, weights. And there are **two views** on what the word means in this verse:

| View | Explanation | Ground |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | **The loads of those who fight** = **weapons and the instruments of war.** "Until the war lays down its weapons" — that is, until the war ends. | The weapon is the heaviest load the fighter carries; calling a weapon a "load" is a known usage in Arabic. |
| **2** | **The war's own loads** = **the weights, hardships and sins the war brings.** | The dominant use of the root in the Qurʾān is "sin"; and the subject is the war itself, not those who fight. |

**Both explanations are found in the classical commentaries, and I make no preference.**

I record this much: **both explanations arrive at the same result** — the expression describes the **ending** of the war. The difference between them lies not in what brings it to an end but in what the "load" points to.

**And an observation about the expression itself — it is my own reading:** the Qurʾān does not describe the end of the war as "until victory is won" or "until the enemy is destroyed". It describes it as **the setting down of a load**. That choice positions war not as a gain but as a **weight**. A value judgement is hidden inside the expression: what is carried is something to be put down.

**I do not present this as a binding exegesis;** but the choice of the word stands in the text, and the "weight" sense of the root is beyond dispute.

### ذَٰلِكَ — the word that stands alone

In the middle of the sentence, a demonstrative standing alone: **ذَٰلِكَ**.

The grammarians resolve this in several ways: the predicate of an elided subject (*al-amru dhālika* — "the matter is this"), or the subject of an elided predicate (*dhālika ḥukmuhum* — "this is the ruling concerning them").

**Its function is clear: it seals a section.** What has been said up to here was a regulation; it says *dhālika* and the regulation closes. What follows is another subject.

### وَلَوْ يَشَآءُ ٱللَّهُ لَٱنتَصَرَ مِنْهُمْ — the qualification the verse puts upon itself

**And now comes the most striking sentence of the verse.**

**لَوْ** — in Arabic this is the particle of the **counterfactual condition** (*imtināʿiyya*): it announces a condition that has not come about and is not expected to. "Had it been so, it would have been thus — but it was not."

**The sentence says this: "Had God willed, He would Himself have taken retribution from them."**

**ٱنتَصَرَ** — root ن-ص-ر, Form VIII. **This root has been treated in many places in this commentary** (`059-hasr.md`, `061-saff.md`, `054-kamer.md`, `067-mulk.md`, `071-nuh.md`, `072-cin.md`, `086-tarik.md`); and the *intiṣār* pattern in particular was taken up in `051-zariyat.md` and `055-rahman.md`: **to help oneself, to take one's own due, to take one's revenge.** **I do not repeat it here.**

**This sentence of the verse binds everything regulated before it to a qualification.**

It says: this is not a matter God could not have handled otherwise. It is not a question of power. Had it been a question of power, *la'ntaṣara minhum* — He would have handled it Himself.

**Why, then, is it so?**

### وَلَٰكِن لِّيَبْلُوَا۟ بَعْضَكُم بِبَعْضٍ — the ground

**لَٰكِن** — the particle of correction (*istidrāk*): "but".

**لِيَبْلُوَ** — the *lām* of purpose (*taʿlīl*) + the root **ب-ل-و**: **to try, to test.**

**Root: ب-ل-و.** The primary sense the lexicographers record is interesting: **to wear a thing out, to make it threadbare.** *Baliya'th-thawb* — the garment wore out. From the same root **بَالٍ** (worn out).

**The connection between testing and wearing out is this:** to try a thing is to use it; and use wears it out. The trial wears down the one tried.

**This root has been touched on in other places in this commentary;** what is to be added here is the **object** of the word in this verse.

**بَعْضَكُم بِبَعْضٍ** — "some of you by means of others".

**Note: the object is not "them".** The sentence does not say "We shall test you by means of them". It says "**some of you by means of others**". The word *baʿḍ* occurs twice, both times indefinite.

**The meaning of this needs to be dwelt on.** The sentence takes both sides inside the same pronoun. The trial is not one-directional; it is mutual. For one side to be a test for the other means that the other is a test for that side too.

**And this is the second qualification set upon the verse's regulation of war:** the conflict is positioned not as an end but as **a ground of testing**. Not a result, but a process.

**This reading rests on the wording of the text** (*li-yabluwa* — the ground of testing is given explicitly); but the conclusion I draw from it, that conflict is not an end, **is my own inference** and is not binding.

### وَٱلَّذِينَ قُتِلُوا۟ فِى سَبِيلِ ٱللَّهِ — and a variant reading

**There is a variant reading and it affects the meaning:**

| Reading | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **قُتِلُوا۟** (*qutilū*) — passive, perfect | "**those who were killed**" |
| **قَاتَلُوا۟** (*qātalū*) — Form III (*mufāʿala*), perfect | "**those who fought**" |

**Both readings are transmitted.** The difference is not trivial: the first makes having been killed a condition; the second holds having fought to be sufficient.

**I make no preference.** I record this: the two readings do not cancel each other, they **complete** each other. One puts the outcome first, the other the act.

**The qualification فِى سَبِيلِ ٱللَّهِ must not be passed over either.** This qualification was the object of the verb *ṣadd* in the first verse (*ṣaddū ʿan sabīli'llāh* — they turned others away from the path of God). Now the same construct becomes the place to which the death is attributed.

**And the measure recorded on 61/4 in `061-saff.md` holds here too:**

> "The qualification **فِى سَبِيلِهِۦ** must not be passed over either: 'in His path'. Not every war."

### فَلَن يُضِلَّ أَعْمَٰلَهُمْ — a return to the first verse

**And the verse closes by returning to the first sentence of the sūra.**

| | **47/1** | **47/4** |
|---|---|---|
| Subject | Concealed (God) | Concealed (God) |
| Verb | **أَضَلَّ** — made them lose their way | **لَن يُضِلَّ** — will never make them lose their way |
| Object | **أَعْمَٰلَهُمْ** | **أَعْمَٰلَهُمْ** |
| Tense | Perfect | Imperfect + **لَن** (perpetual negation) |

**The same verb, the same object, the opposite ruling.** And the negation is made with **لَن** — the strongest negation of the future in Arabic.

**This says something about the structure of the verse.** The verse began with the regulation of the field of battle; it continued with the qualification about power; it went on with the ground of testing; and it ended with the promise that **the deeds will not be lost**. That is, it began with a regulation and closed with **deed** — with the key word of the sūra.

---

### The disagreement: the relation between this verse and al-Anfāl 8/67 and other verses

**The debate the classical commentaries conduct around this verse is essentially the relation of the following three texts to one another:**

- **Muḥammad 47/4** — after subduing, bind; then either *mann* or *fidāʾ*.
- **al-Anfāl 8/67-70** — *"It is not for a prophet to take captives until he has subdued in the land…"* and then verse 70, which addresses the captives.
- **al-Tawba 9/5** — the verses regulating the rulings of war and peace.

**The views transmitted, in a table:**

| # | View | Ground |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | **There is no abrogation; the two verses say the same thing.** al-Anfāl forbids the taking of captives before *ithkhān*; Muḥammad 47/4 regulates what is to be done **after** *ithkhān*. The two are consecutive; they do not conflict. | Both verses use the root **ث-خ-ن** as the same threshold; one regulates what precedes the threshold, the other what follows it. |
| **2** | **Muḥammad 47/4 abrogated al-Anfāl 8/67**; that is, the ruling on taking and releasing captives came later. | It rests on reports about the order of revelation. |
| **3** | **Muḥammad 47/4 has been abrogated**; the ruling was changed by verses that came later. | It rests on other reports about the order of revelation. |
| **4** | **There is no abrogation; the ruling has been left to the discretion of the head of state or the commander** (*takhyīr*). The verse's *immā… wa immā* pattern is already a pattern of choice. | The pattern itself announces *takhyīr*. |

**In this commentary I make no preference and issue no legal ruling.** Per the method (`STYLE.md`), the views of the schools of law are transmitted and no ruling is issued; and I have no sound measure by which to prefer among the reports concerning the order of revelation.

**What is to be recorded is this:** the debate itself shows that the verse has not been read as **a general ruling standing alone and settled**. The classical exegetical tradition has always taken this verse up **together with other verses**. To take the verse by itself and present it as a general ruling is the exact opposite of what that tradition does.

### The limits the verse sets for itself — gathered together

**The limits that can be read directly from the wording of the text:**

| # | Limit | Its ground in the wording |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | The ruling is tied to the state of **encounter** | **فَإِذَا لَقِيتُمُ** — the particle of condition |
| **2** | The scene is **the field of battle** | the use of the verb *liqāʾ* in the context of war; the words *riqāb*, *wathāq*, *ḥarb* |
| **3** | The striking has an **upper threshold** | **حَتَّىٰٓ إِذَآ أَثْخَنتُمُوهُمْ** |
| **4** | After the threshold the act is **binding**, not killing | **فَشُدُّوا۟ ٱلْوَثَاقَ** |
| **5** | For captives **only two alternatives** are counted, and both are release | **فَإِمَّا مَنًّۢا … وَإِمَّا فِدَآءً** |
| **6** | The regulation has an **end point** | **حَتَّىٰ تَضَعَ ٱلْحَرْبُ أَوْزَارَهَا** |
| **7** | That the matter **has nothing to do with power** is written explicitly | **وَلَوْ يَشَآءُ ٱللَّهُ لَٱنتَصَرَ مِنْهُمْ** |
| **8** | The ground is **mutual testing** | **لِّيَبْلُوَا۟ بَعْضَكُم بِبَعْضٍ** |

**All eight of these items are in the verse's own wording.** Not one of them is a qualification brought in from outside.

**And to "tear a verse from its context" means exactly this:** to drop the first and third of the eight items above and take the second clause on its own. In that state the verse says something else — but what it says is no longer what the verse says.

### What this says to the present

**I have to be careful here, because the most dangerous thing that could be written under the heading "what this says to the present" for this verse is to attach it to a conflict of today. I shall not do that.**

There are two observations that can be drawn from the verse and that are **attached to no side**:

**One. The hardest sentence of a text is read together with the qualifications the text itself sets.** This verse carries eight separate qualifications, and all of them are inside the text. To take the hard sentence and leave the qualifications while reading a text is not reading but selecting. This is a measure valid not only for this verse but for every text.

**Two. Both of the alternatives the verse counts regarding captives are release** — and this is a regulation worth noting within the text's own historical context. From the moment the fighting is subdued the verse **changes the status** of the captive: he ceases to be a combatant and becomes one who has been bound, and finally one who is to be released.

**I record one further inference from here — it is my own reading:** the structure of the verse is built upon violence **setting a limit to itself**. Two *ḥattā*s, one *fa-shuddū*, one *immā… wa immā*. The text says where what it permits will end, in the very place where it permits it.

---

## 47/5-6 — سَيَهْدِيهِمْ وَيُصْلِحُ بَالَهُمْ · وَيُدْخِلُهُمُ ٱلْجَنَّةَ عَرَّفَهَا لَهُمْ

*Sa-yahdīhim wa yuṣliḥu bālahum · Wa yudkhiluhumu'l-jannata ʿarrafahā lahum*

"He will guide them and set right their **bāl** · And He will bring them into the garden He has made known to them."

### Word order: four verbs, one subject

The sentence that began at the end of the fourth verse continues here. The same subject (concealed, God), the same object (those killed in the path of God), and four verbs:

| # | Verb | Tense | What it does |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **لَن يُضِلَّ** (4) | Future, negative | Will not make the deeds lost |
| 2 | **سَيَهْدِى** (5) | Future (with the *sīn*) | Will guide |
| 3 | **يُصْلِحُ** (5) | Imperfect | Will set the *bāl* right |
| 4 | **يُدْخِلُ** (6) | Imperfect | Will bring into the garden |

**The order needs to be examined.** The expected order is this: the one who dies receives his reward. But the verse puts **guidance** first.

Guiding is something done for a person who is walking. Here, though, the one addressed is a person who has been **killed**. In the classical commentaries this is explained as "his being guided on his way in the hereafter"; some say it is "the continuation of their being guided while they were in this world". **Both explanations are transmitted; I make no preference.**

This much is visible from the text: **the verb presupposes a *walking* even after death.** The reward is not a state of standing still.

### يُصْلِحُ بَالَهُمْ — the link with the second verse

**In the second verse this expression came in the *perfect*: أَصْلَحَ بَالَهُمْ. Here it comes in the *imperfect*: يُصْلِحُ بَالَهُمْ.**

**The same expression, in the same sūra, in two different tenses.**

| Verse | Tense | To whom |
|---|---|---|
| **2** | **أَصْلَحَ** — perfect | To those who believe and do righteous deeds |
| **5** | **يُصْلِحُ** — imperfect | To those killed in the path of God |

**And two of the four occurrences of the word *bāl* in the Qurʾān are these two.** The word is used twice in the sūra, both times together with a single verb.

**What this does needs to be recorded — and this is my own reading:**

The second verse gave a promise to living believers and used the perfect: **the setting right of the inward state is something already done.** The fifth verse gives it to those who have died and uses the imperfect: **the setting right is something that will continue.**

That is, the sūra puts the same promise at both ends of life: to the living, "it has happened"; to the dead, "it will happen".

**I do not present this distinction as a binding exegesis;** the difference of tense stands in the text, the meaning I draw from it is mine. It is also possible that the difference of tense arises from the context in which the sentences stand (one where past rulings are being listed, the other where future promises are).

### عَرَّفَهَا لَهُمْ — three readings

**This two-word expression is one of the places where the classical commentaries differ most.**

**عَرَّفَ** — root **ع-ر-ف**, Form *tafʿīl*. The family of primary senses in the root is wide:

- **عَرَفَ** — he knew, he recognised.
- **مَعْرِفَة** — the knowledge of recognition.
- **عُرْف** — 1) custom, what is known and accepted; 2) the **mane** of a horse, the comb of a cock — that is, a rising edge; 3) **sweet scent**.
- **عَرَفَة / عَرَفَات** — a place name.
- **تَعْرِيف** — to make known; and also **to delimit, to determine** (in grammar *taʿrīf* is the making of a noun definite).

**And the three separate branches of this root give the verse three separate readings:**

| # | Reading | Which branch of the root | Meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| **1** | **"He made it known to them"** | *maʿrifa* — to know | On entering the garden they know it as they know their own houses; they do not ask the way |
| **2** | **"He made it sweet-scented"** | *ʿarf* — sweet scent | The garden has been made fragrant for them |
| **3** | **"He determined it, marked it off for them"** | *taʿrīf* — determination, *ʿurfa* — boundary | Each one's portion has been set apart in advance, its boundary drawn |

**All three readings are found in the classical commentaries. I make no preference.**

I record this observation — **it is my own reading:** all three readings look in the same direction. Recognition, scent, and the drawing of a boundary — all of them describe **the lifting of strangeness**. To know a place the first time you enter it, to know its scent, and to know that your own place there has been set apart: all three say "this belongs to you".

**I present this as an attempt at a synthesis, not as a preference.**

### The word order of the clause: an adjective or a circumstantial?

**The parsing of the clause عَرَّفَهَا لَهُمْ has also been disputed:** is it an adjective qualifying **ٱلْجَنَّةَ** ("the garden He has made known to them"), or a circumstantial ("having made the garden known to them"), or an independent sentence ("He has made it known to them")?

The difference of meaning is not great; **I content myself with recording it.**

One detail: the verb is in the **perfect** (*ʿarrafa*), whereas the verbs around it are in the imperfect (*sa-yahdī, yuṣliḥu, yudkhilu*). That is, a past verb has come in among three future ones.

**This shows that the making known is *before the entry*** — I record it as an observation. The bringing into the garden is in the future; the making known is done and finished.

---

## 47/7 — يَٰٓأَيُّهَا ٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوٓا۟ إِن تَنصُرُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ يَنصُرْكُمْ وَيُثَبِّتْ أَقْدَامَكُمْ

*Yā ayyuha'lladhīna āmanū in tanṣuru'llāha yanṣurkum wa yuthabbit aqdāmakum*

"O you who believe! If you help God, He will help you and make your feet firm."

### The first call of the sūra

**For six verses the sūra spoke in the third person.** "Those who deny", "those who believe", "they". There is no one addressed; there is a situation recounted.

**And in the seventh verse direct address comes for the first time:** *yā ayyuha'lladhīna āmanū.*

This will happen twice in the sūra: **7** and **33**. Both come with a command, and both are points where the sūra changes direction.

| Verse | Call | What follows |
|---|---|---|
| **7** | *yā ayyuha'lladhīna āmanū* | **إِن تَنصُرُوا۟** — a conditional sentence |
| **33** | *yā ayyuha'lladhīna āmanū* | **أَطِيعُوا۟ … وَلَا تُبْطِلُوا۟** — command + prohibition |

**The first call brings an offer, the second a warning.** And the twenty-six verses between them recount why the warning is needed.

### The construction of the condition

**The sentence is a conditional and it has three parts:**

**إِن** (the particle of condition) + **تَنصُرُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ** (the verb of the condition, jussive) + **يَنصُرْكُمْ وَيُثَبِّتْ** (the answer, jussive)

**All three verbs are jussive** (ending in sukūn): *tanṣurū* (the *nūn* dropped), *yanṣur* (the vowel of the *rāʾ* dropped), *yuthabbit* (the vowel of the *tāʾ* dropped).

**This shows the grammatical tightness of the sentence:** three verbs locked to one another by the same grammatical bond. If the condition is broken the answer is broken too; the two are a single structure.

**And note the choice of particle: إِنْ, not إِذَا.**

- **إِذَا** — for conditions whose occurrence is expected, close to certain (in the preceding verse there was *fa-**idhā** laqītum*).
- **إِنْ** — for conditions whose occurrence is not certain, conditions of possibility.

**That is, "helping God" is not, in the verse's own language, a *certain* thing; it is a possible thing.** The one addressed is a community that has believed, and even so the condition is built with *in*.

**This is an observation about word order.** In Arabic this distinction is not applied rigidly everywhere; but when two verses in succession use two different particles it is worth noticing.

### إِن تَنصُرُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ — "to help God"

**On a first reading the expression raises a question:** does God need help?

**And the verse itself had already closed this question in the preceding verse.** The fourth verse had said: *"Had God willed, He would Himself have taken retribution from them."* That is, that it is not a question of power was written explicitly three verses earlier.

**In the classical commentaries the explanation of this expression is given thus:** "helping God" is helping His religion, His messenger and His command — the governed term has been elided (*in tanṣurū **dīna**'llāh*). This explanation is widespread and reasonable.

**I add an observation to it — it is my own reading:** the verse chooses to elide the governed term and say directly "if you help God". This does a piece of work: it says **where the work done goes**. When a person does a work as "service to religion", what he serves may be an institution, a community or an opinion. By naming the recipient of the service directly, the verse closes off that drift.

**This is a reading and it is not binding.**

### يَنصُرْكُمْ — and the concrete sense of the root

**Root: ن-ص-ر.** **This root has been treated in many places in this commentary:** `059-hasr.md`, `061-saff.md`, `054-kamer.md`, `067-mulk.md`, `071-nuh.md`, `072-cin.md`, `086-tarik.md`, and `110-nasr.md`. **I do not repeat it here.**

Two of the things recorded there are decisive here:

> `067-mulk.md`: "*Nuṣra* is standing by someone in the face of an enemy. The English word 'help' is broader; *naṣr* is narrower and carries the smell of war."
>
> `059-hasr.md` and `061-saff.md`: "The root has another sense as well: *naṣara'l-ghaythu'l-arḍ* — the rain helped the earth, that is, it brought it to life."

**What is to be added here is that the word occurs *twice* in this verse** — the same root, the same form, one in the second person and one in the third:

**تَنصُرُوا۟ … يَنصُرْ**

The same verb, in two directions. And note: the verse does not give the requital with **a different word**. It does not say "if you help you will receive a reward"; it says "if you help **you will be helped**".

**This is the plainest form of the figure of reciprocity (*muqābala*):** the subject and the object of the same verb change places.

### وَيُثَبِّتْ أَقْدَامَكُمْ — a concrete picture

**And the second part of the requital is far more concrete than the first.**

**ثَبَّتَ** — root **ث-ب-ت**, Form *tafʿīl*. The primary sense of the root: **to stand in place, to remain fixed, not to be dislodged.** *Thabata'sh-shayʾ* — the thing stood in place.

And the root has a concrete use for plants too: a tree's root **holding** in the soil. The Qurʾān uses this picture at Ibrāhīm 14/24: *"a good tree whose root is firm (**thābit**) and whose branch is in the sky."*

**أَقْدَام** — the plural of *qadam*: **feet.** The root is **ق-د-م**, and from the same root *taqdīm* (to put forward), *qadīm* (that which comes before), *muqaddima*. **The foot is what is put forward** — what goes first in walking.

**Now let us look at the picture itself.**

What is the physical form of defeat on a battlefield? **The slipping of the feet.** A rank breaks when it leaves its place; and leaving its place is the feet going backwards. In Arabic the idiom *zallat qadamuh* — "his foot slipped" — describes both a physical and a moral fall.

**The verse takes an abstract promise of "help" and brings it down under the feet.**

- *yanṣurkum* — abstract, wide, hard to measure.
- *yuthabbit aqdāmakum* — concrete, narrow, seen at once.

**The second says how the first comes about.** Help is described not as something coming down from the sky but as **the foot's holding on the ground**.

**And this must be read together with the next verse of the sūra** — because the eighth verse will give exactly the opposite of this picture.

### The exegesis of the Qurʾān by the Qurʾān: *thabāt* and *aqdām*

This pair occurs together in several places in the Qurʾān, and all of them are in the context of war:

- **al-Baqara 2/250** — *"Our Lord! Pour patience upon us, **make our feet firm** and help us against the denying people"* (*wa **thabbit aqdāmanā** wa'nṣurnā*). **Note: in this prayer too *thabāt* and *nuṣra* stand side by side.**
- **Āl ʿImrān 3/147** — *"…and **make our feet firm**"* (the same expression, again inside a prayer).
- **al-Anfāl 8/11** — *"…to strengthen your hearts and with it to **make the feet firm**"* (*wa yuthabbita bihi'l-**aqdām***).

**al-Anfāl 8/11 is particularly striking:** there what makes the feet firm is **the rain** — the water that came down hardened the sand beneath the foot.

**And when this comes side by side with the rain-related sense of the root ن-ص-ر (*naṣara'l-ghaythu'l-arḍ*), an interesting weave is formed:** help too is rain, and what makes the foot firm is also rain.

**I record this weave as my own reading;** both data have their counterpart in the lexicon and in the text, but the connection between them is one I am making, and it carries no claim of a play on words.

### What this says to the present

The concrete picture of the verse works outside the field of battle as well.

**The real difficulty in sustaining something is not beginning but staying in place.** To set out on a work is a decision made once; to stay in that work is a decision made anew every day. And most of the time people do not abandon the work — **they withdraw slowly.** The foot slips.

The promise of the verse looks exactly here: the form of the help is not a miracle but **being able to keep a footing on the ground**.

**I record this as an application drawn from the verse;** the wording of the verse is in the context of war, and the extension here is my own reading.

---

## 47/8 — وَٱلَّذِينَ كَفَرُوا۟ فَتَعْسًا لَّهُمْ وَأَضَلَّ أَعْمَٰلَهُمْ

*Wa'lladhīna kafarū fa-taʿsan lahum wa aḍalla aʿmālahum*

"And as for those who deny: a fall on the face for them! [God] has made their deeds go astray."

### تَعْس — the root ت-ع-س and the exact opposition with the seventh verse

**Root: ت-ع-س.** The concrete sense the lexicographers record is this: **to fall on one's face, to stumble and go headlong.** *Taʿisa'r-rajul* — the man stumbled and fell on his face. As its opposite they record **إِنْتِعَاش** (to straighten up and rise).

The word is also used as a pattern of imprecation: *taʿsan lak* — "may your face come to the ground".

**And now look at the preceding verse.**

| | **47/7** | **47/8** |
|---|---|---|
| To whom | To those who believe | To those who deny |
| What is given | **وَيُثَبِّتْ أَقْدَامَكُمْ** — He makes your feet firm | **فَتَعْسًا لَّهُمْ** — a fall on the face for them |
| The picture | **The foot holds on the ground** | **The foot slips, the face comes to the ground** |

**Two verses in succession give the two ends of the same physical picture.**

This is an opposition standing in the text, and it is invisible unless the concrete senses of the words are known.
