Set "He makes your feet firm" beside "may they perish" and no link appears; but set "the foot holds on the ground" beside "to go headlong on the face" and the link comes out of itself.

**And this is a good example of what root analysis is for:** the concrete sense of words makes the link between verses visible.

### Word order: تَعْسًا — a sentence without a verb

**تَعْسًا** is in the accusative and has no verb. The grammarians count it an **absolute object**; the implied form: *atʿasahumu'llāhu taʿsan* — "may God make them fall on their faces".

**That is, the verb has been dropped and the verbal noun has remained** — just like the *fa-ḍarba'r-riqāb* of the fourth verse. The sūra uses this construction twice.

**And in Arabic this construction is the standard form of sentences of imprecation and prayer:** *saqyan lahu*, *tabban lahu*. Indeed the opening of Sūrat al-Masad (*tabbat yadā Abī Lahab*) was treated in `111-tebbet.md`; the construction there and this verse are of the same family.

### وَأَضَلَّ أَعْمَٰلَهُمْ — an exact repetition of the first verse

**This clause is the last two words of the first verse of the sūra. Letter for letter.**

47/1: … **أَضَلَّ أَعْمَٰلَهُمْ**
47/8: … **وَأَضَلَّ أَعْمَٰلَهُمْ**

The only difference is a *wāw* placed at its head.

**This repetition turns the first eight verses of the sūra into a ring:**

| | |
|---|---|
| **1** | Those who deny → *aḍalla aʿmālahum* |
| **2** | Those who believe → *kaffara / aṣlaḥa* |
| **3** | The ground |
| **4-6** | The regulation of war; and *lan yuḍilla aʿmālahum* |
| **7** | Those who believe → *yanṣur / yuthabbit* |
| **8** | Those who deny → *taʿsan / aḍalla aʿmālahum* |

**Eight verses, opening with the same sentence and closing with the same sentence.** And in between there is a regulation of war. That is, the regulation has been placed between the descriptions of the two groups.

**I record this framing as an observation about word order.**

---

## 47/9 — ذَٰلِكَ بِأَنَّهُمْ كَرِهُوا۟ مَآ أَنزَلَ ٱللَّهُ فَأَحْبَطَ أَعْمَٰلَهُمْ

*Dhālika bi-annahum karihū mā anzala'llāhu fa-aḥbaṭa aʿmālahum*

"That is because they disliked what God sent down; so He voided their deeds."

### The second sentence of ground

The second occurrence of the sūra's *dhālika bi-anna* pattern. In the third verse the ground of verses 1-2 was given; here the ground of verse 8 is given.

**And the ground is not an *act* but a *feeling*.**

In the third verse the ground was **إِتِّبَاع** — following, setting off after; a behaviour. Here the ground is **كَرِهُوا۟** — disliking; an inward state.

**This opens the door to the later sections of the sūra.** The sūra will move gradually from outside inward: from behaviour to feeling, from feeling to the heart (20, 24, 29), and finally to the hidden rancour inside the heart (29, 37).

### كَرِهَ — the root ك-ر-ه

**Root: ك-ر-ه.** The lexicographers give two nuances to two readings:

| Reading | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **كَرْه** (*karh*) — with fatḥa | Compulsion coming from outside; doing something unwillingly |
| **كُرْه** (*kurh*) — with ḍamma | Dislike arising from within, revulsion |

**The common core: a thing's weighing heavily on the soul.** Its opposites are **رِضَا** (*riḍā*) and **مَحَبَّة** (love).

**The root occurs three times in this sūra** and its distribution is instructive:

| Verse | Expression | What they dislike |
|---|---|---|
| **9** | *karihū mā **anzala**'llāh* | What God has **sent down** |
| **26** | *karihū mā **nazzala**'llāh* | What God has **sent down** (a different form) |
| **28** | *wa karihū **riḍwānahu*** | God's **good pleasure** |

**In the three occurrences there are two objects: what is sent down, and good pleasure.** And verse 28 builds an expression that looks odd on a first reading — "to dislike good pleasure" — which I shall take up there.

**There is also a difference of form:** in verse 9 **أَنزَلَ** (*ifʿāl*), in verse 26 **نَزَّلَ** (*tafʿīl*). The same verb, two forms. I shall take this difference up as a whole at verse 20, because there the two forms will stand side by side **in the same sentence**.

### فَأَحْبَطَ أَعْمَٰلَهُمْ — the third verb of annulment

**The sūra speaks for the third time of deeds coming to nothing — and for the third time it uses a different verb.**

| Verse | Verb | Root | Picture |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1, 8 | **أَضَلَّ** | ض-ل-ل | **To make lost** — the deeds are there, their owner cannot find them |
| **9**, 28, 32 | **أَحْبَطَ** | ح-ب-ط | **To swell up and burst** |
| 33 | **لَا تُبْطِلُوا۟** | ب-ط-ل | **To render invalid** |

**The root ح-ب-ط was analysed in full in the section `049-hucurat.md`** (49/2 — *an taḥbaṭa aʿmālukum*). What was recorded there is decisive here and **I do not repeat it**; the summary is this:

> "The concrete use the lexicographers record is this: **حَبِطَتِ ٱلدَّابَّةُ** — the animal ate its fill of a harmful plant in the pasture; its belly swelled; and the swelling killed it. […] That is, *ḥabṭ* carries this picture in its root: **growth from without, rot from within.** […] The deeds do not disappear; **they swell and burst.** The appearance stays, the content goes."

**What is to be added here is that the three verbs stand side by side *in the same sūra*** — and that the three give three separate pictures:

- **إِضْلَال:** the deed is in place, the **connection** has been broken.
- **إِحْبَاط:** the deed has **swollen**, its inside has emptied out.
- **إِبْطَال:** the deed has been counted **invalid**.

**All three give the same result but by three different roads.** And the sūra keeps the third — the one thing that lies in a person's own hands — for the end.

**I record this classification as my own reading.** The senses of the three roots are lexical data; the division of labour among them is a frame I am setting up.

---

## 47/10 — أَفَلَمْ يَسِيرُوا۟ فِى ٱلْأَرْضِ فَيَنظُرُوا۟ كَيْفَ كَانَ عَٰقِبَةُ ٱلَّذِينَ مِن قَبْلِهِمْ دَمَّرَ ٱللَّهُ عَلَيْهِمْ وَلِلْكَٰفِرِينَ أَمْثَٰلُهَا

*A-fa-lam yasīrū fi'l-arḍi fa-yanẓurū kayfa kāna ʿāqibatu'lladhīna min qablihim; dammara'llāhu ʿalayhim, wa li'l-kāfirīna amthāluhā*

"Have they not travelled the earth and looked at how the end of those before them was? God brought ruin down upon them. And for the deniers there are the likes of it."

### Two verbs: to walk and to look

**The verse describes a method of acquiring knowledge, and it divides the method into two verbs:**

1. **يَسِيرُوا۟** — root **س-ي-ر**: to walk, to make one's way, to travel. From the same root *sayr*, *sīra* (a person's course, the way he lives), *sayyāra* (a caravan; in today's Arabic, a motor car).
2. **يَنظُرُوا۟** — root **ن-ظ-ر**: to look. And the use of this root in the Qurʾān is not the same as "to see" (ر-أ-ي): *naẓar* is **a deliberate and attentive looking**; a look turned in a direction. From the same root *naẓariyya* (theory), *intiẓār* (waiting), *munāẓara*.

**And the two verbs are joined with a *fāʾ*: يَسِيرُوا۟ فَيَنظُرُوا۟.**

The *fāʾ* here announces **causation** and **sequence**: that they should walk **and so** look. That is, the looking is the result of the walking; without walking there is no looking.

**This is a sentence about method:** there is something that cannot be seen without getting up from where you are. Knowledge is taken with the foot.

**And note: the second verb is jussive as well** (*yanẓurū*, its *nūn* dropped) — that is, the effect of *lam* reaches the second verb too. Neither has happened: they have neither walked nor looked.

### أَفَلَمْ — interrogation and denial

**The أَ** (hamza) at the head is the particle of interrogation, and here it is an **interrogative of denial** (*istifhām inkārī*): a question that expects no answer and conveys reproach.

**فَلَمْ** — the *fāʾ* links to the preceding context, *lam* negates the past.

**That is, the sentence says not "do they not look?" but "have they not looked?"** Past tense. The possibility was there; it was not used.

### عَٰقِبَة — the root ع-ق-ب

**Root: ع-ق-ب.** Its concrete sense is **heel** — *ʿaqib*. From the same root: *ʿaqaba* (to come after), *taʿqīb* (following up), *ʿiqāb* (punishment — what comes after), *ʿuqbā* (outcome), and the idiom **عَقِب**: *inqalaba ʿalā ʿaqibayh* — "he turned upon his heels", that is, he went back.

**And the word *ʿāqiba* means exactly "what comes after":** what follows on the heels of a thing, its end.

**This gives the picture the root has chosen:** an outcome stands *behind* the event. To see it one has to get behind the event — and this is the same idea as the **تَدَبُّر** we shall take up in the twenty-fourth verse (د-ب-ر: behind).

**Two roots, two different words, the same picture: to see the end of a thing, look behind it.**

**I record this connection as my own reading;** that both roots carry the sense of "behind" is lexical data, the parallel between them is a connection I am making.

### دَمَّرَ ٱللَّهُ عَلَيْهِمْ — and a particle

**دَمَّرَ** — root **د-م-ر**, Form *tafʿīl*: to destroy, to demolish, to lay waste. From the same root *tadmīr*, *damār*.

**And a grammatical detail:** the verb has been made transitive with the particle **عَلَىٰ** — *dammara **ʿalayhim***. Yet the verb is normally transitive directly: *dammarahum* (He destroyed them).

**The grammarians explain this usage thus:** the particle *ʿalā* announces that the ruin came down **upon** them. That is, the verb takes on the sense not of "He destroyed them" but of **"He brought ruin down upon them"** — as though a building had collapsed on top of them.

And this is a picture the Qurʾān uses elsewhere. al-Naḥl 16/26: *"…God tore out their buildings from the foundations, and the roof fell down upon them from above"* (*fa-kharra ʿalayhimu's-saqfu min fawqihim*).

**The two expressions point in the same direction: the ruin comes not from the side but from above.**

### وَلِلْكَٰفِرِينَ أَمْثَٰلُهَا

**There is a problem of the pronoun, and the exegetes have dwelt on it:** *amthāluhā* — "the likes of it". The pronoun **هَا** is feminine; what does it refer to?

| View | Referent | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | An elided word (*al-ʿāqiba* — the end) | "The likes of that end" |
| **2** | An elided word (*al-tadmīr* — the ruin) | "The likes of that ruin" |

**The difference is not great; I content myself with recording it.**

**And this is the second occurrence of the root م-ث-ل in the sūra** (3, **10**, 38). In the third verse God was giving people "their likenesses"; here for the deniers there are "the likes of it"; and the last word of the sūra will again be from this root.

### What this says to the present

**The verse proposes looking at history as a *source of knowledge*** — and it proposes it not as a reading but as a **walking**.

The distinction is worth noting. The verse does not say "have they not read the accounts of them?" It says "have they not travelled the earth and looked?" That is, what is proposed is that the ruin be seen **in its place**.

Why this makes a difference can be dwelt on — and this is **my own reading:** a ruin recounted leaves the hearer with a piece of information; a ruin seen leaves him with a **measure**. To stand before the remains of a city shows at one and the same time that the city once stood and that those who stood in it did not think of this.

**What this verse says to the present is here, and there is no need to force it:** archaeological sites, abandoned cities, museums — all of them are today's counterpart of the act the verse describes. And the verse does not command "take a lesson"; it asks **"have they not looked?"** That is, the act is already possible; what is not done is simply looking.

---

## 47/11 — ذَٰلِكَ بِأَنَّ ٱللَّهَ مَوْلَى ٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوا۟ وَأَنَّ ٱلْكَٰفِرِينَ لَا مَوْلَىٰ لَهُمْ

*Dhālika bi-anna'llāha mawla'lladhīna āmanū wa anna'l-kāfirīna lā mawlā lahum*

"That is because God is the **mawlā** of those who believe; and the deniers have no mawlā."

### The third sentence of ground — and this time positive

**The third occurrence of the sūra's *dhālika bi-anna* pattern.** And this is the most striking use of the pattern.

The two previous grounds showed **something the addressee had done**: they followed falsehood (3), they disliked (9). Here the ground is not something the addressee has done; it is **who God is.**

**That is, the sentence finds the cause of a destruction not in a human act but in the presence or absence of a relationship.**

### مَوْلَى — the root و-ل-ي and the breadth of the word

**Root: و-ل-ي.** And this root possesses one of the widest families of meaning in Arabic. Its primary sense: **nearness, adjacency, standing side by side with nothing else in between.** *Waliya'sh-shayʾ* — he adjoined that thing, he came right after it.

**The derivatives that come out of this core:**

| Word | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **وَلِيّ** (*walī*) | The one who is near; friend; protector; the one who takes charge of a matter |
| **وِلَايَة** (*walāya*) | The authority of taking charge of a matter |
| **مَوْلَى** (*mawlā*) | Taken up separately below |
| **تَوَلَّى** (*tawallā*) | To take charge of a matter; **and also: to turn away and go** |
| **أَوْلَى** (*awlā*) | Nearer, more deserving |

**The two opposite senses of the verb تَوَلَّى are interesting, and both will occur in this sūra:** in verse 22 *in tawallaytum*, in verse 38 *wa in tatawallaw*. **How the word carries the senses of "to turn towards" and "to turn away" together I shall take up at verse 22.**

### The two-sidedness of the word مَوْلَى

**And the word *mawlā* is one of the most discussed words in Arabic; because it stands close to the class of *aḍdād* (words that carry opposite meanings together).**

*Mawlā* is used for all of the following:

- **The master** — the one who frees his slave.
- **The freedman** — the one who is freed.
- **A helper, a friend, a supporter.**
- **A cousin, a kinsman.**
- **The one who takes charge of a matter, the one responsible.**

**That is, the word can name *both parties* to a relationship.** The common core is the relationship itself: a bond established between two people with no one in between.

**In this verse the word is in the sense of "helper, the one who takes a person's part, the one who takes charge"** and the context determines this: over against it stands *lā mawlā lahum*.

### The word order of the sentence: the two sides built differently

**The two clauses are not symmetrical, and the difference is meaningful.**

**The first side:** **ٱللَّهَ مَوْلَى ٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوا۟**
The structure: **noun + construct.** "God is the *mawlā* of those who believe."

**The second side:** **ٱلْكَٰفِرِينَ لَا مَوْلَىٰ لَهُمْ**
The structure: **لَا + indefinite noun + adverbial.** "As for the deniers: there is no *mawlā* at all for them."

**And the لَا here is called in grammar *lā al-nāfiya li'l-jins* (the *lā* that negates the genus).** This particle announces that **not one individual** of a thing is present — not "there is not one" but "there is none of the kind".

**That is, the sentence does not say:** "the *mawlā* of the deniers is not God".
**It says:** "the deniers have **no** *mawlā* at all."

**The difference is large.** The first sentence would have rejected a relationship; the second says that the position is **empty**.

### The link with the preceding verse

In the tenth verse a destruction was recounted: *dammara'llāhu ʿalayhim.* And the eleventh verse gives the cause of that destruction.

**The cause is not a crime but a solitude.**

This joins with the following verse as well: in verse 13 it will be said *fa-lā nāṣira lahum* — "and they had no helper". **The same pattern, two verses apart, with two different words:**

| Verse | Expression | Word |
|---|---|---|
| **11** | *lā **mawlā** lahum* | و-ل-ي — the one who takes a person's part |
| **13** | *fa-lā **nāṣira** lahum* | ن-ص-ر — the one who helps |

**Both are built with the *lā al-nāfiya li'l-jins*.** The sūra marks the same emptiness twice, with two different words.

**And this connects directly with the central question of the section `059-hasr.md`:** that sūra asked from beginning to end "what are you relying on?" and counted off the collapsing securities one by one. These two verses ask the same question from the other side: **who is behind you?**

---

## 47/12 — إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ يُدْخِلُ ٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوا۟ وَعَمِلُوا۟ ٱلصَّٰلِحَٰتِ جَنَّٰتٍ تَجْرِى مِن تَحْتِهَا ٱلْأَنْهَٰرُ وَٱلَّذِينَ كَفَرُوا۟ يَتَمَتَّعُونَ وَيَأْكُلُونَ كَمَا تَأْكُلُ ٱلْأَنْعَٰمُ وَٱلنَّارُ مَثْوًى لَّهُمْ

*Inna'llāha yudkhilu'lladhīna āmanū wa ʿamilu'ṣ-ṣāliḥāti jannātin tajrī min taḥtiha'l-anhār; wa'lladhīna kafarū yatamattaʿūna wa yaʾkulūna kamā taʾkulu'l-anʿāmu wa'n-nāru mathwan lahum*

"God will surely bring those who believe and do righteous deeds into gardens beneath which rivers flow. As for those who deny, they take their enjoyment and they eat as the grazing animals eat; and the fire is their **lodging**."

---

**The comparison in the middle of this verse is a place that has to be treated in careful and unwounding language. Let us set up the structure of the verse first; I shall come to the comparison after.**

### Word order: two tenses, two groups

**The most striking feature of the verse is that in speaking about two groups it uses *two different planes of time*.**

| | **The believers** | **Those who deny** |
|---|---|---|
| Verb | **يُدْخِلُ** — will bring in | **يَتَمَتَّعُونَ وَيَأْكُلُونَ** — they take enjoyment, they eat |
| Time | **Future** | **Now** |
| Place | **جَنَّٰتٍ** — gardens | **ٱلنَّارُ** — the fire (a nominal clause) |
| Type of clause | Verbal sentence | Verbal sentence + **nominal sentence** |

**That is, the verse does not situate the two groups in the same time.**

The place where the believers are has not yet come; the verb is imperfect and in the sense of the future. What those who deny do is happening **right now**: they eat, they take their enjoyment.

**And then the sentence closes with a nominal clause: وَٱلنَّارُ مَثْوًى لَّهُمْ.** In Arabic the nominal sentence announces **fixity** (*thubūt*, permanence); the verbal sentence announces **coming-to-be** (*ḥudūth*, renewal).

**The result:** their present state is given with a verbal sentence (temporary, flowing), their place of arrival with a nominal one (fixed).

### يَتَمَتَّعُونَ — the root م-ت-ع

**Root: م-ت-ع.** Its primary sense: **to derive benefit from something, to enjoy**; and the concrete sense the lexicographers record: **a thing's stretching out and continuing** — *mataʿa'n-nahār* — the day grew long.

From the same root **مَتَاع** (*matāʿ*) — the thing enjoyed, a means of living, goods. And an expression the Qurʾān uses often: **مَتَاعُ ٱلْغُرُور** — the enjoyment of delusion (Āl ʿImrān 3/185, al-Ḥadīd 57/20; the latter treated in `057-hadid.md`).

**And at the centre of the word lies an idea of *duration*:** *matāʿ* is an enjoyment that has an end. The Qurʾān uses the expression *matāʿun ilā ḥīn* (enjoyment until a term) in several places.

**تَمَتَّعَ** — Form V (*tafaʿʿul*). This form announces **intent and taking pains** (*takalluf*): doing a thing willingly, applying oneself to it. That is, *tamattuʿ* is not ordinary enjoyment; it is **turning towards enjoyment**.

### The work of the comparison: كَمَا تَأْكُلُ ٱلْأَنْعَٰمُ

**I have to be careful here, because this comparison can easily be turned into a sentence of contempt — and that is not what the verse does.**

**First I have to write this: the verse does not condemn the animal's eating.**

This can be seen from the text itself. The Qurʾān counts the *anʿām* (that is, the grazing livestock) elsewhere as a **favour**, and there is a sūra named for them (al-Anʿām, 6). At al-Naḥl 16/5-8 the creation of these animals is recounted as a bounty, together with the warmth, food and transport they provide for people.

**That is, the verse does not say "eating like an animal is bad".** An animal's eating is exactly the right thing for an animal.

**What, then, does the verse say?**

### أَنْعَام — and the root of the word

**Root: ن-ع-م.** And there is a subtlety here in the choice of the word.

**This root has been treated in many places in this commentary** (`056-vakia.md`, `102-tekasur.md`, `093-duha.md`, `088-gasiye.md`, `080-abese.md`, `070-mearic.md` and others). The family of meanings in the root:

- **نِعْمَة** (*niʿma*) — a gift, a kindness.
- **نَعِيم** (*naʿīm*) — a state of abundance.
- **نَاعِم** (*nāʿim*) — soft, fine. *Nuʿūma* — softness.
- **أَنْعَام** (*anʿām*) — grazing animals.

**The lexicographers explain the *anʿām* taking this name in two ways:** either because they are a **favour**, or because of the **softness** of their gait. **Both explanations are transmitted; I make no preference.**

**And this detail is worth noting:** in the same verse the place where the believers will go is named **جَنَّات** (gardens); and the frequent epithet of the garden in the Qurʾān is **نَعِيم** (*jannātu'n-naʿīm*). That is, the same root carries the animal on one side of the verse and the name of the garden on the other.

**I record this as a lexical observation; I do not claim that these two uses have been deliberately set side by side in this verse.**

### The real subject of the comparison

**Now let us come to what the comparison does.**

The clause is this: **يَأْكُلُونَ كَمَا تَأْكُلُ ٱلْأَنْعَٰمُ** — "they eat as the grazing animals eat".

**The particle of comparison كَمَا attaches to the verb, not to the agent.** That is, the sentence does not say "they are like animals"; it says **"their eating is like the animal's eating"**.

**This difference is everything.**

What does an animal's eating comprise? Hunger, searching, satiety. And when it is over it is over. **There is nothing missing in the animal's eating** — it is doing exactly what is expected of it.

**What the verse records is that a person's eating stays *at just that much*.**

A person eats too; the verse does not forbid this and does not condemn it. The Qurʾān makes eating a subject in its own right and commands eating what is lawful and good (al-Baqara 2/168). **The matter is not eating; it is *whether there is anything beyond* the eating.**

And the verse says this by way of a subtraction: in the sentence nothing is added to the person's eating. Not thanks, not thought, not the question of where it came from, not with whom it is shared. There is only the act.

**That is, the comparison is not a contempt but a *measurement*:** the space a person's life occupies is measured, and the result comes out the same as the space an animal's life occupies.

**And the verse says explicitly to whom this measurement is to be applied: *alladhīna kafarū*.** Per the method (`STYLE.md`): this is not an identity but an **attribute**. Whoever carries that attribute is included in it — and the attribute is "being content with just this much".

**I rest this reading on the wording of the text** (*kamā taʾkulu* — the particle of comparison attached to the verb); the "measurement" interpretation I draw from it is my own inference.

### The exegesis of the Qurʾān by the Qurʾān

The Qurʾān sets up this comparison in one other place, at greater length — and there it writes out explicitly what the measure is:

> *"We have created for hell many of the jinn and of mankind: **they have hearts with which they do not understand, eyes with which they do not see, ears with which they do not hear.** Those are like the grazing animals; indeed they are further astray. Those are the heedless ones."* (al-Aʿrāf 7/179)

**Note: the verse in al-Aʿrāf counts three organs before setting up the comparison** — heart, eye, ear. That is, the measure of the comparison is not the absence of the organs but their **not being used**.

**And al-Aʿrāf enters one further qualification: أُو۟لَٰٓئِكَ كَٱلْأَنْعَٰمِ بَلْ هُمْ أَضَلُّ** — "indeed further astray". This addition matters: the animal is not "astray" because it lives **according to its own measure**. The straying arises from not using what has been given.

**That is, when the two verses are read together the direction of the comparison becomes clear: the comparison is not against the animal but in favour of the capacity given.**

### مَثْوًى — the root م-ث-و

**Root: م-ث-و.** Its meaning: **to stay in a place, to reside, to lodge.** *Mathawā bi'l-makān* — he stayed there.

**مَثْوًى** — a noun of place: **the place where one stays, a lodging, a dwelling.**

**And there is something in the word:** *mathwā* can also carry the sense of a temporary lodging place (a traveller's lodging), but in the Qurʾān it is generally used in the sense of **the final place of arrival**.

**The word occurs twice in this sūra:**

| Verse | Expression | To whom |
|---|---|---|
| **12** | *wa'n-nāru **mathwan** lahum* | To those who deny |
| **19** | *wa'llāhu yaʿlamu mutaqallabakum wa **mathwākum*** | **To the one addressed — that is, to all of us** |

**And this is a turn worth recording.** In the twelfth verse *mathwā* is the place of arrival of a group. In the nineteenth verse the same word comes back as one of the two states of the addressee's own life: *mutaqallab* (where you move about) and *mathwā* (where you come to rest).

**The sūra turns a word it used about a group back upon the one it addresses, seven verses later.** This is an example of the sūra's general method; we shall see the same movement in the word *aḍghān* (rancours) in verses 29 and 37.

**I record this observation as textual data;** the two occurrences of the word are fixed, this reading of a "turning back" is mine.

---

## 47/13 — وَكَأَيِّن مِّن قَرْيَةٍ هِىَ أَشَدُّ قُوَّةً مِّن قَرْيَتِكَ ٱلَّتِىٓ أَخْرَجَتْكَ أَهْلَكْنَٰهُمْ فَلَا نَاصِرَ لَهُمْ

*Wa ka-ayyin min qaryatin hiya ashaddu quwwatan min qaryatika'llatī akhrajatka; ahlaknāhum fa-lā nāṣira lahum*

"And how many a town stronger than your town which drove you out — We destroyed them, and they had no helper."

### كَأَيِّن — a construction

**كَأَيِّن** — an expression in Arabic conveying **many or few** (*taqlīl* / *takthīr*): "how many, how very many". It does the same work as *kam*.

Structurally it is said to be formed from the combination of **كَ** (the *kāf* of comparison) + **أَىّ** (which) + nunation, and its spelling preserves that combination.

**Then مِّن comes and brings a specification (*tamyīz*):** *min qaryatin* — "of the kind town". This is the standard structure that follows expressions of number in Arabic.

### قَرْيَة — and the word chosen

**Root: ق-ر-ي.** Its primary sense: **to gather, to bring together.** *Qarā'l-māʾa fi'l-ḥawḍ* — he gathered the water in the basin. From the same root **قِرَى** (*qirā*) — hospitality to a guest, the entertaining of a visitor.

**That is, *qarya* is not "village"; it is *the place where people gather*.** The usual English rendering "village" carries a suggestion of a small settlement, and that suggestion is misleading: the Qurʾān uses the word *qarya* for large cities too.

**And the work of the word in this verse is that it brings forward not the people but *the place*.** The verse does not say "how many a community stronger than you"; it says "how many a **town** stronger than your town".

### قَرْيَتِكَ ٱلَّتِىٓ أَخْرَجَتْكَ — two details

**The first detail: "your town".**

The Prophet is being addressed and the town is still **attributed to him**: *qaryatika* — your town. That is, his having been driven out has not ended the belonging.

**This is a fact standing in the choice of words.** It could have been said "that town" or "the town that drove you out"; it says "your town".

**The second detail: the subject of the verb is the town.**

**أَخْرَجَتْكَ** — "it drove you out". The subject of the verb is **قَرْيَة** — the town itself. Not the people of the town, but the town.

**The grammarians call this *majāz ʿaqlī*:** the attribution of a verb not to its real agent but to the place where the act occurred or to its cause. Like "the road wore me out", "the city stifled me".

**And this choice adds a weight to the sentence** — I record it as an observation: had it been said "its people drove you out", particular persons would have been blamed. When it is said "the town drove you out", what does the driving out is not a person but **the whole of a place**.

### Historical background — and its limit

**It is transmitted in the classical sources that this verse came down during the emigration, on the road out of Mecca.** The report is widespread and agrees with the wording of the verse.

**I do not present this with a claim of certainty.** The wording of the verse gives no time; *akhrajatka* (it drove you out) is a perfect verb and shows that the departure has taken place, but it does not say when.

**And this qualification is required per the method (`STYLE.md`):** the expression "the town that drove you out" is not a blanket ruling about a town or a community. The ruling the verse gives is not even about that town — the sentence says that **other towns** were destroyed, and makes a comparison.

Indeed the Qurʾān speaks about the same town in an entirely different language elsewhere: Sūrat al-Balad opens *"I swear by this town"* (90/1), and the word order of that oath was treated in `090-beled.md`. The same place is named in two different sūras with two different faces.

### أَهْلَكْنَٰهُمْ — and the shift in the pronoun

**There is a striking movement of language:** while the sentence is speaking about a *qarya* (feminine, singular), the verb **أَهْلَكْنَٰهُمْ** — "We destroyed them" — comes with a **masculine plural** pronoun.

**In grammar this is called *ḥaml ʿala'l-maʿnā*:** agreement according to the meaning intended rather than the wording. *Qarya* is verbally feminine singular, but what is meant is **its people**; and so the pronoun refers to them.

**And this shift does a piece of work:** had it been said "We destroyed the town", the demolition of a building would have been understood. It says "We destroyed them".

**There is a change of person too:** at the head of the sentence the speaker was in the third person (*akhrajatka*); with *ahlaknā* it moves to the first person plural (the *nūn* of majesty). That is, the act of destruction is spoken as something directly taken upon oneself.

### فَلَا نَاصِرَ لَهُمْ — the second emptiness

**A repetition of the pattern in the eleventh verse.**

| Verse | Expression |
|---|---|
| **11** | *wa anna'l-kāfirīna **lā mawlā lahum*** |
| **13** | *ahlaknāhum **fa-lā nāṣira lahum*** |

**Both are built with the *lā al-nāfiya li'l-jins*:** not one of the kind.

**And the difference is in the words:** *mawlā*, the one who takes a person's part; *nāṣir*, the one who helps. One is **belonging**, the other **support**. The sūra marks two separate emptinesses with two separate words.

**And the distribution of the root ن-ص-ر in the sūra is as follows:**

| Verse | Expression | Direction |
|---|---|---|
| **4** | *la'ntaṣara minhum* | God could have taken His own due |
| **7** | *in tanṣuru'llāha **yanṣurkum*** | Mutual help |
| **13** | *fa-lā **nāṣira** lahum* | No helper at all |

**Three occurrences, three situations: where help is not needed, where it is mutual, where there is none at all.**

---

## 47/14 — أَفَمَن كَانَ عَلَىٰ بَيِّنَةٍ مِّن رَّبِّهِۦ كَمَن زُيِّنَ لَهُۥ سُوٓءُ عَمَلِهِۦ وَٱتَّبَعُوٓا۟ أَهْوَآءَهُم

*A-fa-man kāna ʿalā bayyinatin min rabbihī ka-man zuyyina lahu sūʾu ʿamalihī wa'ttabaʿū ahwāʾahum*

"Is one who stands upon a clear proof from his Lord like those to whom their evil deed has been made to look fair and who have followed their own desires?"

### The pattern of the question: أَفَمَن … كَمَن

**A pattern of comparison the Qurʾān uses often:** *a-fa-man… ka-man* — "is one who is…, like one who is…?"

**The question is one of denial:** it expects no answer, it announces a negation. "Of course they are not the same."

**And the work of the pattern is to make the addressee say the answer.** No ruling is issued; the addressee is asked to draw the ruling himself.

### عَلَىٰ بَيِّنَةٍ — "upon a proof"

**بَيِّنَة** — root **ب-ي-ن**: to separate, to come out into the open, for a gap to open between two things. From the same root *bayān* (explanation), *tabyīn*, *mubīn* (manifest), *bayn* (the space between).

**At the centre of the root lies *separation*:** a thing becomes visible when it is separated from other things.

**بَيِّنَة** is an intensive on the *faʿīla* pattern: **that which is utterly clear, the proof that distinguishes.**

**And the particle matters: عَلَىٰ.** "**Upon** a proof". In Arabic *ʿalā* announces standing on top of something, treading on it. That is, the proof is not a piece of knowledge the person possesses; it is **a ground he stands on**.

**This gains its meaning when compared with the description of the other side:** there is no ground beneath them; something has been **made to look fair** to them.

### زُيِّنَ لَهُۥ — the passive and the non-naming of the agent

**زَيَّنَ** — root **ز-ي-ن**, Form *tafʿīl*: to adorn, to make look fair.

**This root was treated in `057-hadid.md` (57/20 — *zīna*).** What was recorded there is decisive here:

> "The Qurʾān uses this root in an interesting way: mostly in the sense of a thing's *'being made to look fair'*. […] **The adornment is not the thing itself; it is what is placed upon the thing.** And what is adorned looks as good as its adornment."

**What is to be added here is that the verb is *passive*: زُيِّنَ — "it was made to look fair".**

**Who made it so? The verse does not say.**

And this is the Qurʾān's habit with this construction: the same verb is used in some places of Satan (al-Anʿām 6/43, al-Naml 27/24), in some places left passive (this verse, Fāṭir 35/8); and in one place God's name occurs as the agent (at al-Anʿām 6/108 the making fair of deeds is named as a custom).

**This distribution enters into the classical debates of theology, and I do not enter that debate here.** What is to be recorded is this: **the verse chooses not to name the agent, and this is a choice.**

**An observation — it is my own reading:** the passive has an effect. When the agent is not named, the person **cannot see** the one who adorns. And to notice that something has been made to look fair requires noticing the one who made it so. That is, the verb form itself carries the mechanism of the deception.

### سُوٓءُ عَمَلِهِۦ — and the key word of the sūra

**One of the occurrences of the root عَمَل in the sūra is here** — and this is the only place where the root is attributed not to the collective deeds of the believers or of those who deny but to **the deed of a single person**: *ʿamalihī* — his deed.

**سُوء** — root **س-و-أ**: to be bad, to become ugly. From the same root *sayyiʾa* (an evil deed — it occurred at 47/2: *kaffara ʿanhum **sayyiʾātihim***), *aswaʾ*, *sūʾ*.

**And a link appears between two verses:**

| Verse | Expression | What happens |
|---|---|---|
| **2** | *kaffara ʿanhum **sayyiʾātihim*** | Their evil deeds are **covered** |
| **14** | *zuyyina lahu **sūʾu ʿamalihī*** | His evil deed is **adorned** |

**Both verbs cover something over.** But one makes it **invisible** (*takfīr*), the other makes it **look good** (*tazyīn*).

**The difference is this — it is my own reading:** what is covered is still bad for the one who knows it has been covered; what is adorned is done again, because it is not known to be bad.

### وَٱتَّبَعُوٓا۟ أَهْوَآءَهُم — the shift from singular to plural

**There is a marked slippage in the word order:**

- **مَن زُيِّنَ لَهُۥ** — singular ("the **one** to whom it has been made to look fair"), and the pronouns singular: *lahu*, *ʿamalihī*.
- **وَٱتَّبَعُوٓا۟ أَهْوَآءَهُم** — **plural** ("they followed their desires").

**In grammar this is again called *ḥaml ʿala'l-maʿnā*:** *man* (who) is verbally singular but plural in meaning; at the head of the sentence the wording is followed, at the end the meaning.

**And this slippage does a piece of work** — I record it as an observation: the deception is recounted in the singular (each person is deceived alone), the following is recounted in the plural (following desire takes on a collective form).

**I do not present this as a settled rhetorical explanation;** *ḥaml ʿala'l-maʿnā* is a usage frequently seen in the Qurʾān and in most places it carries no particular meaning.

### هَوَى — the root ه-و-ي

**Root: ه-و-ي.** **This root has been treated in this commentary** (`053-necm.md` — 53/1, *wa'n-najmi idhā **hawā***; `101-karia.md`, `079-naziat.md`, `054-kamer.md`). **I do not repeat it here.**

The main point recorded there is decisive here: the primary sense of the root is **to fall from above downward**. *Hawā* — it fell. **هَاوِيَة** — an abyss, a bottomless pit (al-Qāriʿa 101/9).

**And from the same root هَوًى (*hawā*) — the appetite of the soul, its inclination.**

**The connection the lexicographers make is this:** *hawā* is the inclination that pulls a person **downward**. The word carries in its name the direction it goes in.

**And the word occurs twice in this sūra:** 14 (*wa'ttabaʿū ahwāʾahum*) and 16 (*wa'ttabaʿū ahwāʾahum*). **The same expression, the same words, two verses apart.**

| Verse | Who | Expression |
|---|---|---|
| **14** | Those whose deed has been made to look fair | *wa'ttabaʿū ahwāʾahum* |
| **16** | Those who listen and take nothing from it | *wa'ttabaʿū ahwāʾahum* |

**This repetition binds the two groups together** and is to be kept in mind in passing to the sixteenth verse.

**And a reminder: in the third verse the verb *ittabaʿū* occurred twice** — once for those who follow falsehood, once for those who follow the truth. That is, the root **ت-ب-ع** occurs four times in the sūra and in all four the same form (Form VIII) is used: 3 (twice), 14, 16. And once more at 28: *ittabaʿū mā askhaṭa'llāh*.

**Five occurrences, one verb.** This is the sūra's way of describing the human being: **a human being is a creature that sets off after something.** The difference lies in what he sets off after.

**I record this finding as my own reading.**

---

## 47/15 — مَّثَلُ ٱلْجَنَّةِ ٱلَّتِى وُعِدَ ٱلْمُتَّقُونَ فِيهَآ أَنْهَٰرٌ مِّن مَّآءٍ غَيْرِ ءَاسِنٍ وَأَنْهَٰرٌ مِّن لَّبَنٍ لَّمْ يَتَغَيَّرْ طَعْمُهُۥ وَأَنْهَٰرٌ مِّنْ خَمْرٍ لَّذَّةٍ لِّلشَّٰرِبِينَ وَأَنْهَٰرٌ مِّنْ عَسَلٍ مُّصَفًّى وَلَهُمْ فِيهَا مِن كُلِّ ٱلثَّمَرَٰتِ وَمَغْفِرَةٌ مِّن رَّبِّهِمْ كَمَنْ هُوَ خَٰلِدٌ فِى ٱلنَّارِ وَسُقُوا۟ مَآءً حَمِيمًا فَقَطَّعَ أَمْعَآءَهُمْ

*Mathalu'l-jannati'llatī wuʿida'l-muttaqūn; fīhā anhārun min māʾin ghayri āsin, wa anhārun min labanin lam yataghayyar ṭaʿmuh, wa anhārun min khamrin ladhdhatin li'sh-shāribīn, wa anhārun min ʿasalin muṣaffā; wa lahum fīhā min kulli'th-thamarāti wa maghfiratun min rabbihim — ka-man huwa khālidun fi'n-nāri wa suqū māʾan ḥamīman fa-qaṭṭaʿa amʿāʾahum*

"The likeness of the garden promised to those who guard themselves is this: in it are rivers of **water that does not go stale**, rivers of **milk whose taste does not change**, rivers of **wine, a delight to those who drink**, and rivers of **strained honey**. There they have every kind of fruit, and a forgiveness from their Lord. [Is such a one] like one who abides for ever in the fire and is given **boiling water** to drink, so that it tears his bowels apart?"

---

**A note on method, at the very start:** the debate over whether the descriptions of the garden are **literal or figurative** was treated in full in the section `056-vakia.md` — including the question of the addressee, the table of the two views, and the making of no preference. In the same section the word **حَمِيم** was also analysed (together with its references to `069-hakka.md` and `070-mearic.md`).

**I do not repeat that debate here and I rely on that treatment.** What is to be done here is to take up what is **particular to this verse**: the order among the four rivers, and the opposition at the end of the verse.

### The parsing of the sentence: a comparison left incomplete

**The verse is disputed grammatically, and the reason for the dispute must be seen.**

The sentence runs: **مَّثَلُ ٱلْجَنَّةِ … كَمَنْ هُوَ خَٰلِدٌ فِى ٱلنَّارِ.**

Word for word: "The likeness of the garden… is it like one who abides for ever in the fire?"

**As it stands this is inconsistent:** a *likeness* cannot be compared to a *person*. The two sides of the comparison are not of the same kind.

**The grammarians and the exegetes resolve this in several ways:**

| # | Resolution | Implied form |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | There is an **ellipsis**, and the interrogative hamza of the preceding verse is carried over here | *a-**man huwa fī hādhihi'l-jannati** ka-man huwa khālidun fi'n-nār* — "Is one who is in this garden like one who abides for ever in the fire?" |
| **2** | The word *mathal* means "description, condition"; it is the subject, and its predicate is elided | *mathalu'l-jannati… **kamā wuṣifa*** |
| **3** | The *kāf* in *ka-man* is counted redundant | The comparison is made directly |

**The first resolution is the most widely transmitted**, and there is a reason for it: **the preceding verse (14) was built with exactly this pattern** — *a-fa-man kāna ʿalā bayyinatin min rabbihī **ka-man** zuyyina lahu sūʾu ʿamalihī.*

**That is, verse 14 set up the *a-fa-man… ka-man* pattern; verse 15 repeats the same pattern and lengthens its first half by inserting the description of the garden.**

**This shows that the two verses are a pair:**

| | **47/14** | **47/15** |
|---|---|---|
| The first side | *man kāna ʿalā bayyinatin min rabbihī* | *[man huwa fī] jannati… wa maghfiratun **min rabbihim*** |
| The comparison | **كَمَن** | **كَمَنْ** |
| The second side | *zuyyina lahu sūʾu ʿamalihī* | *huwa khālidun fi'n-nār* |

**And note: the first side of both verses ends with the construct مِّن رَّبِّهِ.** In 14, *bayyinatin **min rabbihī***; in 15, *maghfiratun **min rabbihim***.

**I record this repetition as an observation about word order.** The two verses are sealed with the same construct.

**And a reminder: the same construct was present in verses 2 and 3 of the sūra as well** (*wa huwa'l-ḥaqqu **min rabbihim***; *ittabaʿu'l-ḥaqqa **min rabbihim***). That is, the sūra uses this construct four times: 2, 3, 14, 15.

### The four rivers: how the description is built

**Four rivers are counted, and all four come in the same pattern: أَنْهَٰرٌ مِّن + [drink] + [quality].**

| # | Drink | Root | Quality | The form of the quality |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **1** | **مَّآء** — water | م-و-ه | **غَيْرِ ءَاسِنٍ** — not gone stale | **Negation** (*ghayr*) |
| **2** | **لَّبَن** — milk | ل-ب-ن | **لَّمْ يَتَغَيَّرْ طَعْمُهُۥ** — its taste unchanged | **Negation** (*lam*) |
| **3** | **خَمْر** — wine | خ-م-ر | **لَّذَّةٍ لِّلشَّٰرِبِينَ** — a delight to those who drink | **Affirmation** |
| **4** | **عَسَل** — honey | ع-س-ل | **مُّصَفًّى** — strained | **Affirmation** (but one announcing purification) |

**And what the table shows is this: all four qualities remove that drink's *defect* in this world.**

- **Water** goes foul when it stands still; its colour and taste spoil. → This water does not go stale.
- **Milk** turns sour when it is kept. → The taste of this milk does not change.
- **Wine** in this world means drunkenness and a headache. → This is **delight only**.
- **Honey**, when it comes out of the comb, is mixed with wax, bee residue and sediment; it has to be strained. → This honey is already strained.

**That is, the description is not an enrichment resting on imagination; it is the removal of four known defects from four known things.**

**And this method was recorded in the section `056-vakia.md` as well** — there 56/32-33 (*lā maqṭūʿatin wa lā mamnūʿa* — fruit neither cut off nor forbidden) and 56/19 (*lā yuṣaddaʿūna ʿanhā wa lā yunzifūn*) were analysed in the same way: **the description works by removing subtractions.**

**The observation to be added here is this — and it is my own reading:** this method is perhaps the only honest way of recounting something the addressee does not know. The picture of an unknown thing cannot be drawn; but the **defect** of a known thing can be removed. The text does not describe a new substance to the addressee; it takes a known defect out of a known substance.

**How far this method is literal and how far figurative was — as recorded above — debated in `056-vakia.md`, and there too no preference was made.**

### ءَاسِن — the root أ-س-ن

**Root: أ-س-ن.** And this word occurs only here in the Qurʾān.

The sense the lexicographers record: **water's standing for a long time and its colour, taste or smell spoiling.** *Asina'l-māʾ* / *asana'l-māʾ* — the water spoiled, went foul.

**Two readings are transmitted:** **ءَاسِن** (*āsin* — an active participle, on the *fāʿil* pattern) and **أَسِن** (*asin* — an adjective on the *faʿil* pattern). There is no difference of meaning; I content myself with recording it.

**And the concrete picture of the root matters:** water spoils **because it does not flow**. The cause of the spoiling is not contamination but **stillness**.

**This makes the word's place in this sentence meaningful.** The sentence says **أَنْهَٰر** (rivers) — that is, it is already speaking of flowing water. And to flowing water it adds the quality "not gone stale".

**An observation — it is my own reading:** in this world flowing water can spoil too; a river stops, becomes a pond, goes foul. The quality guarantees the **continuity** of the flow.

**I present this as an inference; the verse does not speak of the continuity of the flow, it speaks of the absence of spoiling.**

### لَّمْ يَتَغَيَّرْ طَعْمُهُۥ — the quality of the milk

**The root غ-ي-ر:** to alter, to change. **تَغَيَّرَ** — Form V: to change of itself, to pass into another state.

**And the quality is placed on the *taste* of the milk: طَعْمُهُ.**

This is a choice. When milk spoils many things about it change: its colour, its smell, its consistency. The verse selects **taste** from among these — that is, the property the drinker meets directly.

**And the distribution of the qualities of the four rivers across the senses is worth noting:**

| River | Which sense the quality looks to |
|---|---|
| Water — *ghayri āsin* | Smell and taste (the marks of spoiling) |
| Milk — *lam yataghayyar **ṭaʿmuh*** | **Taste** |
| Wine — *ladhdhatin li'sh-shāribīn* | **Taste / pleasure** |
| Honey — *muṣaffā* | **Appearance and texture** (the straining is seen) |

**All four are qualities measured by the senses.** None of them is abstract.

### خَمْر لَّذَّة — a word and a note

**خَمْر** — root **خ-م-ر**: **to cover, to conceal.** From the same root **خِمَار** (*khimār*) — a covering. And wine bears this name because it **covers the mind.**

**That is, the word itself carries in its name the defect of wine in this world.**

**And after using this word the verse adds a quality to it: لَّذَّةٍ لِّلشَّٰرِبِينَ.**

**لَذَّة** is a **verbal noun** ("delight"), not an adjective. To bring a verbal noun in the position of an adjective announces **intensification** in Arabic: not "giving delight" but **"delight itself"**.

**And the qualification *li'sh-shāribīn* must not be passed over either: "for those who drink".** That is, the delight is described not as something in the drink itself but as something that is **for the one who drinks**.

**A note on method is required here.** This verse uses in a description of the garden the name of a drink forbidden in this world, and this is not a new question; the Qurʾān itself makes the distinction explicitly elsewhere:

> *"…from which they get neither headache nor loss of their wits"* (al-Wāqiʿa 56/19 — treated in `056-vakia.md`).
>
> *"White, a delight to those who drink… in it there is no stupefying, nor do they become drunk on it"* (al-Ṣāffāt 37/46-47).

**That is, in using the word the Qurʾān removes the word's this-worldly defects separately each time.** What is shared is the name; what is not shared is the effect the name points to.

**No legal conclusion is drawn from this in this commentary, and nothing is said about the ruling in this world.**

### عَسَل مُّصَفًّى — strained honey

**مُّصَفًّى** — root **ص-ف-و**, the passive participle of Form *tafʿīl*: **strained, purified, separated from its sediment.**

From the same root: **صَفَا** — it became clear; **صَفْوَة** — the essence, the choicest part of a thing; **ٱصْطَفَى** — he chose, he sifted out (in the Qurʾān it is used of the choosing of the prophets); and **ٱلصَّفَا** — the hill of al-Ṣafā.

**And the lexicographers record the word *ṣafwān* too: a smooth, polished stone.** In `002-bakara.md` this word was treated in the exegesis of 2/264 (the slippery rock with a fine layer of soil on it).

**The work of the word in this verse is concrete:** in this world honey is not pure as it comes out of the comb. Straining is a work done by a person. The verse says that the work **has been done**.

**An observation — it is my own reading:** three of the four qualities are the **absence of a spoiling** (water, milk, wine); the fourth is **a process having been carried out** (honey). That is, in three of them a subtraction is removed, and in the fourth something is presented as already worked upon.

### مِن كُلِّ ٱلثَّمَرَٰتِ وَمَغْفِرَةٌ — and the one abstract thing added to the list

**After the four rivers two more things are counted:**

1. **مِن كُلِّ ٱلثَّمَرَٰتِ** — of every kind of fruit.
2. **وَمَغْفِرَةٌ مِّن رَّبِّهِمْ** — and a forgiveness from their Lord.

**And this second is the only abstract item on the list.**

Everything counted is drunk or eaten: water, milk, wine, honey, fruit. And the last is **forgiveness** — which is neither drunk nor eaten.

**This is worth noting in respect of its being placed at the end of the list.** In the classical commentaries it is pointed out that this addition shifts the centre of gravity of the description: on top of all the favours counted, something of another kind is added.

**مَغْفِرَة** — root **غ-ف-ر**: **to cover, to cover over.** And as was recorded in `061-saff.md`, the concrete source of the root is the *mighfar* — the war helmet that covers the head:

> "That is, forgiveness is not the treating of a sin as non-existent but **the covering of it**."

**And here three roots stand side by side, all three having to do with "covering":**

| Word | Root | What it covers |
|---|---|---|
| **خَمْر** | خ-م-ر | It covers **the mind** |
| **مَغْفِرَة** | غ-ف-ر | It covers **sin** |
| *(and in verse 2)* **كَفَّرَ** | ك-ف-ر | It covers **sin** |

**Two roots of covering in the same verse, three in the same sūra.** I record this as a lexical observation; it carries no claim of deliberateness.

### كَمَنْ هُوَ خَٰلِدٌ فِى ٱلنَّارِ وَسُقُوا۟ مَآءً حَمِيمًا — the opposition

**And after the four rivers the verse passes to a single drink.**

**How the opposition is built needs to be seen in a table:**

| | **The side of the garden** | **The side of the fire** |
|---|---|---|
| Number | **Four** rivers + fruit + forgiveness | **One** drink |
| Word | **أَنْهَٰر** — rivers (plural) | **مَآء** — water (singular, indefinite) |
| Quality | Not gone stale, unchanged, delightful, strained | **حَمِيم** — boiling |
| Verb | *fīhā* — there is there (a nominal sentence) | **سُقُوا۟** — they were given to drink (**passive**) |
| Result | — | **فَقَطَّعَ أَمْعَآءَهُمْ** |

**And two details of word order sharpen this table:**

**One. On the side of the garden there is no verb, there is existence.** *Fīhā anhārun* — "in it are rivers". A nominal sentence; it announces fixity. No one is giving anything to anyone; it is there.

**Two. On the side of the fire there is a verb, and it is passive: سُقُوا۟ — "they were given to drink".** That is, they do not drink; **they are made to drink.** Will is not present in the sentence.

**I record this opposition as my own reading;** the structural difference between the two clauses is textual data, the "will" reading I draw from it is my own inference.

### حَمِيم — and a cross-reference within the commentary

**Root: ح-م-م.** **This root was analysed in full in `069-hakka.md` (69/35); it was recorded again in `070-mearic.md` and `056-vakia.md`.** **I do not repeat it here.**

The summary recorded there: the root has two branches of meaning — **boiling water** and **close friend** — and the lexicographers join them by way of "heat".

**The only thing to be added here is the *object* of the word in this verse.** The verse does not say *ḥamīm* alone; it says **مَآءً حَمِيمًا** — "boiling **water**".

**And this comes into direct confrontation with the first of the four rivers:**

| **47/15, the first river** | **47/15, the last drink** |
|---|---|
| **مَّآءٍ غَيْرِ ءَاسِنٍ** — water not gone stale | **مَآءً حَمِيمًا** — boiling water |

**The same word (*māʾ*), with two qualities, at the two ends of the same verse.**

**This is the sharpest opposition in the word order of the verse** and it is hard to think it accidental: the verse counts four drinks, and on the other side it places **the same substance as the first** of those four.

**I record this as an observation about word order.**

### فَقَطَّعَ أَمْعَآءَهُمْ

**قَطَّعَ** — root **ق-ط-ع**: to cut. And the form here is Form *tafʿīl*.

Form *tafʿīl*, besides transitivity, announces **multiplicity** (*takthīr*: abundance, repetition, extension). That is, *qaṭṭaʿa* does not mean "he cut"; it means **"he cut to pieces, he cut again and again"**.

**The difference lies in the pattern itself:** *qaṭaʿa* is a single cutting, *qaṭṭaʿa* a plural one.

**أَمْعَآء** — the plural of *miʿā*: **bowels.** The word occurs only here in the Qurʾān.

**And the work of the choice is this:** the verse does not speak of an outward punishment. What is spoken of is something that **goes inside**. The drink does its work where it has gone.

**And this sets up an exact symmetry with the first half of the verse** — it is my own reading:

The four rivers are also things to be drunk; that is, they too go inside. The verse counts, on both sides, things that **go inside**. The difference lies in what they do once they have gone in: one nourishes, one tears apart.

**I present this symmetry as a reading;** the verse does not build such a sentence of comparison, but that both sides are described by way of drinks stands in the text.

### A note: on the language of this description

**What was written on this subject in the section `056-vakia.md` holds here too and I do not repeat it.** It was taken up there under three headings: the question of the addressee (what water, shade and fruit meant to a community living in the desert), the debate between literal and figurative (in a table, with no preference made), and the scope of the address.

**I make only one addition here, and it is particular to this verse:** this verse places the word **مَّثَل** at the head of the description — *mathalu'l-jannati.*

**And there is disagreement over the meaning of the word *mathal*:**

| View | The meaning of *mathal* | Its consequence |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | **Description, condition, characterisation** | The description is a direct characterisation |
| **2** | **Representation, comparison** | The description is a comparison |

**I make no preference.** I record this much: **the root م-ث-ل occurs three times in this sūra** (3, 10, 38), and its occurrence in the third verse was explicitly in the sense of "giving a likeness": *kadhālika yaḍribu'llāhu li'n-nāsi amthālahum.*

**This appears to support the second view; but a root is not obliged to be used in the same sense throughout a sūra, and I make no preference.**

---

## 47/16 — وَمِنْهُم مَّن يَسْتَمِعُ إِلَيْكَ حَتَّىٰٓ إِذَا خَرَجُوا۟ مِنْ عِندِكَ قَالُوا۟ لِلَّذِينَ أُوتُوا۟ ٱلْعِلْمَ مَاذَا قَالَ ءَانِفًا أُو۟لَٰٓئِكَ ٱلَّذِينَ طَبَعَ ٱللَّهُ عَلَىٰ قُلُوبِهِمْ وَٱتَّبَعُوٓا۟ أَهْوَآءَهُمْ

*Wa minhum man yastamiʿu ilayka ḥattā idhā kharajū min ʿindika qālū li'lladhīna ūtu'l-ʿilma mādhā qāla ānifā; ulāʾika'lladhīna ṭabaʿa'llāhu ʿalā qulūbihim wa'ttabaʿū ahwāʾahum*

"And among them are those who listen to you; then, when they go out from your presence, they say to those who have been given knowledge: 'What did he say just now?' Those are the ones on whose hearts God has set a seal, and who have followed their own desires."

### The scene

**The verse sets up a scene, and the details of the scene matter:**

- There is a **place**: *min ʿindika* — "from your presence". That is, a gathering.
- There is an **act**: *yastamiʿu* — to listen.
- There is a **departure**: *kharajū* — they went out.
- There is a **question**: *mādhā qāla ānifā* — "what did he say just now?"
- And the question has an **addressee**: *alladhīna ūtu'l-ʿilma* — those who have been given knowledge.

**That is: two groups seated in the same gathering. Having heard the same voice. When the gathering ends one asks the other.**

### يَسْتَمِعُ — Form VIII and the deliberateness of listening

**And here is the most striking detail of the verse.**

**Root: س-م-ع** — to hear. But the form used in the verse is **يَسْتَمِعُ** — Form X (*istifʿāl*).

**The difference in Arabic between *samiʿa* and *istamaʿa* is one the lexicographers record particularly:**

| Verb | Form | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| **سَمِعَ** (*samiʿa*) | The bare form | **To hear** — the sound reaches the ear; no will is required |
| **ٱسْتَمَعَ** (*istamaʿa*) | *Istifʿāl* | **To listen** — to lend the ear, to turn deliberately |
| **أَنْصَتَ** (*anṣata*) | *Ifʿāl* | **To fall silent and listen** — to stop one's own voice and listen |

**The Qurʾān uses all three together and preserves the distinction:** *"When the Qurʾān is recited, **listen to it (fa'stamiʿū)** and **be silent (wa anṣitū)**"* (al-Aʿrāf 7/204).

**That is, the verb in this verse recounts not an absent-minded hearing but a deliberate listening.**

**And this carries the whole weight of the verse.**

Had the verb been *yasmaʿu*, the outcome would be intelligible: they were not paying attention, they missed it. But the verb is *yastamiʿu*. **They listened.** They lent their ears. They came and sat in the gathering.

**And when they went out they had nothing in their hands.**

**This is where the verse makes its diagnosis:** the emptiness does not come from inattention. The act was done, the outcome did not form.

### إِلَيْكَ — and a particle

**The verb is made transitive with the particle إِلَىٰ: *yastamiʿu ilayka*.**

In Arabic this verb can take either *ilā* or a direct object. When it comes with *ilā* the sense of **turning towards** is strengthened: "he lends his ear in your direction".

**And note: what is listened to is not the words but the person.** The verse does not say "they listen to what you say"; it says **"they listen to you"**.

**An observation — it is my own reading:** listening to a person and listening to what is said are different things. One who listens to the person is present at the gathering, turns his face, even shows interest. One who listens to the words follows what the words say. The scene of the verse shows that the first is possible without the second.

**I do not present this as a binding exegesis;** the use of the particle *ilā* is standard in Arabic and it is not necessary to draw a meaning from it.

### حَتَّىٰٓ إِذَا خَرَجُوا۟ — and the shift of person

**A detail of word order:** the sentence begins in the **singular** (*man yastamiʿu* — the one who listens) and moves to the **plural** (*kharajū*, *qālū*, *ahwāʾahum*).

**Again *ḥaml ʿala'l-maʿnā*:** *man* is verbally singular, plural in meaning. The same shift was present in the fourteenth verse.

**And the *ḥattā idhā* pattern:** the same pattern was present in the fourth verse (*ḥattā idhā athkhantumūhum*). This pattern marks the **threshold** of a process: something continues, and at a certain point something else happens.

**The threshold marked here is the door of the gathering.** Inside, the listening goes on; on stepping out of the door, the question comes out.

### مَاذَا قَالَ ءَانِفًا — the question

**ءَانِفًا** — root **أ-ن-ف**. And the concrete sense of the root: **the nose.**

**أَنْف** (*anf*) — nose. And the family of meanings that comes out of it:

| Word | Meaning | Connection |
|---|---|---|
| **أَنْف** | Nose | The **protruding** part of the face |
| **أَنْف الشَّىْء** | The **head, the front part** of a thing | *Anfu'l-jabal* — the nose of the mountain, its projection |
| **ٱسْتَأْنَفَ** | To begin again | To take hold of a thing by its **head** |
| **ءَانِف** | **What has just happened** | The **nearest** end of time |
| **أَنَفَة** | Self-regard, turning up the nose | Holding the nose in the air |

**That is, *ānifan* means "the near past", and the picture of the word is this: the *nose* of the past — its outermost, nearest part.**

**The word occurs in this form only here in the Qurʾān.**

**And the choice of the word stresses how short a time ago the question refers to.** The question is not "what did he say the other day?"; it is **"what did he say just now?"**

**They have stepped out of the door. The voice is still in the air.**

### The tone of the question: a disagreement

**With what intention this question is asked has been debated in the classical commentaries:**

| View | Tone | Ground |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | **Mockery and belittlement** | The question is asked in order to show that what was said is not taken seriously; in the sense of "what was that nonsense?". |
| **2** | **A genuine failure to understand** | They listened but grasped nothing; the question is a sincere expression of ignorance. |
| **3** | **Indifference** | Neither mockery nor curiosity; simply that no trace has remained. |

**In the classical commentaries the first two views are widely transmitted. I make no preference.**

**But I record this: the continuation of the verse makes a diagnosis** — *ṭabaʿa'llāhu ʿalā qulūbihim* — and this diagnosis agrees with all three readings. The mocker, the one who does not understand and the one who is indifferent have all arrived at the same outcome: **the word did not enter.**

### لِلَّذِينَ أُوتُوا۟ ٱلْعِلْمَ — the addressee of the question

**And this expression is the sharpest detail in the verse.**

**Whom do they ask? *Those who have been given knowledge*.**

**أُوتُوا۟** — passive: "they were given". That is, knowledge is named not as something acquired but as something **given**.

**And this completes the scene:**

| | **The first group** | **The second group** |
|---|---|---|
| Where they were | In the same gathering | In the same gathering |
| What they did | **يَسْتَمِعُ** — listened | *(heard the same words)* |
| What is in their hands on the way out | **Nothing** | **Something — enough to be asked about** |
| Word | — | **أُوتُوا۟ ٱلْعِلْمَ** |

**That is, the verse records the difference between two groups who were in the same room.**

**And this is the subject of the next verse (17).** The sixteenth verse recounts those who were emptied, the seventeenth those in whom there was increase.

### طَبَعَ ٱللَّهُ عَلَىٰ قُلُوبِهِمْ — the seal

**طَبَعَ** — root **ط-ب-ع**: **to seal, to press a stamp upon something.** From the same root **طَابِع** (a seal), **طَبِيعَة** (nature — the unchanging make of a thing, stamped upon it), **طِبَاعَة** (printing, the press).

**The concrete picture of the root: pressing a stamp into a soft material (wax, clay) so that the material now takes that form.**

**And the particle is striking: عَلَىٰ.** *Ṭabaʿa **ʿalā** qulūbihim* — "He set a seal **upon** their hearts". The seal is placed not inside the heart but on top of it.

**This will come back in the twenty-fourth verse** — there the *locks* too will be **upon** the heart: *am **ʿalā** qulūbin aqfāluhā.*

| Verse | Expression | Object | Particle |
|---|---|---|---|
| **16** | *ṭabaʿa'llāhu **ʿalā** qulūbihim* | A seal | **عَلَىٰ** |
| **24** | *am **ʿalā** qulūbin aqfāluhā* | Locks | **عَلَىٰ** |

**The same particle, the same organ, two different means of closing.** And there is an important difference: in verse 16 **the agent is stated** (God); in verse 24 **it is not.** I shall take this up separately at verse 24.

**And on the matter of the heart:** this concept was treated in full in `002-bakara.md` (2/7 — *khatama'llāhu ʿalā qulūbihim*) and the root ق-ل-ب was taken up in `067-mulk.md`, `083-mutaffifin.md`, `084-insikak.md`, `104-humeze.md` and `079-naziat.md`. **I do not repeat it here.** The one note to be carried over from there is this:

> "**قَلْب** is in the Qurʾān not the organ of feeling but the centre of **understanding, deciding and turning towards**. Its root is **ق-ل-ب**, meaning 'to turn, to turn over' — the heart's bearing this name is explained by its turning from state to state."

**And this sharpens the picture of the seal** — it is my own reading: the heart is the thing that **turns**; the seal is the thing that **stops the turning**. The root of the word and the operation performed upon it are each other's opposite.

### وَٱتَّبَعُوٓا۟ أَهْوَآءَهُمْ — a repetition of the fourteenth verse

**This clause is letter for letter the same as the last clause of verse 14.**

47/14: … *wa'ttabaʿū ahwāʾahum*
47/16: … *wa'ttabaʿū ahwāʾahum*

**The same sentence, two verses apart.** And the subjects of the two verses appear to be different groups: in 14 those whose deed has been made to look fair, in 16 those who listen and take nothing.

**The sūra seals two different scenes with the same sentence.**

**An observation — it is my own reading:** in both cases there is a **filling**. The person whose deed is made to look fair fills the empty place with an adornment; the person who listens and takes nothing fills the empty place with his own desire. That the sentence is the same in both places says that the emptiness is filled with the same thing.

**I present this as a reading, not as a transmitted report.**

---

## 47/17 — وَٱلَّذِينَ ٱهْتَدَوْا۟ زَادَهُمْ هُدًى وَءَاتَىٰهُمْ تَقْوَىٰهُمْ

*Wa'lladhīna'htadaw zādahum hudan wa ātāhum taqwāhum*

"And as for those who have found the way, [God] has increased them in guidance and has given them their guarding of themselves."

### Two attitudes, one input

**These two verses — 16 and 17 — form a pair and must be read together.**

| | **47/16** | **47/17** |
|---|---|---|
| Who | *minhum man yastamiʿu* | *alladhīna'htadaw* |
| Form of the verb | **ٱسْتَمَعَ** — Form X | **ٱهْتَدَىٰ** — Form VIII |
| Outcome | *mādhā qāla ānifā* — nothing | **زَادَهُمْ هُدًى** — increase |
| Diagnosis | *ṭabaʿa'llāhu ʿalā qulūbihim* | *wa ātāhum taqwāhum* |

**And note: both verbs are in forms that involve *effort*.**

- **ٱسْتَمَعَ** (Form X) — to lend one's ear; a deliberate act.
- **ٱهْتَدَىٰ** (Form VIII) — to seek guidance, to try to find the way; deliberate again.

**That is, the place where the two groups part is not "one made an effort and the other did not".** Both of them did something.

**The distinction enters an area connected with the analysis of ز-ي-غ recorded at 61/5 in the section `061-saff.md`** — what was recorded there about how an inclination becomes fixed works here too. It may be consulted; I do not repeat it here.

### ٱهْتَدَىٰ — the root ه-د-ي and Form VIII

**Root: ه-د-ي.** **This root was treated in detail in the section `001-fatiha.md` (*ihdina'ṣ-ṣirāṭa'l-mustaqīm*) and taken up again in `090-beled.md`, `093-duha.md`, `087-ala.md`, `067-mulk.md` and `079-naziat.md`. I do not repeat it here.** In summary: the concrete sense of the root is **to show the way, to go in front**; and *hadiyya* (a gift) is from the same root — something directed towards someone.

**What is to be added here is the work of Form VIII.**

**ٱهْتَدَىٰ** — "he found guidance, he entered the right way". This form here announces **acceptance of the result of an act** (*muṭāwaʿa*).

That is: *hadāhu fa'htadā* — "He guided him, and he took the way". The verb is the **meeting** of a guidance that comes from outside.

**This explains the structure of the verse:** *ihtidāʾ* is already an act of receiving. And more is given to the one who receives.

### زَادَهُمْ هُدًى — who is the agent?

**The subject of the verb is not stated** and the exegetes have dwelt on this:

| View | Agent | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | **God** | "God increased their guidance" |
| **2** | **The words they heard / the sūra sent down** | "Those words increased their guidance" |
| **3** | **Guidance itself** | "Guidance increased them" |

**In the classical commentaries the first and second views are widely transmitted.** The ground of the second is the context: in the preceding verse a **word** was being listened to.

**I make no preference.** But I record this: **if the second view is accepted, the opposition between the two verses is complete** — the same words leave no trace at all in one and produce increase in the other.

### The exegesis of the Qurʾān by the Qurʾān: two results of the same thing

**The Qurʾān sets this idea up explicitly in several places, and in all of them it uses the same verb (**ز-ي-د**):**

> *"When a sūra is sent down, some of them say: 'Which of you has this increased in faith?' **As for those who believe, it has increased them in faith** and they rejoice. **As for those in whose hearts is a disease, it has added defilement to their defilement.**"* (al-Tawba 9/124-125)

**These two verses are almost an exact counterpart of our verses 16-17** — and there too the matter is **the sending down of a sūra**. Indeed verse 20 of this sūra will take up exactly this: *"When a sūra is sent down it is said…"*

> *"And We send down of the Qurʾān that which is **a healing and a mercy for the believers**; **and it increases the wrongdoers in nothing but loss.**"* (al-Isrāʾ 17/82)

**All three texts build the same structure: a single input, two separate outputs.**

**And this is the most remarkable feature of the verse — I record it as my own reading:** the text describes itself as a **separator**. The work of the word is not only to give information; it is to **divide** those addressed. The same words, spoken to two people, move the two apart from one another.

### وَءَاتَىٰهُمْ تَقْوَىٰهُمْ — and the subtlety in the pronoun

**The last two words of the verse carry an expression that deserves to be dwelt on.**

**تَقْوَىٰهُمْ** — "**their** guarding of themselves". A pronoun has been attached.

**And the verb: ءَاتَىٰ** — to give. That is, *taqwā* is something **given**.

**These two together look strange:** is *taqwā* not a state a person earns for himself? If it is something given, why is it called "theirs"?

**The explanations transmitted in the classical commentaries run as follows:**

| View | Explanation |
|---|---|
| **1** | *Taqwāhum* = "the *taqwā* that **befits** them, that belongs to them" — to each according to his own rank. |
| **2** | *Taqwāhum* = "**the reward of** their *taqwā*" — the governed term elided (*thawāba taqwāhum*). |
| **3** | *Taqwā* **itself** has been given; the power to accomplish is a gift. |

**I make no preference.**

**But I record an observation — it is my own reading:** the double structure of the expression (its being both given and belonging to them) may be not a tension but a **description**. What is most a person's own may at the same time have been given to him. The wording holds both directions at once: *ātāhum* (given) and *taqwāhum* (theirs).

**And the word *taqwā*:** the root is **و-ق-ي** — to guard, to guard oneself. The word has been treated in many places in this commentary (`002-bakara.md`, `049-hucurat.md` and others) and **I do not repeat it here.** In summary: *taqwā* is, before fear, **guarding**; it is putting a shield between oneself and something.

### What this says to the present

**Verses sixteen and seventeen together say something about knowledge, and what they say looks to the present without any need to force it.**

**Two people who listen to the same lesson do not take away the same thing.** This is a fact everyone knows, and it is generally explained by "attention", "intelligence" or "prior knowledge".

**The distinction the verse sets is none of these.** In both verses there is an act of listening, and in both there is intent. The distinction shows itself in **what the listener means to do**: in one, a question is asked on the way out; in the other, there is increase.

**And the scene of the sixteenth verse is more than familiar today:** a talk is listened to, one sits through it from beginning to end, one even takes notes; and when it is over not a single sentence remains. The act has been performed in full; the result has not formed.

**The verse does not explain the cause of this as "inattention".** The diagnosis it gives is far heavier and has two parts: the heart's having been sealed, and desire's being followed. **Whether the second is the cause or the consequence of the first — the verse does not say, and neither do I.**

---

## 47/18 — فَهَلْ يَنظُرُونَ إِلَّا ٱلسَّاعَةَ أَن تَأْتِيَهُم بَغْتَةً فَقَدْ جَآءَ أَشْرَاطُهَا فَأَنَّىٰ لَهُمْ إِذَا جَآءَتْهُمْ ذِكْرَىٰهُمْ

*Fa-hal yanẓurūna illa's-sāʿata an taʾtiyahum baghtatan; fa-qad jāʾa ashrāṭuhā; fa-annā lahum idhā jāʾat-hum dhikrāhum*

"Are they waiting for anything but the Hour, that it should come upon them suddenly? Its **signs** have already come. And when it has come to them, what use will their remembering be?"

### يَنظُرُونَ — the same verb, another sense

**Root: ن-ظ-ر.** In the tenth verse this verb was in the sense of "**to look**": *fa-yanẓurū kayfa kāna ʿāqibatu…*

**Here it is in the sense of "to wait".**

**And these two senses are joined to one another in the root:** *naẓar* is a look turned in a direction; and to wait for something is likewise to turn one's eye in that direction. From the same root come **إِنْتِظَار** (*intiẓār* — waiting) and **مُنْتَظِر**.

**The sūra uses the same root in two senses, eight verses apart:**

| Verse | Expression | Sense |
|---|---|---|
| **10** | *fa-**yanẓurū** kayfa kāna ʿāqibatu* | **To look** — at the past |
| **18** | *fa-hal **yanẓurūna** illa's-sāʿata* | **To wait** — for the future |

**And this sets up a noteworthy opposition** — it is my own reading: the tenth verse proposed looking back; the eighteenth says that those who do not look back are looking forward. **The same verb, two directions.** And what they are waiting for is the very thing they do not look at: a destruction.

### هَلْ … إِلَّا — the *qaṣr* construction

**The sentence looks like a question but announces a ruling.**

The combination of **هَلْ** (the particle of interrogation) + **إِلَّا** (the particle of exception) announces **restriction** (*qaṣr*) in Arabic: "anything other than…?" = "only …".

**That is, the sentence says: the only thing they are waiting for is the Hour.** They have no other expectation.

**And this is a hard sentence** — because no one says he is waiting for the Hour. The verse names what they are **in fact** waiting for: the end of what they are doing, and that alone.

### بَغْتَةً — the root ب-غ-ت

**Root: ب-غ-ت.** Its meaning: **suddenly, all at once, to be caught unprepared.** *Baghatahu'l-amr* — the matter caught him suddenly.

**بَغْتَةً** is in the accusative, and the grammarians count it either a circumstantial or an absolute object; the difference of meaning is not great.

**And what the word carries is not the lack of time but the lack of *preparation*.** A thing does not come "suddenly"; it comes suddenly to the one who is unprepared.

### فَقَدْ جَآءَ أَشْرَاطُهَا — the signs

**أَشْرَاط** — the plural of **شَرَط**. **Root: ش-ر-ط.**

**The family of meanings in the root is interesting:**

| Word | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **شَرْط** (*sharṭ*) | The qualification to which a matter is tied — a "condition" |
| **شَرَط** (*sharaṭ*) | **A sign, a mark, an indication** |
| **أَشْرَاط** | Signs |
| **شُرْطَة** (*shurṭa*) | The police — it is said that they took this name because they carried a distinguishing **mark** upon them |
| **شَرِيط** | A strip, a band |

**The common core: a distinguishing mark placed upon a thing.**

**And that *sharṭ* and *ʿalāma* are from the same root is meaningful** — I record it as an observation: both show that one thing is **tied** to another. A condition is the qualification to which a result is tied; a sign is the mark to which what is coming is tied.

**The tense of the sentence is perfect: فَقَدْ جَآءَ — "they have already come".** And at its head is **قَدْ**; with the perfect it announces **certification** (*taḥqīq*).

**That is, the verse states in a language of certainty that the signs have come.**

**And a note on method is needed here:** **the verse does not say what the signs are.** It gives no number, no list, no description.

**In the classical commentaries there is a wide literature on these signs, and I do not enter that literature in this commentary.** The reason is the method (`STYLE.md`): I do not transmit reports of which I am not certain, and the subject of the signs of the Hour contains more disputed transmission than the method of this commentary can carry.

**The only thing I shall record is an explanation widely transmitted in the classical commentaries:** that the Prophet's having been sent is itself counted among the signs. **I transmit this as a view; there is no such determination in the wording of the verse.**

**And a warning — I write it plainly:** no event of the present day will be marked as a "sign" on the strength of this verse. The verse already uses the perfect: the signs **have come**. To draw a reckoning for today comes not from the language of the verse but from outside it.

### فَأَنَّىٰ لَهُمْ … ذِكْرَىٰهُمْ

**أَنَّىٰ** — an interrogative particle: "how, from where, in what way". It is an interrogative of denial: "from where should it come?"

**The word order of the sentence is inverted and requires attention:**

- **ذِكْرَىٰهُمْ** — the subject (at the end).
- **أَنَّىٰ لَهُمْ** — the predicate (at the head).
- **إِذَا جَآءَتْهُمْ** — the condition interposed.

Turned into a straight order: *dhikrāhum annā lahum idhā jāʾat-hum* — "their remembering, when it comes upon them, from where [should it profit] them?"

**ذِكْرَىٰ** — root **ذ-ك-ر**: to remember, to call to mind. *Dhikrā* is a verbal noun in the sense of "remembering, taking heed".

**And the pronoun is striking again: ذِكْرَىٰهُمْ — "their taking heed".** That is, taking heed is named not as something that comes from outside but as **an act they will perform**.

**What the sentence says is simple, and its hardness lies here:** taking heed has a **time**, and that time is before the event happens. To "understand" after the event has happened is no longer taking heed.

**The Qurʾān sets this idea up elsewhere too** — al-Fajr 89/23: *"On that Day man will remember; but what use will the remembering be to him?"* (treated in `089-fecr.md`).

---

## 47/19 — فَٱعْلَمْ أَنَّهُۥ لَآ إِلَٰهَ إِلَّا ٱللَّهُ وَٱسْتَغْفِرْ لِذَنۢبِكَ وَلِلْمُؤْمِنِينَ وَٱلْمُؤْمِنَٰتِ وَٱللَّهُ يَعْلَمُ مُتَقَلَّبَكُمْ وَمَثْوَىٰكُمْ

*Fa'lam annahū lā ilāha illa'llāhu wa'staghfir li-dhanbika wa li'l-muʾminīna wa'l-muʾmināt; wa'llāhu yaʿlamu mutaqallabakum wa mathwākum*

"**Know, then,** that there is no god but God, and ask forgiveness for your sin and for the believing men and the believing women. God knows the place where you move about and the place where you come to rest."

---

**This verse is the turning point of the sūra, and its first word deserves to be dwelt on separately.**

### فَٱعْلَمْ — the commanding of tawḥīd as a piece of *knowledge*

**The word is an imperative: ٱعْلَمْ — "know".**

**And this is a remarkable choice.** The word of tawḥīd occurs many times in the Qurʾān; but here it is set out with **an imperative verb**, and the verb chosen is not **to believe** but **to know**.
