The sentence could have been:

- *fa-āmin annahū lā ilāha illa'llāh* — "believe that…". **It does not say this.**
- *fa-qul lā ilāha illa'llāh* — "say that…". **It does not say this.**
- *fa'shhad* — "bear witness". **It does not say this.**

**فَٱعْلَمْ** — **know.**

### The root ع-ل-م and what *ʿilm* is

**Root: ع-ل-م.** **This root has been treated in many places in this commentary** (`049-hucurat.md` — its distribution in that sūra; `055-rahman.md` — the weave of *ʿallama* and *aʿlām*; `062-cuma.md` — *yuʿallimu* and gradualness; `053-necm.md`). **I do not repeat it here.**

One note to be carried over from there: as was stated in `055-rahman.md`, the lexicographers connect this root with **عَلَامَة** (*ʿalāma* — a mark); on that account knowledge is what is obtained by setting out from the **mark** of a thing.

**And this sharpens the verse's command** — because in the preceding verse exactly **أَشْرَاط** (signs) had occurred.

| Verse | Word | Root |
|---|---|---|
| **18** | **أَشْرَاطُهَا** — its signs | ش-ر-ط |
| **19** | **فَٱعْلَمْ** — know | ع-ل-م |

**Two verses in succession: the signs have come → know.**

**I record this connection as my own reading.** The relation in root between *ʿilm* and *ʿalāma* is a view the lexicographers record and is not certain; to rest this sequence between the two verses upon it is my own inference.

### The order of knowledge and deed

**The word order of the verse sets up a sequence:**

1. **فَٱعْلَمْ** — know.
2. **وَٱسْتَغْفِرْ** — ask forgiveness.

**Knowledge first, act after.** And the two commands are joined with a *wāw*; the *wāw* announces mere conjunction, not sequence — but the **order in the wording** stands in the text all the same.

**In the classical sources this verse has been cited as a proof for the principle that "knowledge comes before deed".** It is transmitted that in al-Bukhārī's *Ṣaḥīḥ*, in the Book of Knowledge, there is a chapter heading to this effect, and that this verse is cited there.

**I transmit this as it is transmitted;** I do not speak with certainty about the exact wording of the source.

**And an observation on the principle itself — it is my own reading:**

The work a person does can be done independently of his knowing why he does it. The sequence of the verse ties the second command (*istighfār*) to the first (knowledge). Asking forgiveness is a behaviour that can be performed without knowing from whom it is asked; the verse closes this off first.

### أَنَّهُۥ — the matter of the pronoun

**فَٱعْلَمْ أَنَّهُۥ لَآ إِلَٰهَ إِلَّا ٱللَّهُ** — the **هُ** pronoun in *annahū* is the **pronoun of the matter** (*ḍamīru'sh-shaʾn*): a pronoun that stands for the whole of the sentence following it and refers back to nothing before it.

**The work of this pronoun is to make what follows it important.** The grammarians record: *ḍamīru'sh-shaʾn* adds a weight and a drawing of attention to the sentence — "the matter is this, that…".

### لَآ إِلَٰهَ إِلَّا ٱللَّهُ — the structure of the sentence

**The word of tawḥīd is one of the densest sentences in Arabic, and its structure should be recorded briefly:**

**لَآ** — *lā al-nāfiya li'l-jins*: the *lā* that negates the genus. In the eleventh verse the same particle was present in *lā mawlā lahum*.

**This particle does not say "there is not a god"; it says "there is nothing of the genus of god".**

**إِلَٰهَ** — its noun, indeclinable on fatḥa.

**And its predicate is elided.** The grammarians dispute what this predicate is: *mawjūd* (existing) or *ḥaqqun* (true)? The second implied form is the widespread one, because if the first is accepted the sentence would deny the **existence** of other objects of worship — whereas what is denied is not their existence but **their being gods**.

**I content myself with recording this debate and I make no preference.**

**إِلَّا ٱللَّهُ** — the exception; and the divine name comes in the nominative (because it is a *badal*).

### وَٱسْتَغْفِرْ لِذَنۢبِكَ — and a disagreement

**ٱسْتَغْفِرْ** — root **غ-ف-ر**, Form X: to **ask** for forgiveness. The form announces a request.

**ذَنۢب** — root **ذ-ن-ب**. And the concrete sense of the root matters: **ذَنَب** (*dhanab*) — **tail.**

**The connection the lexicographers make is this:** *dhanb* is what **comes behind** a deed — its result, its outcome. That is, sin is named in the root of the word as "the thing that trails after".

**And this shares the same picture with the sūra's roots ع-ق-ب (outcome, 10) and د-ب-ر (*tadabbur* / *adbār*, 24-25-27): what comes from behind.**

**The real question in this verse is the addressee of the command:** the Prophet is told "ask forgiveness for your sin".

**The classical commentaries have dwelt on this and given several explanations:**

| View | Explanation |
|---|---|
| **1** | The address is to the Prophet but the community is meant; it is said to the Prophet and made heard by everyone. |
| **2** | *Dhanb* here is not in the sense of major sins; in keeping with the station of the prophets it means "not doing what would have been more fitting" (*tark al-awlā*). |
| **3** | *Istighfār* does not require that a sin has been committed; it is a state of servanthood and is performed continually. |
| **4** | The command is in order to **teach** the community how *istighfār* is to be made. |

**These views are transmitted in the classical commentaries. I make no preference and I do not enter the theological debate on the impeccability of the prophets.**

**The only thing I shall record is what stands in the text:** the verse opens *istighfār* to **two addressees** — first to himself, then to the believers. And the order runs in that direction.

### وَلِلْمُؤْمِنِينَ وَٱلْمُؤْمِنَٰتِ — the two sexes named separately

**And the verse does not content itself with naming the believers by a single word (*li'l-muʾminīn*); it counts men and women separately.**

**In Arabic the masculine plural covers mixed groups as well.** That is, had *li'l-muʾminīn* been said, women would have been included. Even so the verse mentions the two separately.

**This is a usage seen in several places in the Qurʾān** and the best known is al-Aḥzāb 33/35 — where ten pairs of attributes are listed separately for men and for women.

**What is to be recorded is this:** a distinction not grammatically necessary has been made in the text. And the place where this distinction is made is a sentence of **prayer**.

**I record this as a textual observation;** I do not attempt to draw a general conclusion from it, but it needs to be stated that the distinction stands in the text.

### وَٱللَّهُ يَعْلَمُ مُتَقَلَّبَكُمْ وَمَثْوَىٰكُمْ

**The verse closes by repeating at its end the root of its first word:**

- **فَٱعْلَمْ** — know (imperative, addressee: you).
- **وَٱللَّهُ يَعْلَمُ** — God knows (a statement, subject: God).

**The same root at its two ends. And in between something changes: who it is that knows.**

**I record this as an observation about word order.**

### مُتَقَلَّب and مَثْوَى — movement and rest

**مُتَقَلَّب** — root **ق-ل-ب**, the noun of place / verbal noun of Form V: **the place where one turns about**, or **the state of turning about.**

**مَثْوَى** — root **م-ث-و**: **the place where one stops, where one stays.** This word **occurred in verse 12** (*wa'n-nāru mathwan lahum*).

**The two words form a pair and are opposites:**

| Word | Root | What |
|---|---|---|
| **مُتَقَلَّب** | ق-ل-ب — to turn | The state of **movement** / the place of movement |
| **مَثْوَى** | م-ث-و — to stay | The state of **rest** / the place of rest |

**That is, the sentence divides a person's life into two states: the time he moves and the time he is still.** And it says that it knows both.

**And there is disagreement over what the two are:**

| View | *Mutaqallab* | *Mathwā* |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | Your moving about by day | Your sheltering by night |
| **2** | Your movement in this world | Your place in the hereafter |
| **3** | Your coming and going in your affairs | Your resting place in the grave |
| **4** | Your states before coming into the world (your passage from loins to wombs) | Your stay in this world |

**All of these views are transmitted in the classical commentaries. I make no preference.**

**I record this much:** what the four views have in common is that **the two words have been chosen so as to cover the whole of a human life.** Whichever division is taken, there is no time left outside.

**And the pronoun is in the *second person plural*: كُمْ.** The first two commands of the verse were singular (*fa'lam*, *wa'staghfir* — to the Prophet); the last sentence moves to the plural. **This is *iltifāt*** (a shift in the direction of address).

**This shift agrees with the general tendency of the sūra:** the text opens out a word spoken to one person upon everyone. We shall see the same movement in verse 30 as well — there too the clause *wa taʿrifannahum* (singular) will close with *wa'llāhu yaʿlamu aʿmālakum* (plural).

---

## 47/20 — وَيَقُولُ ٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوا۟ لَوْلَا نُزِّلَتْ سُورَةٌ فَإِذَآ أُنزِلَتْ سُورَةٌ مُّحْكَمَةٌ وَذُكِرَ فِيهَا ٱلْقِتَالُ رَأَيْتَ ٱلَّذِينَ فِى قُلُوبِهِم مَّرَضٌ يَنظُرُونَ إِلَيْكَ نَظَرَ ٱلْمَغْشِىِّ عَلَيْهِ مِنَ ٱلْمَوْتِ فَأَوْلَىٰ لَهُمْ

*Wa yaqūlu'lladhīna āmanū law lā nuzzilat sūra; fa-idhā unzilat sūratun muḥkamatun wa dhukira fīha'l-qitālu raʾayta'lladhīna fī qulūbihim maraḍun yanẓurūna ilayka naẓara'l-maghshiyyi ʿalayhi mina'l-mawt; fa-awlā lahum*

"Those who believe say: 'If only a sūra were sent down!' Then, when a firmly fixed sūra is sent down and fighting is mentioned in it, you see those in whose hearts is a disease looking at you **with the look of one in a swoon at death**. And that is what befits them."

### Two forms: نُزِّلَتْ and أُنزِلَتْ — in the same sentence

**This verse is one of the rare places that shows the difference of form I touched on at the second verse *within a single sentence*.**

| Clause | Verb | Form |
|---|---|---|
| The wish: *law lā **nuzzilat** sūra* | **نُزِّلَتْ** | ***tafʿīl*** |
| The occurrence: *fa-idhā **unzilat** sūratun* | **أُنزِلَتْ** | ***ifʿāl*** |

**The distinction recorded in the classical sources is this:** *tanzīl* (*tafʿīl*) announces a sending down piece by piece, gradually; *inzāl* (*ifʿāl*) a sending down all at once.

**When this distinction is applied to this verse a reading like this comes out:** what is wished for is a revelation **continually coming down** ("if only it kept being sent down"); what happens is a sūra that comes down **as a whole**.

**I transmit this reading as an explanation found in the classical commentaries.**

**And I am obliged to enter a caveat:** that this distinction is not applied consistently everywhere in the Qurʾān is stated in the sources themselves. Indeed this same sūra uses *anzala* in verse 9 and *nazzala* in verse 26, and no marked difference of meaning appears there.

**For this reason I record the distinction as a possibility, not as a certainty.**

### لَوْلَا — the particle of wishing

**لَوْلَا** here announces **urging** (*taḥḍīḍ*, encouragement, insistent asking): "if only it were sent down!". It is said that with the imperfect it announces encouragement and with the perfect reproach (*tawbīkh*).

**And note: who makes this wish? *alladhīna āmanū* — those who believe.**

**That is, this section of the sūra opens with a request from the believers.** The request is legitimate: to want revelation to come down.

**And then the request is granted. And exactly when it is granted, something becomes visible.**

### مُّحْكَمَة — the root ح-ك-م

**Root: ح-ك-م.** And the primary sense of the root is not, as is often supposed, "to rule".

**The primary sense the lexicographers record: مَنْع — to hold back, to restrain, to make firm.**

And its concrete source is interesting: **حَكَمَة** — the **bit** put in a horse's mouth. The bit keeps the horse from going where it wants.

**The family of meanings that comes out of this:**

| Word | Meaning | Connection |
|---|---|---|
| **حُكْم** | Judgement | The decision that **holds** a matter back from arbitrariness |
| **حِكْمَة** | Wisdom | The knowledge that **holds** a person back from error |
| **حَاكِم** | Judge | The one who has the authority to hold back |
| **مُحْكَم** | Firm, tight | **Its slack taken out**, not shifting |
| **إِحْكَام** | Making firm | — |

**And a *muḥkam* sūra, by this root, is: a sūra with no slack in it, that cannot be shifted, that cannot be slid about.**

**The exegetes give two senses to the word *muḥkama* in this verse:**

| View | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **1** | A sūra **not abrogated, whose ruling is fixed** |
| **2** | A sūra **clear, with no possibility of interpretation** — the opposite of the ambiguous (*mutashābih*) |

**Both views are transmitted; I make no preference.**

**But I record an observation — it is my own reading:** both senses look in the same direction, and both agree with the root's sense of "not taking slack". And this does a piece of work in the scene of the verse: **a text that cannot be shifted brings out the one who wants to shift it.** An ambiguous word allows everyone to interpret it as he pleases; a firmly fixed word does not. That is why the reaction becomes visible.

### وَذُكِرَ فِيهَا ٱلْقِتَالُ

**ٱلْقِتَال** — root **ق-ت-ل**, the verbal noun of Form III (*mufāʿala*).

**And the form matters.** Form III announces **reciprocity** (*mushāraka*): the act being done mutually between two parties.

- **قَتْل** (*qatl*) — to kill. One-directional.
- **قِتَال** (*qitāl*) — **mutual killing, fighting.** Two-directional.

**That is, the word carries in its own structure the fact that the other side is doing the same thing.** *Qitāl* cannot be done alone.

**And the second name of the sūra — as is transmitted — is exactly this word: *Sūrat al-Qitāl*.** The word occurs only here in the sūra.

**A detail of word order: the verb is passive — ذُكِرَ ("was mentioned").** Who mentioned it is not said. And there is the qualification *fīhā* (in it, in the sūra): fighting occurs as a subject **mentioned inside** the sūra, not as the subject of the sūra.

### نَظَرَ ٱلْمَغْشِىِّ عَلَيْهِ مِنَ ٱلْمَوْتِ — the comparison

**This is the picture at the centre of the verse, and it is built with a single word.**

**مَغْشِىّ عَلَيْهِ** — root **غ-ش-و/غ-ش-ي**: **to cover, to overlay, to envelop.** From the same root **غِشَاوَة** (*ghishāwa* — a film, a covering; at al-Baqara 2/7 the film over the eyes), **غَاشِيَة** (*ghāshiya* — that which covers, that which envelops; the name of sūra 88, treated in `088-gasiye.md`).

**مَغْشِىّ عَلَيْهِ** — "the one who has been covered over", that is, **one who has fainted, whose consciousness has closed.** The English that carries it exactly is "one who has passed out".

**مِنَ ٱلْمَوْتِ** — "from death". That is, this is not an ordinary fainting; it is the state of a person **in the throes of death**.

**And the subject of the comparison is not the person but the look:** *naẓara'l-maghshiyyi ʿalayh* — "a look like **his look**". An absolute object; the comparison attaches to the verb.

**This is the same choice of word order as *kamā taʾkulu'l-anʿām* in verse 12:** the comparison is made not to the person but to the act.

**What, then, is the property of the look of a person in a swoon at death?**

**The description the lexicographers and the exegetes record is this:** the eyes are open, fixed, unblinking; the movement of the eyeballs has stopped; the gaze is as if locked on one place. **The one who looks is not seeing.**

**And this does its work exactly in the scene of the verse:** those people are looking at the Prophet. They are in the gathering. Their eyes are on him. **But what they see is not what is being said; it is their own end.**

**This reading — that the look is "frozen with fear" — I transmit from the explanation widely found in the classical commentaries.**

**And its link with the sixteenth verse is worth noting** — it is my own reading:

| Verse | Organ | Act | Outcome |
|---|---|---|---|
| **16** | **Ear** | *yastamiʿu* — he listens | He takes nothing |
| **20** | **Eye** | *yanẓurūna ilayka* — they look | They do not see |

**The sūra makes the same diagnosis twice, by way of two organs: the act is performed, the outcome does not form.**

### فِى قُلُوبِهِم مَّرَضٌ — disease

**مَرَض** — root **م-ر-ض**: disease; and the definition the lexicographers give: **a thing's departure from equilibrium (*iʿtidāl*), the spoiling of health.**

**And that the word comes indefinite is worth noting: مَّرَضٌ — "a disease".** Indefinite. Unnamed.

**This expression occurs twice in the sūra:** 20 and 29 (*am ḥasiba'lladhīna fī qulūbihim maraḍun*). **In both the same structure: *fī qulūbihim maraḍun*.**

**And the word order is inverted:** the normal order would be *maraḍun fī qulūbihim* (subject + predicate). The verse **brings the predicate forward**: *fī qulūbihim maraḍun.*

**In grammar this fronting announces *ikhtiṣāṣ* (particularisation) and the drawing of attention.** That is, the emphasis falls on the **place** of the disease: the disease is in the heart.

### فَأَوْلَىٰ لَهُمْ — a disagreement over the pause

**And now we come to the two words at the end of the verse. This is one of the best-known disagreements over the pause (*waqf*) in the Qurʾān, and it changes the meaning directly.**

**أَوْلَىٰ لَهُمْ** can be read in two ways, and the reading depends on **where the pause is made**:

| # | Pause | Attachment | Meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| **1** | *fa-awlā lahum* is **the end of verse 20** | To what precedes it | **A threat:** "That is what befits them" / "Woe to them". The next verse begins independently: *"[What falls to them is] obedience and a fitting word."* |
| **2** | *fa-awlā lahum* attaches **to verse 21** | To what follows it | **A direction:** "What would be **better** for them is obedience and a fitting word." |

**Both readings are transmitted in the classical sources, and the marks of pause in the copies of the muṣḥaf reflect this disagreement.**

**I make no preference.** The difference of meaning the two readings give is large: the first is an imprecation, the second an advice.

**And the word *awlā* itself:** the root is **و-ل-ي** — treated in the discussion of *mawlā* at verse 11. *Awlā* is an elative: **"nearer, more deserving".**

**This too feeds the disagreement:** the expression "more deserving" can mean both "deserving the punishment" and "what is more fitting for him". The word is open in both directions.

**I record this much:** the word *awlā* occurs in this construction in one other place in the Qurʾān — al-Qiyāma 75/34-35: *"**Awlā laka fa-awlā, thumma awlā laka fa-awlā**"* (treated in `075-kiyame.md`). The use there is in the direction of threat, and this appears to support the first reading.

**But this is recorded as an indication, not as a preference;** the same construction is not obliged to carry the same meaning in two places.

---

## 47/21 — طَاعَةٌ وَقَوْلٌ مَّعْرُوفٌ فَإِذَا عَزَمَ ٱلْأَمْرُ فَلَوْ صَدَقُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ لَكَانَ خَيْرًا لَّهُمْ

*Ṭāʿatun wa qawlun maʿrūf; fa-idhā ʿazama'l-amru fa-law ṣadaqu'llāha la-kāna khayran lahum*

"[What falls to them is] obedience and a fitting word. And when the matter has been **settled**, had they been true to God it would have been better for them."

### طَاعَةٌ وَقَوْلٌ مَّعْرُوفٌ — the parsing

**The two words are in the nominative and there is no verb in the clause.** The grammarians supply several implied forms:

| # | Implied form | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | The predicate of an elided subject: *amrunā ṭāʿatun* | "Our matter/our command is obedience and a fitting word" |
| **2** | Subject with an elided predicate: *ṭāʿatun… **awlā lahum*** | "Obedience and a fitting word would be better for them" (the second reading of the pause above) |
| **3** | Counted as the object of an elided verb | "Obey and speak a fitting word" |

**I make no preference.**

### طَاعَة — the root ط-و-ع

**Root: ط-و-ع.** And the primary sense of the root is not "to submit".

**The primary sense the lexicographers record: to do a thing *willingly, easily, without being forced*.** Its opposite is **كُرْه** (by force, unwillingly) — and these two words are set explicitly against one another in the Qurʾān: *"Come willingly or unwillingly"* — **طَوْعًا أَوْ كَرْهًا** (Fuṣṣilat 41/11, al-Tawba 9/53).

**And note: the root *ك-ر-ه* occurred three times in this sūra (9, 26, 28).** Now its opposite comes.

**From the same root: تَطَوُّع (*taṭawwuʿ*) — a work done voluntarily and over and above; مُطَاوَعَة; إِسْتَطَاعَة (*istiṭāʿa*) — to be able, to have the power.**

**That is, *ṭāʿa* carries in its root the idea of *doing willingly* more than that of obeying a command.** What is done by force is not called *ṭāʿa* in Arabic.

**This takes on a sharp meaning in the context of the verse:** the verse proposes "obedience" to people who look like one in a swoon at death. And the word proposed is the word that includes **doing willingly**.

### قَوْلٌ مَّعْرُوفٌ — a fitting word

**مَعْرُوف** — root **ع-ر-ف**: to be known, to be recognised. And **مَعْرُوف** is the passive participle: **"known, recognised"** — and from there: **what is accepted as good by common usage, what is reasonable.**

**In the root of the word there is an idea of *commonality*:** *maʿrūf* is what everyone knows and recognises. Its opposite is **مُنكَر** (*munkar*) — **"unrecognised, found strange"**; built on the same logic.

**And this root occurred in the fifth verse as well: عَرَّفَهَا لَهُمْ.** The second occurrence within the sūra.

**قَوْلٌ مَّعْرُوفٌ** — "a known/reasonable word". That is, a word without exaggeration, apt, one the other side too will recognise.

**And this connects with the central matter of the section `061-saff.md`.** That sūra treated from beginning to end **the distance between what is said and what is done**:

> *"Why do you say what you do not do?"* (al-Ṣaff 61/2)

**The same distance is at issue here** — but from the other side: the verse proposes not the speaking of a great word but the speaking of a **reasonable** one.

### فَإِذَا عَزَمَ ٱلْأَمْرُ — a figure

**عَزَمَ** — root **ع-ز-م**: to make a firm decision about a matter, to resolve. From the same root **عَزِيمَة** (*ʿazīma*) and **أُو۟لُوا۟ ٱلْعَزْمِ** (the prophets of firm resolve, al-Aḥqāf 46/35).

**And the word order is striking: the subject of the verb is ٱلْأَمْرُ — the matter itself.**

That is, the sentence does not say "when people decide on the matter"; it says **"when the matter has been settled"**. What resolves is not the person but the matter.

**This is a *majāz ʿaqlī*** — of the same kind as *akhrajatka* (the town drove you out) in verse 13. The verb is attributed not to its real agent but to the thing in which the act took place.

**And this has an effect** — it is my own reading: when it is said "when the matter has been settled", the settling appears as something in no one's hands. The moment of decision has come; there is no one who postpones it or brings it on. The sentence places the **inevitability** of that moment into the language.

### فَلَوْ صَدَقُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ

**لَوْ** — again the counterfactual: a condition that did not come about. "Had they been true… it would have been — but they were not."

**صَدَقُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ** — root **ص-د-ق**: to be true. And the verb is used **directly transitive**: *ṣadaqu'llāha* — "had they dealt truly with God".

**And this usage connects with the concept of *ṣidq* treated in `061-saff.md` and `002-bakara.md`.** The last clause of al-Baqara 2/177 was treated there: *ulāʾika'lladhīna ṣadaqū* — "those are the ones who are true".

**What the root carries is wider than truthfulness in speech:** *ṣidq* is a thing's **matching its own claim**. Of a sword one says *sayfun ṣādiqun* — a sword that is said to cut and really does cut.

**That is, "to be true towards God" is to do what the word given to Him requires.**

**And this clause of the verse answers directly the scene of verse 20.** There was a wish there (*law lā nuzzilat sūra*) and when the wish was granted there was a collapse. What is said here is this: **doing what a wish requires is different from making the wish.**

**I rest this reading on the frame set up in the section `061-saff.md`;** the matter treated there works in exactly the same way in this verse.

### لَكَانَ خَيْرًا لَّهُمْ

**The answer of the sentence: "it would have been better for them".**

**And note: for whom?** *Lahum* — **for them.** That is, the benefit of truthfulness is entered not to the account of the one towards whom it is shown but to that of the one who shows it.

**This is a logic that will be repeated in the last verse of the sūra:** *wa man yabkhal fa-innamā yabkhalu **ʿan nafsih*** (38) — "whoever is miserly is miserly only towards himself."

**Two verses point in the same direction for two different acts:** both what is done and what is not done come back first upon the doer himself.

---

## 47/22 — فَهَلْ عَسَيْتُمْ إِن تَوَلَّيْتُمْ أَن تُفْسِدُوا۟ فِى ٱلْأَرْضِ وَتُقَطِّعُوٓا۟ أَرْحَامَكُمْ

*Fa-hal ʿasaytum in tawallaytum an tufsidū fi'l-arḍi wa tuqaṭṭiʿū arḥāmakum*

"Is it to be expected of you, if you turn away / if you are put in charge, that you will spread corruption in the land and cut off your ties of kinship?"

### A variant reading — and it changes the meaning

**A variant reading is transmitted for this verse and it directly determines the sense of the verse:**

| Reading | Voice | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| **تَوَلَّيْتُمْ** (*tawallaytum*) | Active | "**If you turn away**" |
| **تُوُلِّيتُمْ** (*tuwullītum*) | Passive | "**If you are put in charge / if authority is given to you**" |

**Both readings are transmitted. I make no preference.**

**And the two scenes the two readings give are these:**

- **On the first reading:** the consequence of turning away is corruption. That is, moving away from revelation ends in social dissolution.
- **On the second reading:** the trial of gaining authority is corruption. That is, what a person who comes into power will do first is put in question.

**Both readings stand in the text and both agree with the context of the sūra.**

### تَوَلَّى — the two directions of the word

**Root: و-ل-ي.** It was treated in the discussion of *mawlā* at verse eleven; the note recorded there does its work here:

> "**تَوَلَّى** (*tawallā*) — To take charge of a matter; **and also: to turn away and go.**"

**And the variant reading rests on exactly these two senses.** The word itself carries both directions at once; the reading determines which comes to the front.

**How can one word be both "to take charge" and "to turn away"?**

**The explanation the lexicographers give is this:** the core of the root is **nearness and turning towards**. *Tawallā* is "to turn in a direction". And a person who turns in one direction has **turned away from another**.

**That is, the word names a single movement; the two senses are the two ends of that same movement.**

**And this makes the verse meaningful independently of the variant reading as well** — it is my own reading: in either case there is a **turning**. One turns towards power, the other in a direction away from revelation. In both, a direction is turned to by a choice.

**The word occurs twice in the sūra:** 22 (*in tawallaytum*) and 38 (*wa in tatawallaw*). **The first warning and the last warning of the sūra use the same verb.**

### فَهَلْ عَسَيْتُمْ — the construction

**عَسَىٰ** — a verb in Arabic announcing **hope** (*tarajjī*) and **apprehension** (*ishfāq*). When attributed to God it is used in the sense of "it will certainly be so"; when attributed to human beings, in the sense of "perhaps, it may be that".

**Here it has the interrogative particle at its head: فَهَلْ عَسَيْتُمْ.** This combination sets up a **question of reproach**: "is it that you…?"

**And the hardness of the construction lies here:** the sentence is built not as an accusation but as a **possibility**. It does not say directly "you will spread corruption"; it says "is it that you will?"

**This is a construction that leaves the answer to the addressee** — the same method as the *a-fa-man… ka-man* construction of verse 14.

### أَنْ تُفْسِدُوا۟ فِى ٱلْأَرْضِ — the root ف-س-د

**Root: ف-س-د.** And the opposite of the root is **ص-ل-ح**, which we treated at the second verse.

**The sense the lexicographers record: a thing's departure from equilibrium, its spoiling, its becoming useless.**

**And the opposition of the two sets up a frame in this sūra:**

| Verse | Root | Expression |
|---|---|---|
| **2** | **ص-ل-ح** | *wa **aṣlaḥa** bālahum* |
| **5** | **ص-ل-ح** | *wa **yuṣliḥu** bālahum* |
| **22** | **ف-س-د** | *an **tufsidū** fi'l-arḍ* |

**The sūra ties setting-right to the inward state (*bāl*) and corruption to the earth.** One is inside, the other outside.

**I record this as a lexical observation;** the opposition of the two roots is lexical data, this inside-outside distribution is a connection I am making.

### وَتُقَطِّعُوٓا۟ أَرْحَامَكُمْ — to cut

**تُقَطِّعُوا۟** — root **ق-ط-ع**, Form *tafʿīl*: **to cut to pieces.** The same form occurred in verse 15 as well (*fa-qaṭṭaʿa amʿāʾahum*).

**And when the two occurrences are set side by side something interesting appears:**

| Verse | Expression | What is cut |
|---|---|---|
| **15** | *fa-**qaṭṭaʿa** amʿāʾahum* | **Bowels** — the inner bond of the body |
| **22** | *wa **tuqaṭṭiʿū** arḥāmakum* | **Wombs** — the inner bond of society |

**The same verb, the same form, two different "inner bonds".**

**I record this parallel as my own reading;** I make no claim that a verbal link has been set up between the two verses, but that the verb is the same stands in the text.

### أَرْحَام — the root ر-ح-م

**Root: ر-ح-م.** **This root was treated in full in the discussion of the basmala in the section `001-fatiha.md` and taken up again in `060-mumtehine.md`, `090-beled.md`, `049-hucurat.md` and `057-hadid.md`. I do not repeat it here.**

The one note to be carried over from there: **رَحِم** (*raḥim*) — the mother's womb; and the word *raḥma* comes from this concrete source.

**What is to be added here is the word's use in this verse: أَرْحَام, plural — "ties of kinship".**

The word names not the organ directly but the **relationship** that arises from that organ: the bond among those who come from the same womb.

**And in the word order of the verse two things stand side by side:**

- **فِى ٱلْأَرْضِ** — the earth. **The widest scale.**
- **أَرْحَامَكُمْ** — kinship. **The narrowest scale.**

**That is, the verse counts both ends of the spoiling at once:** the largest circle and the smallest circle.

**And this connects directly with the frame set up in the section `049-hucurat.md`.** It was recorded there:

> "This arrangement carries an idea of order, and the logic of the order is this: **when the outer rings are broken there is visible damage; when the inner ring is broken nothing at all is visible.**"

**This verse places the same two rings side by side in a single sentence.**

---

## 47/23 — أُو۟لَٰٓئِكَ ٱلَّذِينَ لَعَنَهُمُ ٱللَّهُ فَأَصَمَّهُمْ وَأَعْمَىٰٓ أَبْصَٰرَهُمْ

*Ulāʾika'lladhīna laʿanahumu'llāhu fa-aṣammahum wa aʿmā abṣārahum*

"Those are the ones whom God has cursed, so that He has made them deaf and blinded their eyes."

### لَعَنَ — the root ل-ع-ن

**Root: ل-ع-ن.** And the associations of the usual English rendering ("to curse") do not give the primary sense of the root exactly.

**The primary sense the lexicographers record: طَرْد وَإِبْعَاد — to drive away and remove to a distance.**

**That is, *laʿna* is not a word but a *distance*.** God's curse is a person's being removed from mercy.

From the same root **لَعِين** (*laʿīn* — driven away) and **مَلْعُون**.

**And this makes the word order of the verse meaningful:** the curse comes first, and two verbs follow it. That is, the removal is not a result but a **beginning**.

### فَأَصَمَّهُمْ وَأَعْمَىٰٓ أَبْصَٰرَهُمْ — two organs

**أَصَمَّ** — root **ص-م-م**, Form IV: **to make deaf.** And the primary sense of the root: **a thing's inside being full, stopped up.** *Ḥajarun aṣamm* — a solid stone with no cavity in it. *Ṣammāʾ* — without hole, sound.

**That is, deafness is named in the root as "the ear's being blocked, its inside being filled"** — not the lack of an organ but the closing of a passage.

**أَعْمَىٰ** — root **ع-م-ي**, Form IV: **to blind.**

**And note: the objects of the two verbs are different:**

- *aṣamma**hum*** — "He made **them** deaf". The object is the persons themselves.
- *aʿmā **abṣārahum*** — "He blinded **their eyes**". The object is the organ.

**This asymmetry has drawn the attention of the exegetes and is explained thus:** in Arabic the verb *aṣamma* is attributed directly to the person (*aṣammahu*), whereas *aʿmā* can be attributed both to the person and to the eye. That is, the difference may come from usage.

**I record one further observation — it is my own reading:** in the sentence the organ of the ear is not mentioned at all, while the organ of the eye is. And this agrees with two earlier scenes of the sūra: in verse 16 there was listening but no mention of the ear; in verse 20 there was looking and the look was described.

**I do not present this as a binding exegesis.**

### The chain of senses within the sūra

**This verse is a link in a chain built up in the sūra, and the chain needs to be seen as a whole:**

| Verse | Sense / organ | What happens |
|---|---|---|
| **16** | **Ear** — *yastamiʿu* | He listens, takes nothing |
| **16** | **Heart** — *ṭabaʿa ʿalā qulūbihim* | Sealed |
| **17** | *(the opposite)* | It increases |
| **20** | **Eye** — *yanẓurūna ilayka* | They look, they do not see |
| **23** | **Ear + eye** | Made deaf and blind |
| **24** | **Heart** — *aqfāluhā* | Locked |

**Within four verses the sūra closes all three organs: ear, eye, heart.**

**And this triad is the Qurʾān's standard triad.** At al-Aʿrāf 7/179 (which I mentioned at verse 12) the same triad is counted: *"they have hearts with which they do not understand, eyes with which they do not see, ears with which they do not hear."* At al-Baqara 2/7 the same triad again: hearts and ears sealed, a film over the eyes.

**And in this sūra the triad comes *distributed*:** it is not gathered into a single verse but spread over four.

**I record this as an observation about word order.**

### A note on method: the matter of the agent

**In this verse the agent of the verbs is explicitly God:** *laʿanahumu'llāhu fa-aṣammahum wa aʿmā.*

**And this raises an unavoidable question:** if it is God who makes deaf and blinds, to whom does the responsibility belong?

**This is one of the oldest debates in theology, and I do not enter that debate in this commentary.** The reason is the method (`STYLE.md`): the views of the schools may be transmitted but not preferred, and on this matter even the transmitting would be honest only with a long context.

**The only thing I shall record is what stands in the text:**

**One.** At the head of the verse there is a **فَ**: *laʿanahumu'llāhu **fa**-aṣammahum.* The *fāʾ* announces sequence and cause. That is, the making deaf comes **after** the curse.

**Two.** And the curse itself had been tied to a cause: the verbs in verse 22. Since the third verse the sūra has gone on giving the ground of its rulings.

**Three.** And the next verse of the sūra (24) asks a **question**: *"Do they not ponder the Qurʾān?"* — that is, an act is still expected from the one addressed.

**This third point matters:** if the closing were absolute and irreversible, the question of the next verse would be meaningless.

**I record this observation as my own reading and do not present it as a theological preference.**

---

## 47/24 — أَفَلَا يَتَدَبَّرُونَ ٱلْقُرْءَانَ أَمْ عَلَىٰ قُلُوبٍ أَقْفَالُهَآ

*A-fa-lā yatadabbarūna'l-Qurʾāna am ʿalā qulūbin aqfāluhā*

"Do they not **ponder** the Qurʾān? Or are there locks of their own upon hearts?"

---

**This verse is one of the most direct sentences the Qurʾān says about itself, and two of its words deserve to be analysed separately.**

### تَدَبُّر — the root د-ب-ر

**Root: د-ب-ر.** And the concrete sense of the root carries the whole weight of the word: **the back, the back of a thing.**

**دُبُر** (*dubur*) — the back, the rear. And the family of meanings that comes out of this:

| Word | Meaning | Connection |
|---|---|---|
| **دُبُر / أَدْبَار** | Back(s) | The concrete sense |
| **أَدْبَرَ** (*adbara*) | To turn one's back and go | To turn one's back |
| **دَابِر** | The **end**, the root of a thing | What remains furthest behind |
| **تَدْبِير** (*tadbīr*) | To arrange a matter, to reckon its outcome | To think of the **back** of a matter |
| **مُدَبِّر** | The one who arranges affairs | — |
| **تَدَبُّر** (*tadabbur*) | **To get behind a thing, to think of its end** | — |

**And the connection the lexicographers record is this: *tadbīr* is to arrange a matter by looking at its *outcome*.** That is, the word names planning as "seeing the back of a thing".

**This root was taken up in `079-naziat.md` (79/5 — *al-mudabbirāti amrā*), `050-kaf.md`, `052-tur.md`, `054-kamer.md`, `074-muddessir.md`, `059-hasr.md` and `070-mearic.md`.** The analysis here is particular to the word **تَدَبُّر** and is made for the first time in this commentary here.

### Form تَفَعُّل — and beyond reading

**تَدَبَّرَ** — Form V. And this form announces two things:

| Function | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **Taking pains** | To do a thing **with effort, with labour** |
| **Gradualness** | To do a thing **little by little, step by step** |

**That is, *tadabbur* is not something that happens at a glance.** The pattern of the word says that the work **takes time** and **requires labour**.

**Now let us put two things together:**

- **The root:** to get behind a thing.
- **The form:** with effort, step by step.

**The result: *tadabbur* is to advance step by step towards the back of a text, its end, the place it arrives at.**

**And this is different from "reading".** Reading is advancing **over** a text: going from beginning to end. *Tadabbur* is getting **behind** the text.

**A table of comparison:**

| Verb | Root | What it does |
|---|---|---|
| **قَرَأَ** (*qaraʾa*) | ق-ر-أ | **To gather, to bring together** — to read by gathering letters and words |
| **تَلَا** (*talā*) | ت-ل-و | **To go after** — to follow in order |
| **تَدَبَّرَ** | د-ب-ر | **To get behind** — to look at the end, at the place it arrives at |
| **تَفَكَّرَ** | ف-ك-ر | **To take apart into its pieces** (treated in `059-hasr.md`) |

**Four verbs, four different works.** And the verse asks about the **third** of the four.

### The most striking weave within the sūra: the three occurrences of the root د-ب-ر

**And now we come to the most striking verbal weave in this sūra.**

**The root د-ب-ر occurs three times in this sūra, and all three within four consecutive verses:**

| Verse | Word | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| **24** | **يَتَدَبَّرُونَ** | To get behind a thing and think |
| **25** | **عَلَىٰٓ أَدْبَٰرِهِم** | Upon their **backs** [to turn] |
| **27** | **وُجُوهَهُمْ وَأَدْبَٰرَهُمْ** | Their faces and their **backs** |

**Three verses, the same root, three different works.**

**And there is a relation of direction among them — I record this as my own reading, it carries no claim of deliberateness:**

- **24:** To get behind the text — **a movement forward** (going to see the end of a thing).
- **25:** To turn upon one's own back — **a movement backward** (going back to where one came from).
- **27:** Being struck on face and back — **the closing of both directions.**

**That is, the sūra uses the word "back" three times and turns it each time in another direction.** The person who does not get behind a thing turns upon his own back; and in the end both his front and his back are closed.

**I record this weave as a fact standing in the text** (that the three words are from the same root is beyond dispute); **the relation of meaning set up among them, however, is my reading.**

### The exegesis of the Qurʾān by the Qurʾān: where *tadabbur* occurs

**This root occurs in the sense of "to ponder" in four places in the Qurʾān**, and in all four its object is the Qurʾān or its verses:

| Place | Expression |
|---|---|
| **al-Nisāʾ 4/82** | *"Do they not **ponder** the Qurʾān? Had it been from other than God they would have found much contradiction in it."* |
| **al-Muʾminūn 23/68** | *"Have they not **pondered the word**?"* — *a-fa-lam yaddabbaru'l-qawl* |
| **Ṣād 38/29** | *"A blessed Book We have sent down to you, that they may **ponder its verses** and that those of understanding may take heed."* |
| **Muḥammad 47/24** | *"Do they not ponder the Qurʾān?"* |

**All four occurrences are tied to the same object.** The word is used for nothing else in the Qurʾān.

**And Ṣād 38/29 is striking:** there *tadabbur* is given as the **reason for the sending down** of the book — *li-yaddabbarū*. That is, the text ties the cause of its own existence to this verb.

**And at al-Nisāʾ 4/82 the verb is tied to a *method*:** if there is pondering, contradiction will be looked for and will not be found. That is, *tadabbur* is not looking at the text with an acceptance brought from outside; it is **testing the text**.

**When these two verses are read together it becomes clear what *tadabbur* is** — I record it as an observation: the verb is neither a passive reading nor an advance assent. It is getting behind the text and **probing its consistency**.

### أَمْ — which "am"?

**In Arabic there are two kinds of *am*:**

| Kind | Its name | Its function |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | **مُتَّصِلَة** (*muttaṣila*) | "…or…?" — it asks for a choice between two alternatives |
| **2** | **مُنقَطِعَة** (*munqaṭiʿa*) | It has the sense of **بَلْ + هَمْزَة**: "no; rather… is it?" — it changes the subject and opens a new question |

**The *am* here is *munqaṭiʿa*** and this determines how the verse is read.

**That is, the verse is not asking for a choice between two alternatives.** It says: *"Do they not ponder? — No; is it that there are locks upon hearts, then?"*

**The second question is not the answer to the first; it is a possibility put forward as an *explanation* of the first.** And the possibility is left in the form of a question.

### أَقْفَال — the root ق-ف-ل

**Root: ق-ف-ل.** **قُفْل** — a lock; **أَقْفَال** — locks.

**And there is an interesting member in the family of meanings of the root:**

| Word | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **قُفْل** | Lock |
| **قَافِلَة** (*qāfila*) | **Caravan** — and originally: the **returning** caravan |
| **قَفَلَ** | He returned; and: he locked |
| **قَافِل** | One who sets out on a journey and **returns** |

**The lexicographers record that the word *qāfila* originally meant "a company returning from a journey";** later it came to be used for the outgoing caravan too. The Turkish word *kafile* (a party of travellers) comes from this word.

**The root also records a sense of *dryness and hardness*:** *qafala'l-jild* — the hide dried out, it hardened.

**I do not speak with certainty about how these senses are connected to one another.** The lexicographers give different explanations and I have no sound measure by which to prefer among them. **What I record is that the words are found in the same root** — this is lexical data.

**As a reading — it is my own inference — I note this:** a lock prevents a thing from **being opened**; and the sense of "turning" in the root brings to mind the working of a lock (the turning of the key). But this is a conjecture and **I do not present it as a claim about derivation.**

### Word order: "hearts" indefinite, "their locks" definite

**And now we come to the finest point of the verse.**

**عَلَىٰ قُلُوبٍ أَقْفَالُهَآ** — there are two nouns in the clause and the two come in different forms:

| Word | Its state | How |
|---|---|---|
| **قُلُوبٍ** | **Indefinite** | With nunation: *qulūbin* |
| **أَقْفَالُهَا** | **Definite** | A construct with a pronoun: "**their** locks" |

**The first point: *qulūb* is indefinite.**

The verse does not say **ٱلْقُلُوب** (al-qulūb — "the hearts", definite). It says **قُلُوبٍ** — "some hearts", "a portion of hearts".

**In grammar this is called *tankīr li't-taqlīl*: indefiniteness announcing fewness.**

**And its meaning is this: the ruling is set not upon all hearts but upon a portion of them.** The verse does not say "people's hearts are locked".

**In terms of the method (`STYLE.md`) this yields a direct consequence:** the verse does not issue a blanket ruling upon a community. The indefiniteness narrows the scope.

**The second point: *aqfāl* is made definite by a pronoun.**

**أَقْفَالُهَا** — "**their** locks". The pronoun refers back to *qulūb*.

**That is, the locks are not general locks brought from outside; they are locks *belonging to those hearts*.** Each heart's own lock.

**On a first reading this looks like a small detail, but it is not.** The sentence could have been built like this:

- *am ʿalā qulūbihim aqfālun* — "are there locks on their hearts?" (the lock indefinite, the heart definite). **It does not say this.**
- *am ʿalā qulūbin aqfālun* — "are there locks on some hearts?" (both indefinite). **It does not say this either.**

**The verse makes the heart indefinite and the lock belong to that heart.**

**And the picture that comes out of this is this — it is my own reading:** the lock is not a foreign instrument fitted to the heart afterwards; it is **that heart's own lock.** That is, every closing has a form peculiar to itself.

**I present this as a reading.** That the pronoun refers back to *qulūb* is grammatical data; the "each heart's own lock" interpretation I draw from it is my own inference, and I ascribe it to no classical exegete.

**The third point: the plural.**

**أَقْفَال** is plural — not a lock but locks.

**This too is worth recording:** not a single obstacle but more than one. And if a door has more than one lock, opening one is not enough.

### عَلَىٰ — the lock's being *on top*

**And the particle matters again: عَلَىٰ قُلُوبٍ — "upon hearts".**

**The lock is not inside the heart but on top of it.**

**This is the same structure as the seal in the sixteenth verse** (*ṭabaʿa'llāhu **ʿalā** qulūbihim*) and was treated in `002-bakara.md` in the expression *khatama'llāhu **ʿalā** qulūbihim* at 2/7.

**And the picture itself is this:** a lock is fitted from outside. The heart is not a locked chest; **the lock is on the lid.**

**And who fitted the lock?**

**The verse does not say.**

**And this is striking when compared with the sixteenth verse:**

| Verse | Expression | Agent |
|---|---|---|
| **16** | *ṭabaʿa **ٱللَّهُ** ʿalā qulūbihim* | **Explicitly: God** |
| **24** | *am ʿalā qulūbin aqfāluhā* | **Not stated** |

**The same sūra, eight verses apart, recounts the same operation once with an agent and once without.**

**This enters into the theological debate on destiny and I do not enter that debate.** What I record is what stands in the text: **the sūra houses both accounts and does not prefer one to the other.**

### To whom the question is put

**A note on method is needed.**

The verb of the verse is in the **third person plural:** *yatadabbarūna* — "do they not ponder?" That is, it is not asked of the addressee directly.

**But the form of the question — *a-fa-lā*, an interrogative of denial — leaves the answer to the reader.**

**And the context of the verse had described who "they" are in verses 20-23:** those in whose hearts is a disease, that is, those among the people who make a claim to faith.

**This increases the hardness of the verse.** The question is not put to someone who has never heard the Qurʾān. **It is put to someone present in the setting in which the Qurʾān came down, who asks for sūras to be sent down, who sits in the gathering.**

### What this says to the present

**What this verse says to the present has to do not with drawing a method out of it but with keeping the word itself.**

**Three different relations can be had with a text, and the three are different things:**

| Relation | What is done |
|---|---|
| **Reading** | One passes over the text |
| **Memorising** | The text is preserved |
| **Tadabbur** | **One gets behind the text** |

**The verse does not reject the first two; but what it asks about is the third.**

**And the second half of the verse gives a possibility of diagnosis** — it is my own reading: if a text is read over and over and nothing changes, the trouble is not in the text. The verse names this possibility "a lock" and places the lock on top of the heart.

**I present this as an application;** the wording of the verse does not describe a method, it asks a question.

---

## 47/25 — إِنَّ ٱلَّذِينَ ٱرْتَدُّوا۟ عَلَىٰٓ أَدْبَٰرِهِم مِّنۢ بَعْدِ مَا تَبَيَّنَ لَهُمُ ٱلْهُدَى ٱلشَّيْطَٰنُ سَوَّلَ لَهُمْ وَأَمْلَىٰ لَهُمْ

*Inna'lladhīna'rtaddū ʿalā adbārihim min baʿdi mā tabayyana lahumu'l-hudā; ash-shayṭānu sawwala lahum wa amlā lahum*

"Those who have turned back upon their backs after the guidance had become clear to them: Satan has made [the evil] look fair to them and has **drawn out the time** for them."

### ٱرْتَدُّوا۟ عَلَىٰٓ أَدْبَٰرِهِم — the picture of the idiom

**ٱرْتَدَّ** — root **ر-د-د**, Form VIII: **to turn back.** The primary sense of the root: to turn a thing back, to return it. From the same root *radd*, *mardūd*, *irtidād*.

**عَلَىٰٓ أَدْبَٰرِهِم** — "upon their backs".

**And the picture of the idiom is concrete:** in turning back the person does not turn his face and go; **he goes backwards, upon his back.**

**The Qurʾān uses this idiom in several places** and the best known is Āl ʿImrān 3/144: *"If he dies or is killed, will you turn back **upon your heels**?"* — *inqalabtum ʿalā aʿqābikum.*

**Note: Āl ʿImrān uses *aʿqāb* (heels), here *adbār* (backs) is used.** Two different words, the same picture.

**And Āl ʿImrān 3/144 is the verse in which the name this sūra bears occurs** — it was given in the table above, in the section on "the name Muḥammad".

**And the link with the preceding verse:** verse 24 said *tadabbur* (to get behind); verse 25 says *adbār* (backs). **The same root, in two consecutive verses, in two opposite directions.**

### مِّنۢ بَعْدِ مَا تَبَيَّنَ لَهُمُ ٱلْهُدَى — a qualification

**This qualification determines whom the verse is speaking about and must not be passed over.**

**تَبَيَّنَ** — root **ب-ي-ن**, Form V: **to come out into the open, to become clear.** The word *bayyina* was treated at verse 14.

**That is, the verse is not speaking of those who turn back without knowing anything.** What is at issue is: one who **knows, sees, to whom it has become clear** — and then turns back.

**And the same qualification will be repeated letter for letter in verse 32:** *min baʿdi mā tabayyana lahumu'l-hudā.*

| Verse | Expression |
|---|---|
| **25** | *min baʿdi mā tabayyana lahumu'l-hudā* |
| **32** | *min baʿdi mā tabayyana lahumu'l-hudā* |

**The same sentence in two verses.** And both do the same work: they narrow the scope of the ruling to an act performed **after knowledge has been had**.

**In terms of the method (`STYLE.md`) this is a limit that must be recorded:** the verse does not issue a ruling upon one who does not know.

### سَوَّلَ — the root س-و-ل

**Root: س-و-ل.** The sense the lexicographers record: **to make a thing look beautiful, easy and desirable to a person; to adorn what the soul wants and set it before it.**

**The word is explained by the lexicographers with two different derivations:**

| Explanation | Connection |
|---|---|
| **1** | It is connected with **سُؤْل** (*suʾl* — the thing asked for; Ṭāhā 20/36: *ūtīta suʾlaka*): to set before the soul what it asks for |
| **2** | It is connected with the sense of **slackening / letting down** in the root: to **let a thing down** before a person |

**I make no preference between the two.**

**The word occurs in four places in the Qurʾān** and all four have the same structure:

| Place | Expression |
|---|---|
| **Yūsuf 12/18** | *bal **sawwalat** lakum anfusukum amrā* — "your souls have made a thing look fair to you" |
| **Yūsuf 12/83** | The same expression |
| **Ṭāhā 20/96** | *wa kadhālika **sawwalat** lī nafsī* — "and thus did my soul make it look fair to me" |
| **Muḥammad 47/25** | *ash-shayṭānu **sawwala** lahum* |

**And there is a striking distribution:** in three occurrences the agent is **the soul**; only in this verse is the agent **Satan**.

**I record this as a textual observation.**

**And its relation with *zuyyina lahu* in verse 14:** there the verb was passive, the agent was not stated. Here the agent is stated explicitly: **ash-shayṭān**.

| Verse | Verb | Agent |
|---|---|---|
| **14** | **زُيِّنَ** — was made to look fair | **Not stated** |
| **25** | **سَوَّلَ** — made it look fair | **الشَّيْطَان** |

**The sūra recounts the same operation once without an agent and once with one** — just as in verses 16 and 24 it recounted the closing of the heart once with an agent and once without.

**I record this recurring structure as an observation about word order.**

### Word order: ٱلشَّيْطَٰنُ سَوَّلَ لَهُمْ — fronting

**The normal order of the sentence would be *sawwala lahumu'sh-shayṭān* (verb + agent).** The verse brings the agent forward: **ٱلشَّيْطَٰنُ سَوَّلَ لَهُمْ.**

**In grammar this fronting announces *ikhtiṣāṣ* or *taʾkīd*.**

**And further:** a **nominal sentence** comes as the predicate of *inna*. The nominal sentence announces fixity.

**I content myself with recording this.**

### وَأَمْلَىٰ لَهُمْ — the root م-ل-و and two disagreements

**أَمْلَىٰ** — root **م-ل-و / م-ل-ي**, Form IV.

**The primary sense of the root: a long stretch of time.** **مَلَاوَة** (*malāwa*) — a long span of time. **مَلِيًّا** (Maryam 19/46: *wa'hjurnī **maliyyā***) — "for a long while".

**From here: إِمْلَاء — to grant time, to give respite, to let out the rope.**

**A second branch from the same root: إِمْلَاء — to dictate** (to say something for another to write down; the word *imlāʾ* in use today). The connection is that speech is delivered **drawn out**.

**And there are two disagreements:**

**The first disagreement — who is the agent?**

| View | Agent | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | **Satan** (the agent of the preceding clause continues) | "Satan drew out the time for them / gave them empty hopes" |
| **2** | **God** | "God granted them time" — in the sense of *istidrāj* |

**The second disagreement — the variant reading.**

It is also transmitted that the word is read **أُمْلِىَ لَهُمْ** (*umliya lahum* — passive: "time was drawn out for them"). On this reading the agent becomes indefinite.

**I make no preference in either disagreement.**

**And I record this:** there is an indication in the Qurʾān supporting the second view — the verb *imlāʾ* occurs elsewhere attributed to God: *"Let not those who deny suppose that **Our granting them time** is good for them; We grant them time only that they may increase in sin"* (Āl ʿImrān 3/178) — *numlī lahum.*

**This is an indication, not a preference.**

**And the picture the word carries is the same on both views: *time is being drawn out*.** The difference lies in who is drawing it out.

**An observation — it is my own reading:** these two verbs (*sawwala* and *amlā*) together describe a mechanism. The first changes the **appearance** (what is bad looks good); the second draws out the **time** (the outcome is delayed). And there are two ways of understanding that something is wrong: because it looks bad, or because its outcome has arrived. The verse says that both have been closed off.

---

## 47/26 — ذَٰلِكَ بِأَنَّهُمْ قَالُوا۟ لِلَّذِينَ كَرِهُوا۟ مَا نَزَّلَ ٱللَّهُ سَنُطِيعُكُمْ فِى بَعْضِ ٱلْأَمْرِ وَٱللَّهُ يَعْلَمُ إِسْرَارَهُمْ

*Dhālika bi-annahum qālū li'lladhīna karihū mā nazzala'llāhu sa-nuṭīʿukum fī baʿḍi'l-amr; wa'llāhu yaʿlamu isrārahum*

"That is because they said to those who disliked what God sent down: 'We will obey you in some matters.' And God knows what they **keep secret**."

### The fourth sentence of ground

**The fourth occurrence of the sūra's *dhālika bi-anna* pattern** (3, 9, 11, **26**, 28).

**And this time the ground is a *word*: قَالُوا۟.** Neither a behaviour, nor a feeling, nor a relationship — a sentence.

### سَنُطِيعُكُمْ فِى بَعْضِ ٱلْأَمْرِ — partial obedience

**And the sentence spoken is itself the real matter of the verse.**

**سَنُطِيعُكُمْ** — *sīn* + imperfect: the future. "We will obey."

**And the root ط-و-ع was treated at verse 21:** *ṭāʿa* carries in its root the idea of **doing willingly**.

**فِى بَعْضِ ٱلْأَمْرِ** — "in **some** of the matter".

**And this qualification carries the whole weight of the sentence.**

The sentence does not say "we will obey you". It says **"in some matters"**. That is, a limit is set, a bargain is struck, a measure is kept.

**And at first glance this appears to *lighten* the sentence:** not a complete surrender but a partial compliance.

**But the ruling the verse gives is heavy** — and this is the place to dwell on.

**The reason is to whom the sentence is spoken: *li'lladhīna karihū mā nazzala'llāh*** — to those who disliked what God sent down.

**That is, a word of limited obedience is given to an unlimited opposition.**

**I record a reading — it is my own inference:** the qualification "in some matters" clears the speaker in his own eyes. The person has not changed sides completely; he has only fallen in on a part of it. The verse does not accept this qualification — and it says why in the next clause.

**I do not present this as a binding exegesis;** the verse does not write out explicitly why the qualification "in some matters" is not sufficient.

### نَزَّلَ / أَنزَلَ — the distribution within the sūra

**In this verse Form *tafʿīl* is used: نَزَّلَ.** In the ninth verse, in the same context, *ifʿāl* was used: أَنزَلَ.

**The distribution in the sūra as a whole:**

| Verse | Verb | Form | Context |
|---|---|---|---|
| **2** | **نُزِّلَ** | *tafʿīl* (passive) | "what has been sent down to Muḥammad" |
| **9** | **أَنزَلَ** | *ifʿāl* | "what God sent down" |
| **20** | **نُزِّلَتْ** | *tafʿīl* (passive) | The wish: "if only a sūra were sent down" |
| **20** | **أُنزِلَتْ** | *ifʿāl* (passive) | The occurrence: "when a sūra is sent down" |
| **26** | **نَزَّلَ** | *tafʿīl* | "what God sent down" |

**Five occurrences, two forms.** And 9 and 26 build the same expression (*karihū mā … Allāh*) with two different forms.

**This confirms the caution I recorded at verse 20:** the distinction of gradualness between the two forms is recorded in the classical sources, but it is not applied consistently everywhere in the Qurʾān. **This sūra is an instance of that.**

### وَٱللَّهُ يَعْلَمُ إِسْرَارَهُمْ — and a variant reading

**A variant reading is transmitted:**

| Reading | Structure | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| **إِسْرَارَهُمْ** (*isrārahum*) | The verbal noun of Form IV | "Their **keeping secret**" — the act |
| **أَسْرَارَهُمْ** (*asrārahum*) | The plural of **سِرّ** | "Their **secrets**" — the object |

**Both readings are transmitted. The difference of meaning is fine but real:** one brings forward the **act** of concealing, the other the **things** concealed.

**I make no preference.**

**Root: س-ر-ر.** And the root has an interesting property: **سِرّ** (*sirr* — a secret) and **سُرُور** (*surūr* — joy) are from the same root. So too is **سَرِير** (*sarīr* — a throne, a couch).

**The lexicographers make the connection thus:** joy is something **inside** a person; a secret is also what is inside. The common core may be **concealment and inwardness**.

**I transmit this as an explanation the lexicographers record; I do not present it as a settled relation of derivation.**

**And the work of the clause is clear:** the word spoken was spoken in secret (*li'lladhīna karihū* — to a particular group, separately). The clause records that the secrecy served no purpose.

**And this opens the subject of the next section of the sūra: verses 29-30 will recount how what is concealed comes out.**

---

## 47/27 — فَكَيْفَ إِذَا تَوَفَّتْهُمُ ٱلْمَلَٰٓئِكَةُ يَضْرِبُونَ وُجُوهَهُمْ وَأَدْبَٰرَهُمْ

*Fa-kayfa idhā tawaffat-humu'l-malāʾikatu yaḍribūna wujūhahum wa adbārahum*

"How will it be when the angels take them in death, striking their faces and their backs?"

### فَكَيْفَ — a question with no answer

**كَيْفَ** — an interrogative particle asking after a state: "how?"

**And the predicate of the question is missing from the clause:** it says "how will it be?" but does not say how what will be.

**The grammarians supply an ellipsis:** *fa-kayfa **yaṣnaʿūna / ḥāluhum*** — "how will they act / how will their state be?"

**And the gap that the ellipsis creates is a device of rhetoric** — this is a method the Qurʾān uses often: what is not said stays wider than what is said.

### تَوَفَّتْهُمُ — the root و-ف-ي

**تَوَفَّىٰ** — root **و-ف-ي**, Form V.

**And the primary sense of the root is not "to kill": تَمَام — to complete, to give or take in full.**

- **وَفَّى** — he paid in full.
- **وَفَاء** (*wafāʾ*) — to fulfil one's word completely.
- **أَوْفَى** — he completed, he gave in full. (At al-Muṭaffifīn 83/2-3 the matter of short measure was treated in `083-mutaffifin.md`.)
- **تَوَفَّى** — **to take in full.**

**That is, the verb used for "taking the soul" carries in its root the sense of "receiving in full".**

**And this is a subtlety standing in the choice of the word:** death is named not as a thing's being **cut off** but as a thing's being **completed**.

**I transmit this by way of the root sense the lexicographers record;** for interpretations that might be drawn from it the verse gives no ground.

### يَضْرِبُونَ وُجُوهَهُمْ وَأَدْبَٰرَهُمْ

**The third and last occurrence of the root ض-ر-ب in the sūra** (3, 4, **27**). It was treated at the third verse: three occurrences, three different senses.

**And the objects: وُجُوه (faces) and أَدْبَار (backs).**

**This is also the third and last occurrence of the root د-ب-ر in the sūra** (24, 25, **27**).

**A combined table of the three occurrences — the completion of the weave set up at verse 24:**

| Verse | Word | Direction |
|---|---|---|
| **24** | *yatadabbarūn* | To get behind the text — **what is asked** |
| **25** | *irtaddū ʿalā **adbārihim*** | To turn upon one's own back — **what is done** |
| **27** | *wujūhahum wa **adbārahum*** | Front and back — **what is closed** |

**And in the last verse *both face and back* are named:** that is, both directions.

**An observation — it is my own reading:** in verse 25 the person had turned upon his back; that is, he had chosen a direction. In verse 27 both directions are struck. No direction is left to turn to.

**I present this as a reading; the verse does not set up such a comparison.**

**And a note on method:** the scene the verse describes (the angels striking as they take the soul) occurs in one other place in the Qurʾān in almost the same words — al-Anfāl 8/50: *"When the angels take the souls of those who deny they **strike their faces and their backs**"* (*yaḍribūna wujūhahum wa adbārahum*).

**The same sentence in two verses.** I record this as textual data.

---

## 47/28 — ذَٰلِكَ بِأَنَّهُمُ ٱتَّبَعُوا۟ مَآ أَسْخَطَ ٱللَّهَ وَكَرِهُوا۟ رِضْوَٰنَهُۥ فَأَحْبَطَ أَعْمَٰلَهُمْ

*Dhālika bi-annahumu'ttabaʿū mā askhaṭa'llāha wa karihū riḍwānahu fa-aḥbaṭa aʿmālahum*

"That is because they followed what **angers** God and disliked His good pleasure; so He voided their deeds."

### The fifth and last sentence of ground

**The fifth and last occurrence of the sūra's *dhālika bi-anna* pattern.**

**And this is the densest use of the pattern:** in a single sentence there are **two grounds** (they followed + they disliked) and **one result** (*aḥbaṭa*).

### ٱتَّبَعُوا۟ — the fifth occurrence

**The fifth and last occurrence of the root ت-ب-ع in the sūra** (twice in 3, then 14, 16, **28**).

**And the finding I recorded at verse 14 is completed here:** the sūra describes the human being as **a creature that sets off after something**. In all five occurrences the same form (Form VIII), and each time a different object:

| Verse | Object |
|---|---|
| **3** | *al-bāṭil* — falsehood |
| **3** | *al-ḥaqq min rabbihim* — the truth from their Lord |
| **14** | *ahwāʾahum* — their desires |
| **16** | *ahwāʾahum* — their desires |
| **28** | *mā askhaṭa'llāh* — what angers God |

**Five objects, one verb.** This is the sūra's description of the human being.

### أَسْخَطَ — the root س-خ-ط

**Root: س-خ-ط.** Its meaning: **to be angry, to be displeased.** And the lexicographers describe it in opposition to **رِضَا** (*riḍā*).

**أَسْخَطَ** — Form IV: to make angry.

**And the word is in the same semantic field as *ghaḍab* (غضب), but the lexicographers record a distinction:** *sakhaṭ* is the opposite of good pleasure and its aspect of **displeasure** weighs more heavily; *ghaḍab* is anger itself.

**I transmit this distinction as the lexicographers record it; I do not draw firm boundaries.**

**And the word order of the verse bears this out:** in the second half of the sentence its exact opposite comes — **رِضْوَٰنَهُۥ**.

### وَكَرِهُوا۟ رِضْوَٰنَهُۥ — "to dislike good pleasure"

**This expression is strange on a first reading and deserves to be dwelt on.**

**رِضْوَان** — root **ر-ض-و/ر-ض-ي**: to be well pleased, to be content. **رِضْوَان**, on the *fiʿlān* pattern: **a great and complete good pleasure.**

**The word is used in the Qurʾān for God's good pleasure** and occurred at 57/20 in `057-hadid.md` (*wa maghfiratun mina'llāhi wa riḍwān*).

**How, then, does a person "dislike" God's good pleasure?**

**In the classical commentaries the explanation is given thus:** what is meant is not disliking good pleasure **itself** but disliking **what leads to it**. The governed term has been elided: *karihū **mā yūjibu** riḍwānahu* — "they disliked what would earn His good pleasure".

**I transmit this explanation and find it reasonable.**

**But the wording of the verse has chosen to avoid this ellipsis** and this has an effect — it is my own reading:

The sentence looks not at the **result** of the choice but at its **destination**. What a person dislikes may be a command, an obligation or a hardship; but the place that thing goes to is good pleasure. The verse names the act of disliking by **the place it arrives at**.

**I do not present this as a binding exegesis.**

**And the setting of *sakhaṭ* and *riḍwān* against one another in the same sentence is the most striking feature of the word order:**

| | Verb | Object |
|---|---|---|
| First | **ٱتَّبَعُوا۟** — they followed | **مَآ أَسْخَطَ ٱللَّهَ** — what angers |
| Second | **كَرِهُوا۟** — they disliked | **رِضْوَٰنَهُۥ** — His good pleasure |

**Both verbs announce a turning, and the two objects are each other's opposite.** That is, the sentence encloses the choice from both directions at once: they went to one, they fled from the other.

### فَأَحْبَطَ أَعْمَٰلَهُمْ

**The second occurrence of the root ح-ب-ط in the sūra** (9, **28**, and *sa-yuḥbiṭu* in 32).

**In the ninth verse this clause was letter for letter the same: فَأَحْبَطَ أَعْمَٰلَهُمْ.**

**And when the grounds of the two verses are compared a progression appears:**

| Verse | Ground |
|---|---|
| **9** | *karihū mā anzala'llāh* — they disliked what He sent down |
| **28** | *ittabaʿū mā askhaṭa'llāh **wa** karihū riḍwānahu* — they followed what angers Him **and** disliked His good pleasure |

**In the ninth verse there was one verb; in the twenty-eighth there are two.** And the result is the same.

**I record this as an observation about word order.**

---

## 47/29 — أَمْ حَسِبَ ٱلَّذِينَ فِى قُلُوبِهِم مَّرَضٌ أَن لَّن يُخْرِجَ ٱللَّهُ أَضْغَٰنَهُمْ

*Am ḥasiba'lladhīna fī qulūbihim maraḍun an lan yukhrija'llāhu aḍghānahum*

"Or did those in whose hearts is a disease suppose that God would never bring out their **rancours**?"

### أَمْ — again *munqaṭiʿa*

**As in verse 24, here too the *am* is *munqaṭiʿa*:** "no; or did they suppose…?"

**And this is the second use of *am* in the sūra.** Both open a new subject with a question.

### حَسِبَ — the root ح-س-ب

**Root: ح-س-ب.** Its primary sense is **to count, to reckon.** From the same root *ḥisāb*, *muḥāsaba*, *ḥasīb*.

**And the verb in its bare form means "to suppose, to think"** — and the lexicographers explain the connection thus: to "reckon" a thing is to arrive at a conclusion with the data in one's hand; and if the conclusion arrived at is not certain, it is a **supposition**.

**That is, *ḥasiba* is not a random fancy; it is a supposition resting on a reckoning.**

**And this increases the hardness of the verse** — I record it as an observation: the people in question have made a calculation. They have calculated that it would stay hidden. The verse says the calculation is wrong.

### فِى قُلُوبِهِم مَّرَضٌ — a repetition of the twentieth verse

**This expression occurred in verse 20 and is repeated here letter for letter.**

**The two occurrences frame this section of the sūra (20-31):** the matter of the diseased hearts opens at 20 and returns at 29.

### أَضْغَان — the root ض-غ-ن

**And now we come to one of the most distinctive words of the sūra.**

**Root: ض-غ-ن.** **ضِغْن** (*ḍighn*) — its plural **أَضْغَان** (*aḍghān*).

**The sense the lexicographers record: rancour and enmity hidden and kept within.** It is counted synonymous with *ḥiqd* (حقد).

**And a sense of *bending / crookedness* is also recorded in the root:** *ḍaghina'd-dābba* — the animal turned restive, would not go, bent its neck aside. From here: *ḍighn* is the heart's **leaning** to one side.

**I transmit this second explanation as a view the lexicographers record; I do not present it as a settled relation of derivation.** The word's sense of "hidden rancour" is beyond dispute.

**And the most striking property of the word is this: in the Qurʾān it occurs only in this sūra. Twice.**

| Verse | Expression | Pronoun |
|---|---|---|
| **29** | *an lan yukhrija'llāhu **أَضْغَٰنَهُمْ*** | **هُمْ** — **their** rancours |
| **37** | *wa yukhrij **أَضْغَٰنَكُمْ*** | **كُمْ** — **your** rancours |

**And this is one of the most remarkable movements in the sūra.**

**The same word, with the same verb (*yukhrij* — to bring out), is used twice.** In the first the pronoun is **third person**: "their". In the second, **second person**: "your".

**That is, what the sūra diagnosed as "the hidden rancours in diseased hearts" it says eight verses later directly to the one it addresses.**

**This is the clearest example of the sūra's general method** — and it is another appearance of the structure I recorded at the beginning of this commentary in the section "What is the sūra doing?":

| Word | For "them" | For "you" |
|---|---|---|
| **مَثْوَى** | 12 (*mathwan lahum*) | 19 (*mathwākum*) |
| **أَعْمَال** | 1, 8, 9, 28, 32 (*aʿmālahum*) | 33 (*aʿmālakum*) |
| **أَضْغَان** | 29 (*aḍghānahum*) | 37 (*aḍghānakum*) |

**Three words, three times the same movement: what is said about someone else is turned back upon the one addressed.**

**I record this table as textual data** (the places of the words and pronouns are fixed); **the claim that this is a deliberate method, however, is my reading.**

### يُخْرِجَ — to bring out

**أَخْرَجَ** — root **خ-ر-ج**, Form IV: **to bring out.**

**And the choice of the verb is worth noting.** The verse does not say "will know" or "will make manifest"; it says **"will bring out"**.

That is, rancour is something that is inside, and it **will be brought out**. The verb announces a change of place.

**And it has a link with the other occurrences of the root in the sūra:**

| Verse | Expression |
|---|---|
| **13** | *qaryatika'llatī **akhrajatka*** — your town which drove you out |
| **16** | *ḥattā idhā **kharajū** min ʿindika* — when they went out from your presence |
| **29** | *an lan **yukhrija**'llāhu aḍghānahum* | 
| **37** | *wa **yukhrij** aḍghānakum* |

**Four occurrences.** And a weave appears — it is my own reading: in verse 16 the people go out **themselves** (from the gathering); in 29 and 37 what is inside them **is brought out**. The act of going out is attributed first to the person and then to what is inside the person.

**I present this as a reading.**

---

## 47/30 — وَلَوْ نَشَآءُ لَأَرَيْنَٰكَهُمْ فَلَعَرَفْتَهُم بِسِيمَٰهُمْ وَلَتَعْرِفَنَّهُمْ فِى لَحْنِ ٱلْقَوْلِ وَٱللَّهُ يَعْلَمُ أَعْمَٰلَكُمْ

*Wa law nashāʾu la-araynākahum fa-la-ʿaraftahum bi-sīmāhum; wa la-taʿrifannahum fī laḥni'l-qawl; wa'llāhu yaʿlamu aʿmālakum*

"Had We willed, We would have shown them to you and you would have known them by their **marks**. But you will certainly know them by **the bent of their speech**. And God knows what you do."

---

**This verse carries the densest sentence the sūra says about language and will be treated in detail.**

### The structure of the verse: a possibility and a certainty

**The word order of the verse sets two sentences against one another, and the opposition lies in the *moods* of the sentences.**

| | **The first sentence** | **The second sentence** |
|---|---|---|
| Construction | **لَوْ نَشَآءُ لَأَرَيْنَٰكَهُمْ** | **وَلَتَعْرِفَنَّهُمْ** |
| Particle | **لَوْ** — counterfactual | **لَ** (the *lām* of the oath) + **نَّ** (the *nūn* of emphasis) |
| Its meaning | **A condition that did not come about:** it would have been, but it was not | **The strongest emphasis:** it will certainly be |
| The way of knowing | **بِسِيمَٰهُمْ** — by their marks | **فِى لَحْنِ ٱلْقَوْلِ** — by the bent of their speech |
| Its source | Knowledge **given from outside** | The product of **one's own ear** |

**And what the table says is the verse itself:**

**The miraculous way of knowing — God's showing them directly — is named as a *possibility* and passed over.** The particle *law* announces a condition that did not come about: "had We willed… but We did not will."

**And the way that is given is the natural one: to look at how a thing is said.** And this way is made certain with **the *lām* of the oath and the *nūn* of emphasis** — the two strongest devices of intensification in Arabic.

**That is, the verse does this: it withdraws the miraculous knowledge and makes certain a means that is in everyone's hands.**

**I rest this comparison on the wording of the verse** (the difference of mood between the two sentences is beyond dispute); **the conclusion I draw from it — "the natural way in place of the miraculous" — is my reading.**

### سِيمَا — the root س-و-م

**سِيمَا / سِيمَاء** — **a sign, a mark, an outward indication.** It has passed into Turkish as *sima*, but its sense in Arabic is not "face" but **the mark on the face**.

**Root: س-و-م.** And the primary sense of the root, according to what the lexicographers record, is: **to put a mark on something, to brand** (*sawwama* — he marked). From the same root **مُسَوَّمَة** (marked, branded — at Āl ʿImrān 3/14, *khayl musawwama*).

**This root was taken up in `051-zariyat.md` and `055-rahman.md`.**

**And the property of *sīmā* is this: an outward, fixed mark, seen when one looks.** The Qurʾān uses it in several places: al-Baqara 2/273 (*taʿrifuhum bi-sīmāhum*), al-Fatḥ 48/29 (*sīmāhum fī wujūhihim*), al-Raḥmān 55/41 (*yuʿrafu'l-mujrimūna bi-sīmāhum*).

**And in all these occurrences *sīmā* is something known by looking.**

### لَحْن — the root ل-ح-ن

**And now we come to the word at the centre of the verse.**

**Root: ل-ح-ن.** And this root possesses one of the most interesting families of meaning in Arabic; the lexicographers record more than one sense for the word, and these are connected to one another.

**The senses the lexicographers record:**

| # | Sense | Explanation |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | **To turn speech from its proper channel** | *Laḥana fī kalāmihī* — he bent his speech away from being plain |
| **2** | **Hint, allusion, saying something obliquely** | To place a thing beneath the words without saying it directly |
| **3** | **Melody, tune, mode** | **أَلْحَان** (*alḥān*) — melodies; the voice's bending away from a straight line |
| **4** | **A mistake in language, an error of inflection** | A technical sense established later: turning speech aside from its correct form |
| **5** | **Understanding, quickness, grasp** | To understand another's hint; to be able to present one's proof well |

**And the common core of the five senses is a single verb: TO BEND.**

**Every case in which speech is bent in some direction away from its straight channel — that is, from its plainest, most direct state — is named with this root.**

**And the *character* of the bending separates the senses:**

| Kind of bending | What happens | The sense of the word |
|---|---|---|
| **By mistake** | The speech is spoiled | **An error in language** (*laḥn*) |
| **Deliberately, to conceal** | What is meant is put beneath the words | **Allusion** (*laḥnu'l-qawl*) |
| **Deliberately, to beautify** | The voice departs from a straight line | **Melody** (*alḥān*) |
| **On the receiving side** | The bent speech is understood | **Grasp** |

**I build this table by arranging the senses the lexicographers record;** all the senses are lexical data, this classification among them is my own arrangement.

### Its relation with *laḥn* in music

**In Turkish the words *lahn* and *elhan* have survived as musical terms, and the relation is real.**

**The lexicographers record *alḥān* as the voice's rising and falling in a particular order** — that is, its departing from a flat recitation and tracing a curve.

**And the logic of the connection is this: in both allusion and melody, speech departs from its *plain delivery*.** In one the meaning bends, in the other the voice.

**I transmit this connection by way of the root sense the lexicographers record.** That the two senses are found in the same root is lexical data; the conceptual division of labour between them is one I am setting up, and I claim no order of derivation.

### The sense of "understanding", and a hadith

**The fifth sense of the root — grasp, being able to present one's proof well — is recorded among the lexicographers and in the sources of hadith.**

**In a report found in the collections of sound hadith it is transmitted that the Prophet, addressing parties to a dispute, said that one of them might present his proof more effectively (*alḥan*) than the other, and that he himself would judge only according to what he heard.**

**I transmit this report without naming a source, with the note that it is found in the sound collections.** And its value here is linguistic: **أَلْحَن** (*alḥan*) has been used in the sense of "one who can bend and turn his words more effectively, more nimbly, more skilfully".

**That is, in this branch of the word there is a sense of *skill*** — and the skill consists precisely in not saying a thing straight.

### فِى لَحْنِ ٱلْقَوْلِ — and the choice of particle

**And a detail in the word order of the verse stands at the very centre of the matter.**

**The verse uses not the particle بِ but the particle فِى.**

Compare:

- **بِسِيمَٰهُمْ** — "**by** their marks". The *bāʾ* announces means: the instrument of knowing is the mark.
- **فِى لَحْنِ ٱلْقَوْلِ** — "**within** the bent of speech". The *fī* announces adverbiality: the knowing takes place **inside** the speech.

**That is, in the second sentence the knowing comes about not by looking at a sign from outside but by *entering into* the speech.**

**And this completes the difference between the two ways of knowing:**

| | **Sīmā** | **Laḥnu'l-qawl** |
|---|---|---|
