| Particle | **بِ** — means | **فِى** — adverbial |
| Where | **Outside** — on the face | **Inside** — within the speech |
| How | **By looking** | **By listening** |
| Time | **Instantaneous** — at a glance | **Extended** — while the speech goes on |
| Its source | **Given** | **Noticed** |

**I rest this table on the wording of the verse;** the difference of the two particles is textual data, the extension in the table is my reading.

### Not what is said, but the manner of saying

**And now we come to what the verse actually says.**

**The verse does not speak of the *content* of what is said.**

This needs to be seen. The sentence could have been:

- *la-taʿrifannahum bi-mā yaqūlūn* — "you will know them by what they say". **It does not say this.**
- *la-taʿrifannahum bi-qawlihim* — "you will know them by their words". **It does not say this.**

**فِى لَحْنِ ٱلْقَوْلِ** — in the *laḥn* of the speech. That is, not in the speech itself but in its **bent**.

**And the meaning of this is: the content may be correct.**

A person may put together entirely correct sentences. Everything he says may be apt. And even so, **where his speech bends** may give him away: what he emphasises, what he passes over, which word he chooses, where he falls silent, which question he does not ask.

**These are not visible in the content. They are visible in the *laḥn*.**

**And this is a very fine instrument of diagnosis** — because it cannot be defended against. If a sentence is wrong it can be corrected; but the bent of speech cannot be corrected, because the person did not construct it. **The *laḥn* is what comes out unintended.**

**I rest this reading on the root sense of the word** (*to bend*, and *to convey by allusion*); **the conclusion I draw from it — "it gives one away because it was not deliberately constructed" — is my own inference** and I ascribe it to no classical exegete.

**The senses given to this expression in the classical commentaries are transmitted as follows:**

| View | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **1** | The **style and delivery** of their words — how they say them |
| **2** | What their words **imply**, what they do not say openly |
| **3** | The **direction and aim** of their words — where they lead |

**All three views look in the same direction and I make no preference.**

### وَٱللَّهُ يَعْلَمُ أَعْمَٰلَكُمْ — and the limit the verse sets for itself

**And now we come to the most important note on this verse. Per the method (`STYLE.md`), I have to write this out separately and plainly.**

**This verse does not tell people to "read one another's intentions". And the verse itself closes this off in three ways.**

**One. The address is singular and is to the Prophet.**

**وَلَتَعْرِفَنَّهُمْ** — *la-taʿrifannahum*: a **singular** addressee. And in the preceding clause *la-araynā**ka**hum* — singular again. This part of the verse is spoken to the Prophet.

**Two. The verse closes by turning to the second person plural — but by attributing the knowledge to God.**

**وَٱللَّهُ يَعْلَمُ أَعْمَٰلَكُمْ** — "God knows **your** deeds."

**Note: the pronoun has turned to the plural, but the subject of the verb is God.** That is, the sentence does not say "you know their deeds". It says: **"God knows your deeds."**

**That is, having opened the matter of knowing, the verse turns the knowledge to God and the object to the addressee himself.**

**This is *iltifāt*** (a shift in the direction of address) **and at the same time a limit:** the matter of knowing another closes with the sentence "your deeds are known".

**Three. The Qurʾān regulates this field explicitly elsewhere.**

**In the section `049-hucurat.md`, 49/12 was treated in full:**

> *"O you who believe! Avoid much suspicion; for some suspicion is sin. And do not spy on one another (*lā tajassasū*), and let none of you backbite another."* (al-Ḥujurāt 49/12)

**And the logic recorded there holds here too:**

> "**Some** suspicion is sin. But which part it is cannot be known while the suspicion is being formed — had it been known it would not have been suspicion at all. A person cannot tell in advance whether the conjecture he forms is of the sinful kind."

**This is a necessary qualification in reading the verse before us.**

**In conclusion — and I write this plainly:** this verse records that the bent of speech gives something away. This is a statement of fact. **But from the statement no "authority to judge others" follows** — because the verse does not give that authority, it keeps the address singular, and it closes the sentence by turning it back upon the addressee's own deeds.

### What this says to the present

**The fact the verse records is a fact recognised in modern fields that work on language as well, and there is no need to force it.**

In a text or in a speech there is something carried beyond the content that is said: the choice of words, what is brought to the front, what is put in the passive, which subject is never opened. Different names are given to these; the name the verse gives is **laḥnu'l-qawl**.

**And the measure the verse gives reminds us that this fact runs in *two directions*:**

**The first direction — for the listener:** to hear not only what is said but also where the speech bends.

**The second direction — for the speaker, and this is the main one:** **the *laḥn* of our own speech is not our own choice.** A person constructs his sentences; but he cannot construct their bent. That comes out of the person himself.

**And the last sentence of the verse looks exactly here:** *wa'llāhu yaʿlamu **aʿmālakum***.

**I record this second direction as my own reading;** the verse builds no sentence about the speaker's side, but the shift of pronoun in the last clause appears to support this direction.

---

## 47/31 — وَلَنَبْلُوَنَّكُمْ حَتَّىٰ نَعْلَمَ ٱلْمُجَٰهِدِينَ مِنكُمْ وَٱلصَّٰبِرِينَ وَنَبْلُوَا۟ أَخْبَارَكُمْ

*Wa la-nabluwannakum ḥattā naʿlama'l-mujāhidīna minkum wa'ṣ-ṣābirīna wa nabluwa akhbārakum*

"We shall certainly test you — that We may bring out those among you who strive and those who endure, and that We may test your **reports**."

### Emphasis and the pronoun

**The sentence carries the same strong emphasis as the second sentence of the preceding verse: لَ + نَّ** (the *lām* of the oath + the *nūn* of emphasis).

**And the pronoun is again the second person plural: كُمْ.** That is, the address continues from the last clause of verse 30.

**This is the place where the sūra changes direction:** 20 to 29 were about "them"; from the end of 30 "you" begins, and it will continue to verse 38.

### بَلَا — the return of the root

**The root ب-ل-و was treated at verse 4** (*li-yabluwa baʿḍakum bi-baʿḍ*). It was recorded there: the primary sense of the root is **to wear out, to make threadbare**; and the connection between testing and wearing out is that use wears a thing down.

**And the root occurs twice in this verse:** *la-nabluwannakum* and *wa nabluwa akhbārakum.*

**Three occurrences in total in the sūra:**

| Verse | Expression | Object |
|---|---|---|
| **4** | *li-yabluwa baʿḍakum bi-baʿḍ* | **You** — by one another |
| **31** | *la-nabluwannakum* | **You** |
| **31** | *wa nabluwa **akhbārakum*** | **Your reports** |

**And the third is interesting: what is tested is no longer persons but *reports*.**

### حَتَّىٰ نَعْلَمَ — a classical question

**The expression "that We may know" is a place the classical commentaries dwell on:** if God already knows, what does "that We may know" mean?

**The explanations transmitted:**

| # | View |
|---|---|
| **1** | *ʿIlm* here is in the sense of **knowledge of what has occurred, has come about**: God knows a thing before it happens as something that will happen, and after it happens as something that has happened. What changes is not the knowledge but the state of what is known. |
| **2** | The governed term has been elided: *ḥattā **yaʿlama** ahlu's-samāwāti wa'l-arḍ* — in the sense of "that it may be known, that it may come out". |
| **3** | *ʿIlm* here is in the sense of **distinguishing** (*tamyīz*): "that We may separate". |

**These views are transmitted in the classical commentaries; I make no preference.**

**And I record this much:** the particle *ḥattā* announces a **limit** (*ghāya*). That is, the testing has a purpose, and the purpose is written explicitly in the sentence.

### ٱلْمُجَٰهِدِينَ وَٱلصَّٰبِرِينَ — two attributes

**مُجَاهِد** — root **ج-ه-د**, the active participle of Form III.

**The primary sense of the root: جَهْد — strength, capacity, being pressed to one's limit.** *Jahd* is a person's coming up against the last edge of his strength. From the same root **جُهْد** (effort), **إِجْتِهَاد** (the expending of all one's strength), **جَهْد** and **مَجْهُود**.

**And Form III here — as with *qitāl* in verse 20 — announces reciprocity:** *jihād* is an effort with a resistance facing it.

**This root was taken up in the section `061-saff.md` at 61/11 (*wa tujāhidūna fī sabīli'llāhi bi-amwālikum wa anfusikum*).** The frame recorded there holds here too and I do not repeat it.

**صَابِر** — root **ص-ب-ر**. The primary sense of the root: **to hold a thing in a place, to confine, to bind.** *Ṣabara nafsahu* — he held himself back.

**And that the two stand side by side is meaningful:**

| Attribute | Root | Movement |
|---|---|---|
| **مُجَاهِد** | ج-ه-د | **Forward** — to expend strength |
| **صَابِر** | ص-ب-ر | **In place** — to hold, to remain |

**One is movement, the other standing.** And the sūra had already said this in verse 7: *yanṣurkum* (help — movement) **and** *yuthabbit aqdāmakum* (making firm — standing).

**The same pair, in two different places, with two different pairs of words.**

**I record this parallel as my own reading.**

### أَخْبَار — the root خ-ب-ر

**And the last word of the verse is a striking choice.**

**Root: خ-ب-ر.** **This root was taken up in `058-mucadele.md`, `067-mulk.md`, `057-hadid.md`, `059-hasr.md`, `063-munafikun.md`, `049-hucurat.md` and `099-zilzal.md`** (in the contexts of the name *Khabīr* and of *tuḥaddithu akhbārahā*). **I do not repeat it here.**

The note to be carried over from there: the primary sense of the root is **to learn the inner side of a thing by trying it**. *Khabara'l-arḍ* — he ploughed the soil, turned its underside up. And **خَبِير** (*Khabīr*) names the one who knows not the surface but the **inside**.

**And from here comes the word ٱخْتِبَار (*ikhtibār*) — examination, test.**

**Now let us look at the last clause of the verse: وَنَبْلُوَا۟ أَخْبَارَكُمْ.**

**Two words, from two different roots, but both carrying the idea of "testing":**

- **نَبْلُوَ** — root ب-ل-و: to test (in the root: to wear out).
- **أَخْبَار** — root خ-ب-ر: reports (in the root: to learn the inner side by trying).

**That is, the clause makes a word that carries the sense of "testing" in its root the object of the verb "to test".**

**This is a weave of sound and sense and is worth recording.** The root senses of the words are lexical data; **the weave between them is one I am setting up, and it carries no claim of a play on words.**

**And there is disagreement over the meaning of the expression "to test your reports":**

| View | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **1** | **Your reports** = the information that will come out about what you have done; "that We may test your record" |
| **2** | **Your reports** = what you say, your claims; "that We may test your words" |
| **3** | **Your reports** = your condition, your state |

**I make no preference.**

**But the second view agrees strikingly with the context of the sūra** — the preceding verse had speech as its subject (*laḥni'l-qawl*), and the central matter of the section `061-saff.md` was the distance between word and deed.

**I record this as an indication, not as a preference.**

---

## 47/32 — إِنَّ ٱلَّذِينَ كَفَرُوا۟ وَصَدُّوا۟ عَن سَبِيلِ ٱللَّهِ وَشَآقُّوا۟ ٱلرَّسُولَ مِنۢ بَعْدِ مَا تَبَيَّنَ لَهُمُ ٱلْهُدَىٰ لَن يَضُرُّوا۟ ٱللَّهَ شَيْـًٔا وَسَيُحْبِطُ أَعْمَٰلَهُمْ

*Inna'lladhīna kafarū wa ṣaddū ʿan sabīli'llāhi wa shāqqu'r-Rasūla min baʿdi mā tabayyana lahumu'l-hudā lan yaḍurru'llāha shayʾā; wa sa-yuḥbiṭu aʿmālahum*

"Those who deny, and turn others away from the path of God, and stand on the opposite side from the Messenger after the guidance has become clear to them, can do God no harm at all. [God] will void their deeds."

### The return of the first verse — and an addition

**The first two verbs of the verse are letter for letter the same as the first verse of the sūra:**

47/1: **ٱلَّذِينَ كَفَرُوا۟ وَصَدُّوا۟ عَن سَبِيلِ ٱللَّهِ**
47/32: **ٱلَّذِينَ كَفَرُوا۟ وَصَدُّوا۟ عَن سَبِيلِ ٱللَّهِ**

**And a third verb is added: وَشَآقُّوا۟ ٱلرَّسُولَ.**

| | **47/1** | **47/32** |
|---|---|---|
| 1st verb | *kafarū* | *kafarū* |
| 2nd verb | *ṣaddū ʿan sabīli'llāh* | *ṣaddū ʿan sabīli'llāh* |
| 3rd verb | — | **شَآقُّوا۟ ٱلرَّسُولَ** |
| Qualification | — | **مِنۢ بَعْدِ مَا تَبَيَّنَ لَهُمُ ٱلْهُدَىٰ** |
| Ruling | *aḍalla aʿmālahum* (perfect) | *sa-yuḥbiṭu aʿmālahum* (future) |

**That is, towards the end of the sūra the opening sentence comes back with a verb and a qualification added.**

### شَآقُّوا۟ — the root ش-ق-ق

**Root: ش-ق-ق.** Its primary sense: **to split, to divide in two.** **This root has been treated in many places in this commentary** — `084-insikak.md` (the name of the sūra), `080-abese.md` (80/26 — *shaqaqna'l-arḍa shaqqā*), `054-kamer.md` (54/1 — *wa'nshaqqa'l-qamar*), `059-hasr.md`, `050-kaf.md`, `055-rahman.md` and others. **I do not repeat it here.**

**What is to be added here is the work of Form III.**

**شَاقَّ** — Form III: reciprocity. And the picture of the word is this:

**شِقّ** (*shiqq*) — the **half, one side** of a thing. When a thing is split there are two *shiqq*s.

**And *mushāqqa* is the two parties' standing on *separate halves*:** one on one side, the other on the other.

**That is, the word says something more concrete than "enmity": to stand on two separate parts of the same whole.**

**And in the section `059-hasr.md` this word occurred in the same context:** at al-Ḥashr 59/4 there is the expression *shāqqu'llāha wa rasūlahu*, and it was recorded there. **The same word, the same structure, in two Medinan sūras.**

**There is a difference and it is worth noting:** in al-Ḥashr the object is **God and His Messenger**; here it is only **the Messenger** (*shāqqu'r-Rasūla*).

### مِنۢ بَعْدِ مَا تَبَيَّنَ لَهُمُ ٱلْهُدَىٰ — a repetition of verse 25

**This qualification occurred letter for letter in verse 25** and was treated there: **the verse does not issue a ruling upon one who does not know.**

**The use in the two verses is the same, and the repetition shows the importance of the qualification.**

### لَن يَضُرُّوا۟ ٱللَّهَ شَيْـًٔا

**ضَرَّ** — root **ض-ر-ر**: to harm. Its opposite is **نَفْع** (benefit).

**لَن** — the strongest negation of the future. And **شَيْـًٔا** is in the accusative: **"in any thing at all"** — an absolute object, widening the negation.

**That is, the sentence: "they cannot harm Him in any way, not by a particle."**

**And this sentence does the same work as the qualification in verse 4:**

| Verse | Expression | What it says |
|---|---|---|
| **4** | *wa law yashāʾu'llāhu **la'ntaṣara minhum*** | Had God willed He would have handled it Himself |
| **32** | ***lan yaḍurru'llāha shayʾā*** | They can do God no harm |

**Both sentences close off the same misunderstanding:** this is not a work carried on to make good a lack in God.

**And in the section `061-saff.md`, at 61/8 (*yurīdūna li-yuṭfiʾū nūra'llāhi bi-afwāhihim*), the same disproportion was treated.** What was recorded there holds here too.

### وَسَيُحْبِطُ أَعْمَٰلَهُمْ — the third voiding, and the future tense

**The third and last occurrence of the root ح-ب-ط in the sūra** (9, 28, **32**).

**And this time the verb is in the *future*: سَيُحْبِطُ.**

| Verse | Verb | Tense |
|---|---|---|
| **9** | *fa-**aḥbaṭa*** | Perfect |
| **28** | *fa-**aḥbaṭa*** | Perfect |
| **32** | ***sa-yuḥbiṭu*** | **Future** (with the *sīn*) |

**Of the three occurrences two are perfect, the last future.**

**And this stands like a preparation for the next verse** — it is my own reading: verse 33 will say *lā tubṭilū*, that is, it will give the addressee an **act**. The future tense shows that the ruling has not yet closed.

**I present this as a reading;** whether the *sīn* here announces certainty or time can be debated.

---

## 47/33 — يَٰٓأَيُّهَا ٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوٓا۟ أَطِيعُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ وَأَطِيعُوا۟ ٱلرَّسُولَ وَلَا تُبْطِلُوٓا۟ أَعْمَٰلَكُمْ

*Yā ayyuha'lladhīna āmanū aṭīʿu'llāha wa aṭīʿu'r-Rasūla wa lā tubṭilū aʿmālakum*

"O you who believe! Obey God, obey the Messenger, and do not **render your deeds void**."

---

**This verse is the turning point of the sūra. I recorded this at the beginning of this commentary in the section "What is the sūra doing?" and I complete it here.**

### The change of pronoun: أَعْمَٰلَهُمْ → أَعْمَٰلَكُمْ

**For thirty-two verses the sūra spoke of deeds coming to nothing. And each time the pronoun was third person.**

**Here the pronoun turns to the second person: أَعْمَٰلَكُمْ — your deeds.**

**And the subject of the verb changes too:**

| | **Verses 1-32** | **Verse 33** |
|---|---|---|
| Verb | *aḍalla*, *aḥbaṭa*, *sa-yuḥbiṭu* | **لَا تُبْطِلُوا۟** |
| Mood | **Statement** — it happened / it will happen | **Prohibition** — do not do it |
| Subject | **God** | **You** |
| Object | *aʿmālahum* — theirs | *aʿmālakum* — **yours** |

**That is, the sūra puts into the hands of the one it addresses the very thing it has recounted for thirty-two verses.**

**And this is the most striking structural move of the sūra.** The addressee, a spectator for thirty-two verses, becomes in an instant the subject of the verb.

### إِبْطَال — the root ب-ط-ل and the link with al-Baqara

**The root ب-ط-ل was treated in the third verse of the sūra** (*ittabaʿu'l-**bāṭil***). What was recorded there is decisive here:

> "The primary sense the lexicographers record: **a thing's coming to nothing, being void, being unable to stand.** […] *Bāṭil* is not merely what is false; it is what **cannot bear weight**."

**And now the two occurrences of the root in the sūra come face to face:**

| Verse | Expression | Who |
|---|---|---|
| **3** | *ittabaʿu'l-**bāṭil*** | Those who deny — **those who follow falsehood** |
| **33** | *lā **tubṭilū** aʿmālakum* | The believers — **those who can render their own deed void** |

**The same root: in one, the name of the thing followed; in the other, the name of an act that can be performed.**

**And this says something worth noting** — it is my own reading: at the beginning of the sūra falsehood was something **outside**; an object set off after. At the end of the sūra falsehood becomes something **a person can do to his own deed**.

### The link with `002-bakara.md`: 2/264

**This verb has been treated earlier in this commentary and the analysis in `002-bakara.md` holds directly here.**

It was recorded there on 2/264:

> **لَا تُبْطِلُوا۟ صَدَقَٰتِكُم بِٱلْمَنِّ وَٱلْأَذَىٰ** — "do not **render void** your acts of charity by reproach and injury."
>
> **إبطال** — to render a thing invalid, to leave it without effect. **That is, the work done is not erased; it is counted invalid.**

**The relation between the two verses deserves examination:**

| | **al-Baqara 2/264** | **Muḥammad 47/33** |
|---|---|---|
| Verb | **لَا تُبْطِلُوا۟** | **لَا تُبْطِلُوا۟** |
| Object | **صَدَقَٰتِكُم** — your acts of charity | **أَعْمَٰلَكُمْ** — your deeds |
| How it is voided | **بِٱلْمَنِّ وَٱلْأَذَىٰ** — by reproach and injury | **Not stated** |
| Scope | **Narrow** — one kind of deed | **Wide** — all deeds |

**And two differences matter.**

**One. al-Baqara says *how* the voiding happens; Muḥammad does not.**

al-Baqara 2/264 writes explicitly which behaviour voids the deed: reproach and injury. Muḥammad 47/33 sets no such qualification; it sets only the prohibition.

**This widens the scope of the verse.** al-Baqara forbids a particular form of voiding; Muḥammad forbids the act of voiding itself.

**Two. al-Baqara says *acts of charity*, Muḥammad *deeds*.**

**And an observation — it is my own reading:** that the verse does not say *what* does the voiding leaves the addressee to look at his own condition. The sūra gives no qualification; had it been given, every behaviour outside the qualification would have been counted safe.

**And there is a disagreement too, which needs to be recorded:**

**What is meant by "the voiding of deeds" has been debated in the classical commentaries.** Among the views transmitted are these: ostentation (*riyāʾ*), self-admiration (*ʿujb*), reproach and injury (by analogy with al-Baqara), major sins, and — in the context of this sūra — **holding back from the fighting and not complying with the command.**

**These views are transmitted in the classical commentaries. I make no preference and I issue no legal ruling.**

### أَطِيعُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ وَأَطِيعُوا۟ ٱلرَّسُولَ — the repetition of the verb

**The verb is repeated twice: *aṭīʿū… wa aṭīʿū.***

**And this is a point that has been dwelt on.** The sentence could have been built thus: *aṭīʿu'llāha wa'r-Rasūl* — with a single verb.

**The following are transmitted in the classical commentaries as explanations of the repetition:**

| View | Explanation |
|---|---|
| **1** | **Emphasis** (*taʾkīd*); both obediences are stressed separately. |
| **2** | To show that obedience to the Messenger is an **independent** obligation; what he brings is binding even if it is not found in the Qurʾān. |
| **3** | To announce that the two obediences rest on the **same source** but operate on two separate planes. |

**These views are transmitted in the classical commentaries and there is a broad debate in legal theory among them. I make no preference and I do not enter that debate.**

**I record one piece of textual data:** the Qurʾān uses this construction in two different ways. At al-Nisāʾ 4/59, *aṭīʿu'llāha wa aṭīʿu'r-Rasūla wa ūli'l-amri minkum* — the verb is not repeated for the third. Here both verbs are repeated and there is no third party.

**I record that this difference stands in the text; I draw no ruling from it.**

### The second and last call of the sūra

**I complete here the table I recorded at the seventh verse:**

| Verse | Call | What follows |
|---|---|---|
| **7** | *yā ayyuha'lladhīna āmanū* | **إِن تَنصُرُوا۟** — a condition; an **offer** |
| **33** | *yā ayyuha'lladhīna āmanū* | **أَطِيعُوا۟ … وَلَا تُبْطِلُوا۟** — command + **prohibition** |

**The first call opened a possibility; the second sets a limit.**

**And the twenty-six verses between them recounted why the limit is needed:** those who listen and take nothing, those who look as one in a swoon at death, those who give a word of partial obedience, those who turn back upon their backs, those who carry hidden rancour in their hearts.

**Having shown all these scenes, the sūra turns to the one it addresses.**

---

## 47/34 — إِنَّ ٱلَّذِينَ كَفَرُوا۟ وَصَدُّوا۟ عَن سَبِيلِ ٱللَّهِ ثُمَّ مَاتُوا۟ وَهُمْ كُفَّارٌ فَلَن يَغْفِرَ ٱللَّهُ لَهُمْ

*Inna'lladhīna kafarū wa ṣaddū ʿan sabīli'llāhi thumma mātū wa hum kuffārun fa-lan yaghfira'llāhu lahum*

"Those who deny and turn others away from the path of God and then die as deniers: God will never forgive them."

### The third occurrence of the opening sentence

**ٱلَّذِينَ كَفَرُوا۟ وَصَدُّوا۟ عَن سَبِيلِ ٱللَّهِ** — for the third time in the sūra (1, 32, **34**).

**And each time something is added:**

| Verse | What is added |
|---|---|
| **1** | — (its bare form) |
| **32** | *wa shāqqu'r-Rasūla* + *min baʿdi mā tabayyana lahumu'l-hudā* |
| **34** | ***thumma mātū wa hum kuffār*** |

**The sūra builds the same sentence three times and narrows it each time.**

### ثُمَّ مَاتُوا۟ وَهُمْ كُفَّارٌ — the condition at the centre of the verse

**And this is the most important note on the verse, and per the method (`STYLE.md`) it needs to be written out plainly.**

**ثُمَّ** — a conjunction announcing sequence and **interval** (*tarākhī*): "then", and it shows that a stretch of time has passed in between. Unlike the *fāʾ*, it announces not immediate succession but **after time has passed**.

**وَهُمْ كُفَّارٌ** — a **circumstantial clause**: "while they were deniers".

**That is, the ruling is tied to the coming about of *all three* of these:**

1. Denial,
2. Obstruction,
3. **And dying in that state.**

**Without the third the ruling is not in force.**

**And this is a limit set by the verse's own wording. Its consequences are these:**

**One. The ruling cannot be issued about any living person.** Because the third of the conditions — dying in that state — has not yet come about for one who is alive.

**Two. Nor can it be issued by a human being about one who has died.** Because a person's state at his last moment cannot be known.

**Three. And the particle *thumma* shows that the intervening time has been left open.** That is, the verse does not close off the possibility of a change in that interval; on the contrary, by tying the ruling to the **end** of the interval it leaves the interval open.

**These three consequences follow from the wording of the verse.** None of them is an interpretation; they come from the grammatical function of *thumma* and of the circumstantial clause.

**And the relevant article of the method (`STYLE.md`) works here exactly:**

> "No blanket ruling is issued about an ethnic or religious group; what the verse describes are **attributes**, and whoever carries that attribute is included in it."

**This verse also says when the attribute becomes settled: at the moment of death.**

### فَلَن يَغْفِرَ ٱللَّهُ لَهُمْ

**The root غ-ف-ر was treated at verse 15** (*maghfiratun min rabbihim*) and a reference was made to `061-saff.md`: the concrete source of the root is the *mighfar*, and forgiveness is a **covering**.

**لَن** — again the strongest negation of the future.

**And an opposition is worth noting:**

| Verse | Expression |
|---|---|
| **2** | *kaffara ʿanhum sayyiʾātihim* — He **covered** their evil deeds |
| **15** | *wa **maghfiratun** min rabbihim* — a **forgiveness** from their Lord |
| **34** | *fa-**lan yaghfira**'llāhu lahum* — He will **never forgive** |
| **19** | *wa'**staghfir** li-dhanbika* — **ask** forgiveness |

**Four occurrences: two roots of covering (ك-ف-ر and غ-ف-ر), four different directions.**

**And the command in verse 19 is striking:** before describing a situation in which forgiveness is definitively closed, the sūra had commanded the **asking** of forgiveness.

---

## 47/35 — فَلَا تَهِنُوا۟ وَتَدْعُوٓا۟ إِلَى ٱلسَّلْمِ وَأَنتُمُ ٱلْأَعْلَوْنَ وَٱللَّهُ مَعَكُمْ وَلَن يَتِرَكُمْ أَعْمَٰلَكُمْ

*Fa-lā tahinū wa tadʿū ila's-salmi wa antumu'l-aʿlawna wa'llāhu maʿakum wa lan yatirakum aʿmālakum*

"**Do not grow slack** and — while you are the higher — do not call for peace. God is with you and He will not diminish anything of your deeds."

---

**This verse will be treated carefully. Per the method (`STYLE.md`) I have to draw a limit here too, and I shall draw it from the verse's own wording and from the Qurʾān itself.**

### وَهَنَ — the root و-ه-ن

**Root: و-ه-ن.** And the concrete sense of the root carries the whole weight of the verse.

**The primary sense the lexicographers record: weakness, slackness — particularly a *structural* weakness.**

And the most concrete place where the Qurʾān uses this root gives the picture of the word:

> *"My Lord! **My bones have grown weak** (*wahana'l-ʿaẓmu minnī*) and my head is aflame with grey."* (Maryam 19/4 — the prayer of Zechariah (Zakariyyā))
>
> *"His mother carried him **in weakness upon weakness** (*wahnan ʿalā wahn*)."* (Luqmān 31/14)

**In both verses the word recounts the lessening of the power to carry.** *Wahn* is not breaking; it is **becoming unable to bear**.

**And in the first verse the object of the word is *bone*** — the load-bearing structure of the body. This gives the picture of the word exactly: from outside nothing has changed; the load-bearing part inside has slackened.

**This is of the same family as two pictures the sūra has used earlier:**

| Verse | Picture |
|---|---|
| **7** | *yuthabbit **aqdāmakum*** — making the feet firm |
| **8** | ***taʿsan** lahum* — falling on the face |
| **35** | *lā **tahinū*** — the slackening of the power to carry |

**All three have to do with the load-bearing structure of the body.**

### The exegesis of the Qurʾān by the Qurʾān: Āl ʿImrān 3/139

**And there is a nearly exact counterpart of this verse in the Qurʾān:**

> **وَلَا تَهِنُوا۟ وَلَا تَحْزَنُوا۟ وَأَنتُمُ ٱلْأَعْلَوْنَ إِن كُنتُم مُّؤْمِنِينَ**
>
> *"Do not grow slack and do not grieve; **if you are believers** you are the higher."* (Āl ʿImrān 3/139)

**When the two verses are compared:**

| | **Āl ʿImrān 3/139** | **Muḥammad 47/35** |
|---|---|---|
| First prohibition | **وَلَا تَهِنُوا۟** | **فَلَا تَهِنُوا۟** |
| Second element | *wa lā taḥzanū* — do not grieve | *wa tadʿū ila's-salm* — do not call for peace |
| Shared clause | **وَأَنتُمُ ٱلْأَعْلَوْنَ** | **وَأَنتُمُ ٱلْأَعْلَوْنَ** |
| Qualification | **إِن كُنتُم مُّؤْمِنِينَ** | *wa'llāhu maʿakum* |

**The two verses share the same verb and the same circumstantial clause.** And Āl ʿImrān ties the being higher to an explicit condition: *in kuntum muʾminīn.*

**This is an indication worth noting in reading the verse before us.**

### وَتَدْعُوٓا۟ — a grammatical disagreement and the meaning of the verse

**And now we come to the most important question in the verse. It is a grammatical question and it determines the meaning directly.**

**فَلَا تَهِنُوا۟ وَتَدْعُوٓا۟** — the position of the second verb in the sentence is resolved in two ways:

| # | Resolution | Parsing | Meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| **1** | **Coordination** — coordinated to *lā tahinū*, **jussive** | The prohibitive *lā* governs both | "Do not grow slack **and** do not call for peace" — two separate prohibitions |
| **2** | **The *wāw* of accompaniment** — **subjunctive** with an implied *an* | The two verbs are forbidden together | "Do not, growing slack, call for peace" — forbidden **together** |

**The second resolution joins the two verbs inside a single prohibition:** what is forbidden is growing slack *and*, along with it, calling for peace — that is, **a call that arises from slackness.**

**Both parsings have been debated in the classical commentaries.**

**And I record this:** the second reading also agrees with the spelling of the verb — in the muṣḥaf the word is written **تَدْعُوٓا۟**, with *wāw* and *alif*, and this suits the subjunctive reading. Had it been jussive the *nūn* would have dropped, giving *tadʿū*, which is also a possible spelling.

**I make no preference.** But I am obliged to show the difference of meaning the two readings give, because the difference is large.

### What the verse says about peace — and what it does not say

**I have to be careful here, and my ground will be the wording of the text.**

**The first ground: the circumstantial clause.**

**وَأَنتُمُ ٱلْأَعْلَوْنَ** — "**while you are the higher**".

**This is a *circumstantial clause*** — that is, it describes the state in which the prohibition holds. A circumstantial clause is a **qualification** of the main clause: the act is forbidden while that state obtains.

**That is, the verse does not say "do not call for peace". It says "while you are the higher, do not call for peace".**

**And this is a conclusion that follows from the grammatical function of the circumstantial clause, not an interpretation.**

**ٱلْأَعْلَوْنَ** — root **ع-ل-و**: to rise. The plural of *al-aʿlā*, an elative: **"those who are higher, those who are on top".**

**The second ground: the Qurʾān itself.**

**And this is the most decisive. The Qurʾān explicitly regulates the answer to be given to a call for peace:**

> **وَإِن جَنَحُوا۟ لِلسَّلْمِ فَٱجْنَحْ لَهَا وَتَوَكَّلْ عَلَى ٱللَّهِ**
>
> *"And if they **incline** to peace, then incline to it and put your trust in God."* (al-Anfāl 8/61)

**This verse uses the same word as the verse before us: ٱلسَّلْم.**

**And it gives a *command*: *fa'jnaḥ lahā* — "then incline to it".**

**That is, the Qurʾān in one place *commands* inclining to peace. Muḥammad 47/35, therefore, cannot be read as a general prohibition of peace** — because that reading would set the Qurʾān at odds with itself.

**And this bears directly on the measure set by al-Nisāʾ 4/82 (which I mentioned at verse 24):** *"Had it been from other than God they would have found much contradiction in it."* That is, the Qurʾān itself lays down the principle that its texts do not contradict one another; and this principle requires that in reading one verse another be taken into account.

**The third ground: the verb itself.**

**The first thing the verse forbids is not peace but وَهَن — growing slack.** The sentence begins with *lā tahinū*; *tadʿū* is attached to it.

**That is, the subject of the sentence, grammatically and in its word order alike, is slackness.**

### Conclusion — I write it plainly

**The conclusion that follows from the wording of the verse is this:**

**The verse does not reject peace.** What it is concerned with is **a call for peace arising from slackness while one is in the higher position**.

**And all three grounds are in the text:**

| # | Ground | Where |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | **The circumstantial clause** — the prohibition is qualified by "while you are the higher" | The wording of the verse itself |
| **2** | **al-Anfāl 8/61** — inclining to peace is commanded | The Qurʾān itself |
| **3** | **The subject of the sentence is *wahn***, not peace | The word order of the verse |

**And this shows that the verse cannot be presented as a general "opposition to peace".**

**I record one further reading — it is mine and it is not binding:** what the verse distinguishes is two different sources of the same behaviour. A call for peace may be the choice of one of the parties; or it may be the result of the exhaustion of the power to carry. Seen from outside the two look the same. The verse separates the two at their source — and it does so with the word *wahn*.

**I present this as my own inference;** the verse does not write out such a distinction explicitly, but that the word *wahn* stands at the head of the sentence stands in the text.

### ٱلسَّلْم — the root س-ل-م

**Root: س-ل-م.** **This root has been treated in many places in this commentary** — `059-hasr.md` (the name *al-Salām* and two readings), `056-vakia.md`, `002-bakara.md`, `050-kaf.md`, `061-saff.md`, `049-hucurat.md`, `051-zariyat.md`. **I do not repeat it here.**

The note to be carried over from there: the primary sense of the root is **completeness, freedom from defect, a thing's being unspoiled**; and the senses "well-being", "peace", "to surrender" come from here.

**And a variant reading is transmitted for the form of the word in this verse:**

| Reading | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **ٱلسَّلْم** (*al-salm*) | Peace |
| **ٱلسِّلْم** (*al-silm*) | Peace (the same sense, a different vowel) |

**There is no difference of meaning; I content myself with recording it.**

**And an observation — it is my own reading:** *salm* and *islām* are from the same root. That is, the object of the call forbidden in the verse belongs, in its root, to the same family of words as "to surrender". This shows the double direction the word carries: to surrender and to make peace are both a **letting go**; the difference lies in what is let go.

**I record this as a lexical observation; I do not claim that the verse sets up this relation.**

### وَٱللَّهُ مَعَكُمْ — accompaniment

**"God is with you."**

**This is an expression that occurs in several places in the Qurʾān** and is classically called **a particular accompaniment** (*maʿiyya khāṣṣa*): an accompaniment stated for a group, in a particular situation.

**And note: the clause is a *nominal sentence*.** It announces fixity.

**And its place in the word order is worth noting:** the verse says three things, and the order is this:

1. **Do not grow slack** — a prohibition.
2. **God is with you** — a ground.
3. **Your deeds will not be diminished** — a guarantee.

**That is, the prohibition is not left alone; two clauses are placed behind it.**

### وَلَن يَتِرَكُمْ أَعْمَٰلَكُمْ — the sūra's counterweight

**And now we come to one of the most noteworthy sentences of the sūra.**

**يَتِرَ** — root **و-ت-ر.** And the family of meanings in the root is wide:

| Word | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **وَتْر / وِتْر** | **Odd** (of number); the opposite of even. At al-Fajr 89/3, *ash-shafʿi wa'l-watr* — treated in `089-fecr.md` |
| **وَتَرَ** | **To diminish**, to leave a thing short |
| **وَتَرَ** | To cause someone to have a kinsman killed and to seek his blood |
| **مَوْتُور** | One whose kinsman has been killed and whose blood has not been avenged |
| **وَتَر** | **A bowstring** — a single stretched cord |

**And the common core the lexicographers record: *oddness*, and from there *deficiency*.** If one of a pair is taken away, an odd one remains; and what remains odd is **deficient**.

**That is, the verb *watr* is to diminish a thing *by separating it from its pair*.**

**And its use in this verse takes two objects: يَتِرَكُمْ أَعْمَٰلَكُمْ** — "[He will not diminish] you of your deeds".

**The word occurs in this sense only here in the Qurʾān.**

**And now let us look at the place of this sentence within the sūra.**

**Throughout the sūra the coming to nothing of deeds has been recounted:** *aḍalla* (1, 8), *aḥbaṭa* (9, 28), *sa-yuḥbiṭu* (32), *lā tubṭilū* (33). **Six times.**

**And the thirty-fifth verse sets the counterweight:**

> **وَلَن يَتِرَكُمْ أَعْمَٰلَكُمْ** — "He will not diminish anything of your deeds."

**This is the closing sentence of the sūra's whole treatment of deeds.**

**And the standing of the two sentences side by side is the completion of the structure I recorded at the beginning of this commentary:**

| Sentence | Verse | What it says |
|---|---|---|
| **لَا تُبْطِلُوٓا۟ أَعْمَٰلَكُمْ** | **33** | **You** can render your own deed void |
| **لَن يَتِرَكُمْ أَعْمَٰلَكُمْ** | **35** | **He** does not diminish your deed |

**Two verses apart, two sentences: one says what a person can do, the other what God will not do.**

**And the whole tension of the sūra lies here:** a God who does not diminish a deed, and a person who can annul his own.

**I recorded this finding at the beginning of this commentary and complete it here. It is my own reading;** the places of the two sentences in the text are fixed, the opposition between them is a frame I am setting up.

---

## 47/36 — إِنَّمَا ٱلْحَيَوٰةُ ٱلدُّنْيَا لَعِبٌ وَلَهْوٌ وَإِن تُؤْمِنُوا۟ وَتَتَّقُوا۟ يُؤْتِكُمْ أُجُورَكُمْ وَلَا يَسْـَٔلْكُمْ أَمْوَٰلَكُمْ

*Innama'l-ḥayātu'd-dunyā laʿibun wa lahw; wa in tuʾminū wa tattaqū yuʾtikum ujūrakum wa lā yasʾalkum amwālakum*

"The life of this world is only a **play** and a **diversion**. If you believe and guard yourselves, [God] will give you your rewards and will not **ask you for your wealth**."

### لَعِب and لَهْو — and a cross-reference within the commentary

**This pair was analysed in full in the section `057-hadid.md` (57/20)** — the analysis of the list of five words, the distribution of the patterns, and the distinction between *laʿib* and *lahw*. **And the word *lahw* was also analysed in the section `102-tekasur.md`.** **I do not repeat it here.**

Two notes to be carried over from there:

> **لعب** — root **ل-ع-ب**: to play. At the centre of the word lies **purposelessness**: an occupation with no outcome, done for its own sake.
>
> **لهو** — root **ل-ه-و**, the primary sense being **to turn away from a thing, to be occupied with something else**; at the centre of *lahw* there is not merriment but **displacement** — the attention is taken from one place and put in another.

**And the distinction: *laʿib* is aimless play, *lahw* is what draws a person away from what matters.**

### What is to be added here: the *length* of the list

**The Qurʾān describes the life of this world with these two words in several places, and the length of the list varies:**

| Place | List | Number of words |
|---|---|---|
| **al-Anʿām 6/32** | *laʿibun wa lahw* | **2** |
| **al-ʿAnkabūt 29/64** | *lahwun wa laʿib* | **2** (in reverse order) |
| **Muḥammad 47/36** | *laʿibun wa lahw* | **2** |
| **al-Ḥadīd 57/20** | *laʿibun wa lahwun wa zīnatun wa tafākhurun wa takāthur* | **5** |

**And this sūra uses the shortest form.**

**An observation — it is my own reading:** as was recorded in `057-hadid.md`, in the list of five the last two words (*tafākhur* and *takāthur*) were in Form VI (*tafāʿul*), which announces **reciprocity**, and there the matter became social. Those two words are absent in this sūra. And the rest of the sūra already treats the social matter with other words: rancours (29, 37), the secret agreement (26), ties of kinship (22), miserliness (37-38).

**I present this as a reading;** it is not necessary to draw a meaning from the shortness of the list.

### *Laʿib* and *lahw* — a note of caution

**And a note on method is needed, because this verse is easily misread.**

**The verse does not say "the life of this world is worthless".** What it says is narrower and is determined by the root senses of the two words:

- **لَعِب** — an occupation **with no outcome**.
- **لَهْو** — a preoccupation that **draws one away from the real work**.

**That is, the description is not of the world *itself* but of what the world is when taken by itself.**

**And the proof of this is in the second half of the verse:** after giving this description the verse sets the condition of **faith and guarding oneself** and promises a **reward** in return. That is, the same life becomes something else when the condition is met.

**And this was recorded in `057-hadid.md` as well:** al-Ḥadīd 57/20, after listing the five words, says in the same verse *"and in the hereafter there is a severe punishment **and a forgiveness from God and good pleasure**"*. That is, there too the description is not left standing alone.

### وَإِن تُؤْمِنُوا۟ وَتَتَّقُوا۟ — the construction of the condition

**The second conditional sentence of the sūra.** The first was in verse 7: *in tanṣuru'llāha yanṣurkum.*

| Verse | Condition | Answer |
|---|---|---|
| **7** | *in **tanṣurū**'llāha* | *yanṣurkum wa yuthabbit aqdāmakum* |
| **36** | *in **tuʾminū wa tattaqū*** | *yuʾtikum ujūrakum wa lā yasʾalkum amwālakum* |

**Both are built with إِنْ** (the conditional of possibility) and **the answer of each has two parts.**

**I record this parallel as an observation about word order.**

**تَتَّقُوا۟** — root **و-ق-ي**, Form VIII. At verse 17 *taqwāhum* occurred; the word was treated in `002-bakara.md` and `049-hucurat.md` and **I do not repeat it here.**

### أُجُورَكُمْ — and the pronoun

**أَجْر** — root **أ-ج-ر**: the return for a piece of work, a wage.

**And the pronoun is striking again: أُجُورَكُمْ — "your wages".**

**This is the same structure as *taqwāhum* in verse 17:** the requital, though it is something given, is attributed to the one who receives it. Not "a wage" but **"your wage"** — that is, earned, belonging to you.

**I record this as an observation about word order.**

### وَلَا يَسْـَٔلْكُمْ أَمْوَٰلَكُمْ — the recording of the measure

**And the last clause of the verse is the key to this sūra's treatment of spending.**

**The clause: "He does not ask you for your wealth."**

**And the subtlety here lies in the word أَمْوَٰلَكُمْ being *definite, with a pronoun, and whole*.**

The clause could have been:

- *lā yasʾalkum mālan* — "He does not ask you for wealth" (indefinite). **It does not say this.**
- *lā yasʾalkum min amwālikum* — "He does not ask of your wealth". **Nor does it say this.**

**أَمْوَٰلَكُمْ** — definite, with a pronoun, and without a partitive: **"your wealth"**, that is, **the whole of your wealth.**

**In the classical commentaries this is explained thus:** God does not ask for the **whole** of wealth; what He asks is a particular proportion (*zakāt*). The obligation has been set upon a part of what is owned, not upon the whole.

**This explanation is widespread in the classical commentaries and agrees with the wording of the verse.**

**And the verse's link with the next verse confirms it:** verse 37 sets up a hypothetical case with *"had He asked and pressed you hard"*. That is, "not asking" is understood, in the verse's own continuation, as not asking for the whole.

**An observation — it is my own reading:** this qualification is a **limiting** clause placed inside a clause of encouragement. The verse encourages spending and in the same sentence writes the limit of the demand. This prevents the encouragement and the demand from running into one another.

**And I find this qualification particularly important because the text itself does it:** the limit of a religious demand is drawn by the very text that conveys the demand.

---

## 47/37 — إِن يَسْـَٔلْكُمُوهَا فَيُحْفِكُمْ تَبْخَلُوا۟ وَيُخْرِجْ أَضْغَٰنَكُمْ

*In yasʾalkumūhā fa-yuḥfikum tabkhalū wa yukhrij aḍghānakum*

"If He were to ask you for it and **press you to the last** for it, you would be miserly, and [this] would **bring out your rancours**."

### A hypothetical scene

**The verse takes the last clause of the preceding verse and turns it around, and recounts what would happen.**

**And the word order strings three verbs into a chain:**

1. **يَسْـَٔلْكُمُوهَا** — were He to ask,
2. **فَيُحْفِكُمْ** — and were He to press hard,
3. **تَبْخَلُوا۟** — you would be miserly,
4. **وَيُخْرِجْ أَضْغَٰنَكُمْ** — and it would bring out your rancours.

**All three verbs are jussive** (parts of a conditional sentence). And the chain runs from cause to effect.

### أَحْفَىٰ — the root ح-ف-و

**Root: ح-ف-و / ح-ف-ي.** And the concrete sense of the root carries the whole force of the word.

**The senses the lexicographers record:**

| Word | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **حَافٍ / حَفِيّ** | **Barefoot** — walking without shoes |
| **حَفِىَ** | For the foot to be **worn down** by walking, to grow thin |
| **أَحْفَى ٱلشَّارِبَ** | To cut the moustache **to the root**, to take it off at the base |
| **إِحْفَاء فِى ٱلْمَسْأَلَة** | To ask **insistently and to the very bottom** |
| **إِسْتَحْفَىٰ** | To question and enquire, to investigate insistently |

**And the common core: to take a thing *to the bottom*, to leave nothing remaining.**

**That is, إِحْفَاء is not an ordinary asking; it is *pressing to the very end*.** The picture of the word is the foot's being worn down by walking or hair's being cut off at the root: **leaving nothing behind.**

**And this sets up an exact contrast with the clause of the preceding verse:**

| Verse | Expression | What |
|---|---|---|
| **36** | *wa lā yasʾalkum amwālakum* | **He does not ask** |
| **37** | *in yasʾalkumūhā fa-**yuḥfikum*** | **Were He to ask and press to the bottom** |

**The word occurs in this form only here in the Qurʾān.**

### تَبْخَلُوا۟ — the root ب-خ-ل

**Root: ب-خ-ل.** **This root was treated in the sections `092-leyl.md` (92/8 — *wa ammā man bakhila wa'staghnā*) and `057-hadid.md` (57/24). I do not repeat it here.**

The meaning of the root: **to hold back from giving what one has, to withhold.** And the lexicographers define *bukhl* as "not giving where giving is due" — that is, an **obligation** is implied in the word.

**And what the sentence says is clear: the pressing does not produce the miserliness; it *makes it visible*.**

**This follows from the word order of the verse:** between *tabkhalū* (you would be miserly) and *yukhrij aḍghānakum* (it would bring out your rancours) there is a **wāw**; the second verb comes not as the consequence of the first but as something **accompanying** it.

### وَيُخْرِجْ أَضْغَٰنَكُمْ — the bringing out of the rancours

**And now we come to the place where the movement I recorded at verse 29 is completed.**

| Verse | Expression | Pronoun | Verb |
|---|---|---|---|
| **29** | *an lan **yukhrija**'llāhu **أَضْغَٰنَهُمْ*** | **هُمْ** — theirs | God brings out |
| **37** | *wa **yukhrij** **أَضْغَٰنَكُمْ*** | **كُمْ** — **yours** | *(the agent: the pressing / God)* |

**The same word, the same verb, two different pronouns.**

**And this is the most noteworthy movement of the sūra:** the word used in the twenty-ninth verse while speaking about "those in whose hearts is a disease" is said eight verses later **directly to the believers**.

**That is, the sūra carries the diagnosis from outside inward.**

**And in terms of the method (`STYLE.md`) this matters greatly:** if a text sets a heavy diagnosis upon others and leaves the one it addresses outside it, the addressee uses that diagnosis as a weapon. This sūra does not do that. It uses the same word upon the one it addresses too.

**And note: in verse 37 there is a *condition*.** *In yasʾalkum* — "were He to ask". That is, the presence of the rancours is not asserted; **the possibility of their coming out** is recounted.

**This is a fine but important difference:** the verse does not say to the believers "there is rancour in you". It says "were you pressed, what is there would show".

**I rest this finding on the conditional mood of the verse.**

### What this says to the present

**What the verse says looks to the present without any need to force it, and it has two parts.**

**One. The measure of a demand is as important as the demand itself.**

The thirty-sixth verse drew the limit of the demand; the thirty-seventh says what would happen were the limit removed. **That is, the measure is not an arbitrary lightening; it is something required for the system to work.**

When an obligation ceases to be bearable, it produces in the one trying to bear it **something set against the obligation**. The verse calls this *aḍghān*.

**Two. Pressure does not produce what is inside a person; it makes it visible.**

**And the verse does not present this as a *benefit*.** The context of the sentence is negative: the pressing is not done, because its outcome is bad.

**I record this second point as my own reading;** the verse lays down no general rule of the form "pressure shows what is within".

---

## 47/38 — هَٰٓأَنتُمْ هَٰٓؤُلَآءِ تُدْعَوْنَ لِتُنفِقُوا۟ فِى سَبِيلِ ٱللَّهِ فَمِنكُم مَّن يَبْخَلُ وَمَن يَبْخَلْ فَإِنَّمَا يَبْخَلُ عَن نَّفْسِهِۦ وَٱللَّهُ ٱلْغَنِىُّ وَأَنتُمُ ٱلْفُقَرَآءُ وَإِن تَتَوَلَّوْا۟ يَسْتَبْدِلْ قَوْمًا غَيْرَكُمْ ثُمَّ لَا يَكُونُوٓا۟ أَمْثَٰلَكُم

*Hā antum hāʾulāʾi tudʿawna li-tunfiqū fī sabīli'llāh; fa-minkum man yabkhal; wa man yabkhal fa-innamā yabkhalu ʿan nafsih; wa'llāhu'l-ghaniyyu wa antumu'l-fuqarāʾ; wa in tatawallaw yastabdil qawman ghayrakum thumma lā yakūnū amthālakum*

"**Here you are**, called upon to spend in the path of God. And **some** of you are miserly. Whoever is miserly is miserly only **towards himself**. God is the rich, and you are the poor. And if you turn away, [God] will put another people in your place; and then they will not be the likes of you."

---

**The last verse of the sūra. And a verse that gathers all its lines together.**

### هَٰٓأَنتُمْ هَٰٓؤُلَآءِ — a double pointing

**The verse opens with an extraordinary structure for drawing attention.**

**هَا** — the particle of **calling attention** (*tanbīh*): "look, here!"
**أَنتُمْ** — the second person plural pronoun: "you".
**هَٰٓؤُلَآءِ** — a demonstrative (plural): "these people".

**Three elements one upon another: a particle of attention, a pronoun, a demonstrative.**

**The grammarians resolve this structure in two ways:**

| # | Resolution | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | *Antum* is the subject, *hāʾulāʾi* the predicate; the clause that follows qualifies *hāʾulāʾi* | "You are those who…" |
| **2** | *Hāʾulāʾi* is in the sense of a vocative (*yā hāʾulāʾi*), *antum* the subject | "O you people! You…" |

**The difference of meaning is not great; the function is the same: to point at the addressee in the strongest possible way.**

**And this structure occurs in several places in the Qurʾān** and wherever it occurs there is a **confrontation**. Āl ʿImrān 3/66: *hā antum hāʾulāʾi ḥājajtum fīmā lakum bihī ʿilm* — "here you are, you have argued about a matter of which you have knowledge."

**That is, the construction is like pointing at the addressee with a finger.**

**And the sūra's last verse opening with this construction completes the structure I recorded at the beginning of this commentary:** the sūra began with "they" and ends with "here you are".

### تُدْعَوْنَ لِتُنفِقُوا۟ — the passive

**تُدْعَوْنَ** — passive: "you are called". Who calls is not stated.

**And the imperfect:** the call is going on, not done and finished.

**نَفَقَ / أَنفَقَ** — root **ن-ف-ق**. And the concrete sense of the root is interesting: **to run out, to be used up; and for a thing to be current, in demand.**

- **نَفَقَ ٱلشَّىْءُ** — it ran out, it was used up.
- **نَفَاق** — currency, demand; goods being saleable.
- **نَفَق** — **an underground tunnel** (it occurs at al-Anʿām 6/35). And **نَافِقَاء** — the hidden second mouth of a field-mouse's burrow, by which it comes out in a moment of danger.
- **مُنَافِق** (*munāfiq*) — from the same root; like the animal with a two-mouthed burrow, a person with two ways out. **This was treated in the section `063-munafikun.md`.**

**And the word *infāq* comes from the first sense: to *use up* wealth, to let it out of one's hand.**

**An observation — it is my own reading:** the word names spending as "running out". And when this is read together with the clause in the second half of the verse (*innamā yabkhalu ʿan nafsih*) it sets up an interesting tension: in its root spending is a running out, but the verse says that withholding is the loss.

**I present this as a lexical observation.**

### فَمِنكُم مَّن يَبْخَلُ — the recording of the proportion

**And in the word order of this clause there is a measure that must be recorded in terms of the method (`STYLE.md`).**

**The clause: مِنكُم مَّن يَبْخَلُ — "and some of you are miserly."**

The particle **مِن** here announces the **partitive** (*tabʿīḍ*): a part of a whole.

**That is, the verse does not attribute miserliness to the whole of those it addresses. It attributes it to a part.**

**This is worth recording because the clause was not built like this:**

- *wa antum tabkhalūn* — "you are miserly". **It does not say this.**
- *fa-tabkhalūn* — "so you are miserly". **Nor does it say this.**

**The verse gives a heavy attribute with a qualification.**

**And this is a method repeated throughout the sūra:** in verse 24 *qulūbin* came indefinite ("some hearts"), and here *minkum* announces the partitive ("some of you").

**In both places the scope is narrowed.** I record this as an observation about word order and find it important in terms of the method (`STYLE.md`).

### يَبْخَلُ عَن نَّفْسِهِۦ — the choice of particle

**And now we come to the finest point of the verse.**

**In Arabic the verb *bakhila* is generally used with these particles:**

- **بَخِلَ بِـ** — to withhold a thing (what is withheld with *bāʾ*).
- **بَخِلَ عَلَىٰ** — to be miserly towards someone (the one deprived with *ʿalā*).

**The verse, however, uses عَن: يَبْخَلُ عَن نَّفْسِهِۦ.**

**عَن** — a particle announcing separation, moving away: "from".

**That is, the clause says: "he is miserly from his own self" — he practises his miserliness upon himself, he withholds from himself.**

**And this is strange on a first reading:** the miserly person is **holding on to** the wealth; it is not that he is not giving himself something.

**In the classical commentaries the explanation is given thus:** the harm of miserliness returns not to the one deprived but to the miserly person himself. The wealth not given remains in his hand in this world; but he has deprived **himself** of its return in the hereafter.

**And this agrees with the frame set up in the section `092-leyl.md` at 92/8 (*wa ammā man bakhila wa'staghnā*).**

**An observation — it is my own reading:** the choice of particle turns the direction of miserliness around. Miserliness looks like a withholding directed outward; the verse names it a withholding directed inward. And this carries the same logic as the clause *la-kāna khayran **lahum*** in verse 21: both what is done and what is not done return first upon the doer.

### وَٱللَّهُ ٱلْغَنِىُّ وَأَنتُمُ ٱلْفُقَرَآءُ — restriction

**And the word order of this clause determines its meaning directly.**

**There are two nominal clauses in the sentence, and the *predicate of each is definite* (carries the article):**

- **وَٱللَّهُ ٱلْغَنِىُّ** — "**al**-ghanī"
- **وَأَنتُمُ ٱلْفُقَرَآءُ** — "**al**-fuqarāʾ"

**In grammar a definite predicate announces *qaṣr* (particularisation).**

**That is, the sentence does not say:**

- *wa'llāhu ghaniyyun wa antum fuqarāʾ* — "God is rich, you are poor." (an indefinite predicate)

**It says:**

**"The rich one is God; the poor ones are you."**

**The difference is this:** the first sentence states an attribute; the second makes a **particularisation** — the riches are His alone, the poverty yours alone.

**غَنِىّ** — root **غ-ن-ي**: to have no need, to suffice to oneself. And **ٱلْغَنِىّ** is more definite than "rich": **one who has need of nothing.**

**فَقِير** — root **ف-ق-ر**. And the concrete sense of the root matters: **فَقَار** (*faqār*) — **the spine, the backbone.** And *faqīr*, according to what the lexicographers record, is the one **whose back is broken**.

**That is, the word names poverty as an *inability to carry*.**

**And this is of the same family as the picture of the root و-ه-ن in verse 35:** *wahn* was the weakening of the bone; *faqr* is the breaking of the back.

**Two words, two verses apart, coming from the same concrete picture: the collapse of the load-bearing structure.**

**I record this parallel as my own reading;** the concrete senses of the two roots are lexical data, the connection between them is one I am making.

### وَإِن تَتَوَلَّوْا۟ — the third and last condition of the sūra

**تَوَلَّىٰ** — root **و-ل-ي**, treated at verses 11 and 22. The two directions of the word (to take charge / to turn away) were taken up at verse 22.

**And the three conditional sentences of the sūra are completed:**

| Verse | Condition | Answer |
|---|---|---|
| **7** | *in **tanṣurū**'llāha* | Help + firmness |
| **36** | *in **tuʾminū wa tattaqū*** | Wage + measure |
| **38** | *in **tatawallaw*** | **Replacement** |

**The first two are positive conditions, the third negative.** And the sūra closes with the negative one.

### يَسْتَبْدِلْ قَوْمًا غَيْرَكُمْ — replacement

**ٱسْتَبْدَلَ** — root **ب-د-ل**, Form X: **to put another in the place of a thing, to exchange.**

From the same root **بَدَل** (a substitute), **تَبْدِيل** (exchanging), **إِبْدَال**.

**And Form X here announces not a request but the doing of a thing willingly.**

**قَوْمًا غَيْرَكُمْ** — "a people other than you". Indefinite: unspecified.

**And the Qurʾān repeats this warning in several places:**

| Place | Expression |
|---|---|
| **al-Tawba 9/39** | *"…He will punish you with a painful punishment and **put another people in your place**"* — *wa yastabdil qawman ghayrakum* |
| **al-Māʾida 5/54** | *"…God **will bring a people** whom He loves and who love Him"* |
| **al-Nisāʾ 4/133** | *"If He wills He will take you away, O people, and bring others"* |
| **Ibrāhīm 14/19-20** | *"If He wills He will take you away and bring a new creation; and that is no great matter for God"* |

**al-Tawba 9/39 uses the same expression as the verse before us.**

**And the logic of the warning is this — I record it as textual data:** the verses make replaceable not the work being done but **the one who does it**. The work stands; its bearer can change.

### ثُمَّ لَا يَكُونُوٓا۟ أَمْثَٰلَكُم — the last sentence of the sūra

**And the sūra ends with this sentence.**

**ثُمَّ** — a conjunction announcing an interval (*tarākhī*): "then". Time passes in between.

**لَا يَكُونُوٓا۟ أَمْثَٰلَكُمْ** — "they will not be the likes of you".

**And there is disagreement over the meaning of this expression:**

| View | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **1** | "They will not **turn away** as you do" — that is, they will not carry this attribute |
| **2** | "They will not **be miserly** as you are" — according to the verb in the context |
| **3** | "They will not be like you" = **they will be better** — as in the description at al-Māʾida 5/54 |
| **4** | General: "they will not be your likes", the description left open |

**All these views are transmitted in the classical commentaries. I make no preference.**

**And I record this:** the sentence does not say **what** the coming people will be; it says only what they will **not** be. The description is given by way of negation — just like the qualifying of the four rivers in verse 15.

### The third and last occurrence of the root م-ث-ل — and the frame of the sūra

**أَمْثَٰلَكُمْ is the last word of the sūra. And it is the third occurrence of the root م-ث-ل.**

| Verse | Expression | About whom |
|---|---|---|
| **3** | *yaḍribu'llāhu li'n-nāsi **amthālahum*** | People — a likeness is given |
| **10** | *wa li'l-kāfirīna **amthāluhā*** | The deniers — there are the likes of it |
| **38** | *thumma lā yakūnū **amthālakum*** | **You** — they will not be your likes |

**And the order of the pronouns shows exactly the movement I recorded at the beginning of this commentary:**

**هُمْ (theirs, 3) → هَا (its, 10) → كُمْ (yours, 38).**

**The sūra closes in the second person the word it opened in the third.**

### On the closing of the sūra

**And a last observation — it is my own reading.**

**The sūra is a sūra of war. Its second name is given as *Qitāl*. Its fourth verse regulates the field of battle; its thirty-fifth forbids growing slack.**

**And the sūra does not end with a sentence about war.**

**The subject of the last verse is *infāq*** — the spending of wealth. And the last warning is not a warning of defeat; it is a warning of **replacement**.

**That is, the sūra finds the greatest danger not in the enemy but in the one it addresses.** And in the last sentence what the addressee can lose is not the war; it is **the standing of being the bearer.**

**I record this as a reading;** that the sūra has chosen this closing is visible from the text, but the conclusion I draw from it is my own inference.

---

## What remains when the sūra ends

**I write this section in order to gather up the lines of the sūra that have been treated in scattered places.**

### One. Deed — the spine of the sūra

The table I set up at the beginning of this commentary was followed through the sūra and completed:

| Stage | Verses | What happens |
|---|---|---|
| **Diagnosis** | 1, 8, 9, 28, 32 | *Their* deeds come to nothing — the subject is God |
| **Handover** | **33** | **Your** deeds — the subject is **you** |
| **Guarantee** | **35** | Your deeds **will not be diminished** — the subject is God |

**Three verbs of annulment, three separate pictures:** *iḍlāl* (making lost — the breaking of the connection), *iḥbāṭ* (swelling and bursting — analysed in `049-hucurat.md`), *ibṭāl* (rendering invalid — analysed in `002-bakara.md` at 2/264).

**And the third lies in a person's own hands.**

### Two. The turning of the same word back upon the addressee

**The sūra uses three words it had used about "them" for "you" afterwards:**

| Word | For them | For you |
|---|---|---|
| **مَثْوَى** | 12 | 19 |
| **أَعْمَال** | 1, 8, 9, 28, 32 | 33, 35 |
| **أَضْغَان** | 29 | 37 |

**This is the sūra's method and it is decisive in terms of the method (`STYLE.md`):** the text does not leave its heavy diagnoses outside the one it addresses.

### Three. The language of concrete pictures

**The sūra continually ties abstract concepts to bodily pictures:**

| Verse | Word | Concrete picture |
|---|---|---|
| 2, 5 | *bāl* | The inward state |
| 4 | *ithkhān* | A liquid thickening and coming to a stop |
| 4 | *awzār* | Loads carried |
| 7 | *yuthabbit aqdām* | The feet holding on the ground |
| 8 | *taʿsan* | Going headlong on the face |
| 15 | *qaṭṭaʿa amʿāʾ* | The tearing apart of the bowels |
| 16 | *ānifan* | The nose of time (its nearest end) |
| 19 | *dhanb* | Tail — what comes behind |
| 23 | *aṣamma* | The blocking of the ear |
| 24 | *tadabbur / aqfāl* | Getting behind / locks |
| 30 | *laḥn* | The bending of speech |
| 35 | *wahn* | The weakening of the bone |
| 37 | *iḥfāʾ* | Cutting to the root, the wearing down of the foot |
| 38 | *faqīr* | The breaking of the back |

**And among these pictures a family appears: *the power to carry*.**

*Wahn* (the weakening of the bone, 35), *faqr* (the breaking of the back, 38), *taʿs* (falling on the face, 8), *thabāt al-aqdām* (the feet holding, 7). **Four words, the same physical matter: being able to stay on one's feet.**

**I record this family as my own reading.**

### Four. The grounds

**The sūra uses the *dhālika bi-anna* pattern five times** (3, 9, 11, 26, 28) and writes the ground of every heavy ruling it gives in the following verse.

**This determines how the sūra is to be read:** the rulings cannot be separated from their grounds, and the grounds announce an **attribute**, not an identity.

---

## Where this section does not speak with certainty

**At many points throughout the text a language of certainty was deliberately not used. The reader needs to know these together.**

- **47/4 — the matter of captives.** The verse's relation with al-Anfāl 8/67 and the other verses was **tabulated in four separate views** and **none was preferred.** No preference was made among the reports concerning the order of revelation. **No legal ruling was issued.** The eight qualifications that follow from the wording of the verse were listed separately; all of them stand in the text.

- **47/4 — the word *awzār*.** The two explanations (weapons / the war's own loads) were given in a table and no preference was made. The interpretation drawn from the idiom — "the positioning of war as a weight rather than a gain" — is **my own reading.**

- **47/4 — the variant reading** (*qutilū* / *qātalū*) was transmitted and no preference was made; the interpretation that the two readings complete one another is mine.

- **47/5 — *ʿarrafahā lahum*.** Three readings (making known / sweet scent / the marking of a boundary) were given in a table and no preference was made; the attempt to unite the three under the heading "the lifting of strangeness" is **my own reading.**

- **47/12 — the comparison with animals.** That the comparison attaches to the verb and not to the agent is grammatical data; the "measurement" interpretation drawn from it is **my own inference.** And that the verse issues its ruling upon an attribute and not an identity was recorded separately, per the method (`STYLE.md`).

- **47/15 — the language of the descriptions of the garden.** The literal/figurative debate was referred to `056-vakia.md`, where no preference had been made either. The meaning of the word *mathal* (description/representation) was given in a table and no preference was made.

- **47/15 — the finding that the four rivers are built by the method of "removing subtractions"** rests on textual data; the interpretation drawn from it — "the honest way of recounting the unknown" — is **mine.**

- **47/16 — the tone of the question** (mockery / failure to understand / indifference) was given in three views and no preference was made.

- **47/17 — the agent of *zādahum hudan*** and the meaning of the expression ***taqwāhum***: in both, the views were tabulated and no preference was made.

- **47/18 — the signs of the Hour.** Because the verse does not name the signs, they were not named in this commentary either. The classical literature was not entered and **no event of the present day was marked as a sign.**

- **47/19 — the command of *istighfār* to the Prophet.** Four explanations were tabulated, no preference was made, and **the theological debate on impeccability was not entered.** The chapter heading ascribed to al-Bukhārī was transmitted **with the note "it is transmitted"** and without any claim of certainty as to its exact wording.

- **47/19 — *mutaqallab* and *mathwā*.** Four views were tabulated and no preference was made.

- **47/20 — *fa-awlā lahum*.** The disagreement over the pause was given in two readings and **no preference was made.** al-Qiyāma 75/34-35 was mentioned as an indication, not as a preference.

- **47/20 — the *tanzīl* / *inzāl* distinction** was transmitted as it is recorded in the classical sources; and **it was written separately that this distinction is not applied consistently everywhere in the Qurʾān.** Indeed verses 9 and 26 of this same sūra show it.

- **47/21 — the parsing of *ṭāʿatun wa qawlun maʿrūf*.** Three implied forms were given and no preference was made.

- **47/22 — the variant reading** (*tawallaytum* / *tuwullītum*) was given in two readings and **no preference was made.** It was shown that the two readings set up two separate scenes.

- **47/23 — the agent of the verbs being God.** The theological debate on destiny was **not entered.** Only three textual observations were recorded and the next verse's asking a question was mentioned as an indication; this is not a theological preference.

- **47/24 — the connection among the senses of the root ق-ف-ل.** The words the lexicographers record (lock, *qāfila*, dryness) were given; **nothing certain was said about the relation of derivation among them.** The connection built on the turning of a key in a lock was marked **as a conjecture.**

- **47/24 — the "each heart's own lock" reading.** That the pronoun refers back to *qulūb* is grammatical data; the interpretation drawn from it is **my own inference.**

- **47/24 — who fitted the lock.** The verse does not say; **nor was it said in this commentary.** The difference of agent between this and verse 16 was recorded and **no preference was made.**

- **47/25 — the agent of *amlā*** (Satan / God) and **the variant reading** (*amlā* / *umliya*): in both, no preference was made. Āl ʿImrān 3/178 was mentioned as an indication.

- **47/26 — the variant reading** (*isrārahum* / *asrārahum*) was given and no preference was made. The relation in root between *sirr* and *surūr* was transmitted **as one explanation of the lexicographers**, not as a settled derivation.

- **47/29 — the "crookedness" sense of the root ض-غ-ن** was transmitted as one view of the lexicographers; the word's sense of "hidden rancour", however, is beyond dispute.

- **47/30 — *laḥnu'l-qawl*.** The three senses in the classical commentaries were tabulated and no preference was made. The gathering of the five senses of the root around the core "to bend" is **my own arrangement**; all the senses are lexical data. **The relation with *laḥn* in music** was transmitted as the lexicographers record it and **no order of derivation was claimed.** The hadith about *alḥan* was transmitted **without naming a source, with the note that it is found in the sound collections.**

- **47/30 — the finding "it gives one away because it was not deliberately constructed"** is my own inference and has been ascribed to no classical exegete. **And that the verse gives no "authority to judge others" was shown in three ways from the verse's own wording** (the singularity of the address, the direction of the closing sentence, and al-Ḥujurāt 49/12).

- **47/31 — *ḥattā naʿlama*.** Three explanations were given and no preference was made. Three senses were given for ***akhbārakum*** and no preference was made; the second sense's agreement with the context was mentioned as an indication.

- **47/33 — what is meant by "the voiding of deeds".** The views transmitted in the classical commentaries were counted; **no preference was made and no legal ruling was issued.**

- **47/33 — the repetition of the verb *aṭīʿū*.** Three explanations were given; **the debate in legal theory was not entered and no preference was made.**

- **47/34 — the ruling's being tied to the moment of death.** This follows from the grammatical function of the particle *thumma* and of the circumstantial clause and is not an interpretation. And the three consequences that follow from it (including that no ruling can be issued about a living person) were written out plainly, per the method (`STYLE.md`).

- **47/35 — the parsing of *wa tadʿū*.** The two resolutions (jussive coordination / subjunctive of accompaniment) were tabulated and **no preference was made.**

- **47/35 — what the verse says about peace.** **That the verse is not a general prohibition of peace was shown with three grounds, and all three are in the text:** the qualification of the circumstantial clause, the command of al-Anfāl 8/61, and the subject of the sentence being *wahn*. **This is not a preference but the result given by the wording and by the integrity of the Qurʾān.** The distinction recorded in addition to this — "two separate sources of the same behaviour" — is **my own inference** and is not binding.

- **47/38 — *lā yakūnū amthālakum*.** Four views were tabulated and no preference was made.

- **No legal ruling was issued.** The regulation concerning captives at 47/4 and the treatment of spending at 47/36-38 were transmitted; the views of the schools of law were not classified and no practical ruling for the present was drawn.

- **No reading in terms of current politics was made.** This is particularly important for this sūra and was written separately as a note on method at the head of the text: **no verse of the sūra was attached to any event, side or conflict of the present day.** The groups the sūra describes were named by the **attributes** the verses record; no blanket ruling was issued about any ethnic or religious group.

- **No variant reading was ascribed to a named authority.** The variant readings were transmitted with the note "it is transmitted", without naming an authority.

- **No view was ascribed to a named exegete.** The views were given with the note "it is transmitted in the classical commentaries". **The only hadith transmitted** (the report about *alḥan* in the discussion of 47/30) was conveyed without naming a source, with the note that it is found in the sound collections; and **the single reference to a chapter heading of al-Bukhārī** was given without any claim of certainty as to its exact wording.

- **No date of revelation was given for the sūra.** It was recorded that different findings are present in the sources and no preference was made among them. The report that 47/13 came down during the emigration was transmitted **with the note "it is transmitted"** and without a claim of certainty.

- **Statements about word counts** (how many places a word occurs in the Qurʾān) are data countable from the text; but differences of spelling and the boundaries of derivation can affect the count. For this reason the numbers were not made the sole ground of any inference.
